When we talk about fitness, you will not merely ask, “Do I look the way I want to look?” You will ask, “Is my body giving me the capacity to live the life I am called to build?”

<a id="BOOK-B0491"></a>
When we talk about fun, you will not merely ask, “Am I entertained?” You will ask, “Am I creating joy, adventure, rest, celebration, and shared experiences that reflect what matters most?”

<a id="BOOK-B0492"></a>
When we talk about finances, you will not merely ask, “Do I have enough?” You will ask, “Are my resources serving God-given purpose, or are they being consumed by fear, status, comfort, and drift?”

<a id="BOOK-B0493"></a>
That is the difference purpose makes.

<a id="BOOK-B0494"></a>
It turns categories into stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0495"></a>
It turns self-improvement into alignment.

<a id="BOOK-B0496"></a>
It turns a life audit into a blueprint.

<a id="BOOK-B0497"></a>
In the next chapter, we begin with family — the relationships you have been entrusted to love, lead, encourage, protect, and form. For many of us, this is where the life we say we want and the life we are actually building become most visible.

<a id="BOOK-B0498"></a>
Because the people closest to us often experience the truth of our priorities before anyone else does.

<a id="BOOK-B0499"></a>
They know whether our stated values are becoming lived values.

<a id="BOOK-B0500"></a>
They know whether our faith is integrated or compartmentalized.

<a id="BOOK-B0501"></a>
They know whether we are present or merely nearby.

<a id="BOOK-B0502"></a>
They know whether we are building by drift or by design.

<a id="BOOK-B0503"></a>
So before we move forward, pause here.

<a id="BOOK-B0504"></a>
Name what you believe.

<a id="BOOK-B0505"></a>
Name what you value.

<a id="BOOK-B0506"></a>
Name what you sense you are called to steward.

<a id="BOOK-B0507"></a>
Name why the life you are building matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0508"></a>
You do not need a perfect answer.

<a id="BOOK-B0509"></a>
You need an honest beginning.

<a id="BOOK-B0510"></a>
Because once you begin to know why you are building, you can begin to build with wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B0511"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 4

<a id="BOOK-B0512"></a>
This week, draft the five elements of your Foundation: Mission, Governing Values, Life Verse or Guiding Scripture, Current Calling or Assignment, and Daily Purpose. Keep Bedrock—Faith and Identity—above and beneath the entire exercise.

<a id="BOOK-B0513"></a>
Write a first version of each in plain language. Do not force permanent certainty. Mission should endure; Calling should describe this season; Daily Purpose should guide today.

<a id="BOOK-B0514"></a>
Then identify one place where your calendar, spending, habits, or relationships do not reflect the Foundation you just named. Choose one small action that begins closing the gap this week.

<a id="BOOK-B0515"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 4

<a id="BOOK-B0516"></a>
What truth anchors my identity beneath performance and pressure?

<a id="BOOK-B0517"></a>
What enduring contribution do I want my life to make?

<a id="BOOK-B0518"></a>
Which Governing Values must remain visible when good things compete?

<a id="BOOK-B0519"></a>
What Scripture anchors and redirects me?

<a id="BOOK-B0520"></a>
What is my Current Calling or Assignment in this season?

<a id="BOOK-B0521"></a>
How will I show up faithfully today?


<a id="BOOK-B0522"></a>
### PART III — THE FOUR PILLARS OF STEWARDSHIP

<a id="BOOK-B0523"></a>
#### The Core Areas of Life We Are Called to Steward


<a id="BOOK-B0524"></a>
### Chapter 5 — Family

<a id="BOOK-B0525"></a>
#### Leading the Relationships You’ve Been Entrusted With

<a id="BOOK-B0526"></a>
The first Tuesday night, before we opened a book, before we talked about faith or manhood or purpose, I showed them a picture.

<a id="BOOK-B0527"></a>
There were six of them sitting in our house that night—Colton and five of his closest buddies. They were all seniors in high school. Classmates. Athletes. Friends who had grown up together in that strange and beautiful season where boys become young men almost before their parents are ready to admit it.

<a id="BOOK-B0528"></a>
The picture I showed them was from their freshman year.

<a id="BOOK-B0529"></a>
They were in our backyard, swimming in the Arizona heat, no shirts on, still looking like boys even if they would have argued otherwise at the time. The kind of picture parents love and teenage boys immediately regret. Scrawny arms. Undeveloped muscles. Big smiles. Too much confidence for bodies that had not yet caught up with them.

<a id="BOOK-B0530"></a>
I held up the picture and asked them a simple question.

<a id="BOOK-B0531"></a>
“When you look at this, do you see boys or men?”

<a id="BOOK-B0532"></a>
They laughed immediately.

<a id="BOOK-B0533"></a>
They pointed at each other. They made fun of the skinny arms and awkward frames. They called each other out with the kind of brutal affection teenage boys seem to specialize in. There was no hesitation.

<a id="BOOK-B0534"></a>
“Boys,” they said.

<a id="BOOK-B0535"></a>
Then I asked them another question.

<a id="BOOK-B0536"></a>
“When you got ready this morning and looked in the mirror, did you see boys or men?”

<a id="BOOK-B0537"></a>
This time the laughter quieted a little. Not completely, of course. They were still eighteen-year-old guys sitting in my house. But the question landed differently.

<a id="BOOK-B0538"></a>
They looked at each other. They smiled. They sat up a little taller.

<a id="BOOK-B0539"></a>
This time, with more confidence, they agreed.

<a id="BOOK-B0540"></a>
Young men.

<a id="BOOK-B0541"></a>
And that was the point.

<a id="BOOK-B0542"></a>
Within the next six months, those young men were going to experience more freedom than they had ever had in their lives. Mom would not be looking over their shoulder at every decision. Many of them would be moving out of their parents’ homes and into college. They would decide what to do on Friday and Saturday nights. They would decide who to spend time with. They would decide whether church remained part of their lives or quietly disappeared. They would decide how much time they spent on devices. They would decide which friend groups to run with, which temptations to entertain, which habits to normalize, which voices to trust, and which version of themselves they were going to become.

<a id="BOOK-B0543"></a>
So I told them the truth as plainly as I could.

<a id="BOOK-B0544"></a>
“You need to decide now what kind of man you want to be.”

<a id="BOOK-B0545"></a>
Not someday.

<a id="BOOK-B0546"></a>
Not after college.

<a id="BOOK-B0547"></a>
Not after you make a few mistakes.

<a id="BOOK-B0548"></a>
Not after culture has already discipled you.

<a id="BOOK-B0549"></a>
Now.

<a id="BOOK-B0550"></a>
What do you want your life to look like when you graduate college? Will you have experiences you cherish or experiences you wish you could forget? Will you build relationships that are meaningful, or shallow ones that might even haunt you later? Will you become the man God created you to be, or the man society and culture are programming you to become?

<a id="BOOK-B0551"></a>
That night was the beginning of a fourteen-week journey.

<a id="BOOK-B0552"></a>
Every Tuesday night, through the fall of 2025 and into the beginning of 2026, those young men came to our house. I made dinner for them, because if you want teenage boys to show up consistently, feeding them is not optional. From 6:30 to 7:00, we ate. Then from 7:00 to 8:00, we went outside by the fireplace or down into the basement and talked through a chapter of the Wild at Heart study. Afterward, they would hang out, play Ping-Pong, play pickleball in the backyard, laugh, compete, raid the pantry, and stay around the house like they belonged there.

<a id="BOOK-B0553"></a>
And they did.

<a id="BOOK-B0554"></a>
Over those weeks, we had amazing conversations. We had challenging ones too. The details of those conversations will stay where they belong—between those young men and me. It was a safe place. That mattered. But we talked about real things: manhood, faith, fatherhood, sex, pornography, troubled friends, when to help, when to stay away, what courage looks like, what temptation feels like, what kind of men they wanted to become, and what it means to live with conviction when the world around you is trying to shape you by default.

<a id="BOOK-B0555"></a>
Something happened in those Tuesday nights that is hard to fully describe.

<a id="BOOK-B0556"></a>
We were not just going through a study.

<a id="BOOK-B0557"></a>
We were building brotherhood.

<a id="BOOK-B0558"></a>
Those young men began to understand that they could count on one another. They began to understand that they were not stepping into adulthood alone. They began to understand that there were people who saw them, believed in them, expected something from them, and would be there for them—not just during senior year, but a year from now, ten years from now, even twenty years from now.

<a id="BOOK-B0559"></a>
And somewhere in the middle of those Tuesday nights, I realized something too.

<a id="BOOK-B0560"></a>
As much as I hoped they were getting out of the study, I might have been getting even more.

<a id="BOOK-B0561"></a>
It became my favorite part of the week.

<a id="BOOK-B0562"></a>
Not because it was easy. Not because it was convenient. Not because I had extra time sitting around waiting to be used. Life was full. Work was full. Family was full. But when those boys were sitting around our table, or outside by the fire, or downstairs opening up about things they had never said out loud before, I knew there was nothing more important on earth I could have been doing in that moment.

<a id="BOOK-B0563"></a>
That is stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0564"></a>
Not in theory.

<a id="BOOK-B0565"></a>
Not in a diagram.

<a id="BOOK-B0566"></a>
Not in a financial plan.

<a id="BOOK-B0567"></a>
In a room full of young men becoming who they were created to become.

<a id="BOOK-B0568"></a>
That is why family is the first pillar of the Stewardship Blueprint.

<a id="BOOK-B0569"></a>
In the last chapter, you began naming the foundation beneath your blueprint: your beliefs, values, purpose, mission, calling, and direction. Now we begin testing that foundation against real life. And the first test is not theoretical. It is relational.

<a id="BOOK-B0570"></a>
The people closest to you often experience the truth of your priorities before anyone else does. They know whether your stated values are becoming lived values. They know whether your purpose is shaping your presence. They know whether faith is integrated or merely spoken. They know whether you are building by drift or by design.

<a id="BOOK-B0571"></a>
Up to this point in the book, we have been talking about awakening. We have been talking about the danger of drifting through life, building around the wrong scoreboard, confusing urgency with importance, and treating faith as a compartment instead of the center. But now the blueprint becomes personal in a new way.

<a id="BOOK-B0572"></a>
Because the first pillar is not your career.

<a id="BOOK-B0573"></a>
It is not your net worth.

<a id="BOOK-B0574"></a>
It is not your health plan.

<a id="BOOK-B0575"></a>
It is not your bucket list.

<a id="BOOK-B0576"></a>
The first pillar is people.

<a id="BOOK-B0577"></a>
The people closest to us are often the people we assume will understand our absence. They understand when work runs late. They understand when the meeting takes longer than expected. They understand when the phone keeps buzzing, when the client has a need, when the email requires a response, when the deadline cannot wait, when we are tired, distracted, preoccupied, and promising, “I’ll be there in a minute,” even though the minute becomes an hour.

<a id="BOOK-B0578"></a>
And because they understand, we can begin to mistake their patience for permission.

<a id="BOOK-B0579"></a>
A spouse may wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0580"></a>
A child may wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0581"></a>
A parent may wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0582"></a>
A friend may wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0583"></a>
A young person who needs guidance may wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0584"></a>
And because they wait, we tell ourselves everything is fine.

<a id="BOOK-B0585"></a>
But waiting is not the same as thriving.

<a id="BOOK-B0586"></a>
Most people do not neglect their families because they do not care. They neglect them because life gets full. Urgency gets loud. Ambition gets rewarded. Responsibility feels noble. Provision becomes consuming. And the people closest to us are often the least likely to demand attention in the moment.

<a id="BOOK-B0587"></a>
Work rings.

<a id="BOOK-B0588"></a>
Family waits.

<a id="BOOK-B0589"></a>
Email interrupts.

<a id="BOOK-B0590"></a>
Marriage waits.

<a id="BOOK-B0591"></a>
Deadlines demand.

<a id="BOOK-B0592"></a>
Children wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0593"></a>
Notifications buzz.

<a id="BOOK-B0594"></a>
Friendships wait.

<a id="BOOK-B0595"></a>
The urgent rarely needs help getting on your calendar. The important almost always does.

<a id="BOOK-B0596"></a>
That is why family must be stewarded intentionally.

<a id="BOOK-B0597"></a>
Family is not simply something we have.

<a id="BOOK-B0598"></a>
Family is something entrusted to us.

<a id="BOOK-B0599"></a>
When I use the word family, I want you to hear it broadly. For some of you, family means a spouse and children living under your roof. For others, it means adult children, grandchildren, aging parents, siblings, close friends, neighbors, church community, young people you mentor, or the people you are doing life with in this season. Some relationships will be there for a season. Some will be there for life. Some are connected by blood. Others become family through love, loyalty, presence, and shared purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B0600"></a>
You may be single. You may be divorced. You may be widowed. You may not have children. You may have family relationships that are complicated or painful. You may be in a season where the word family brings both gratitude and grief.

<a id="BOOK-B0601"></a>
You are not excluded from this pillar.

<a id="BOOK-B0602"></a>
The stewardship of family is the stewardship of the people God has placed within your relational influence. It is the stewardship of love, trust, presence, guidance, forgiveness, encouragement, protection, example, and belonging.

<a id="BOOK-B0603"></a>
And it begins with a question that is simple but searching:

<a id="BOOK-B0604"></a>
Are the people closest to me receiving my intentional leadership, or are they surviving on my leftovers?

<a id="BOOK-B0605"></a>
Years earlier, when I held Colton in the hospital for the first time, I realized he would absorb my rhythms long before he understood my words. That moment awakened something in me. It made me ask what kind of father I wanted to become, what kind of home Kristi and I wanted to build, and whether everything I was chasing would actually matter if I neglected the people entrusted to me.

<a id="BOOK-B0606"></a>
But as Colton grew older, that question took on new weight.

<a id="BOOK-B0607"></a>
It was no longer only about what he saw in me around the house when he was little. It was about the kind of young man he was becoming. It was about the voices shaping his identity. It was about whether he and his friends would drift into adulthood by accident or be called into it with purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B0608"></a>
Because whether we realize it or not, the next generation is always being discipled.

<a id="BOOK-B0609"></a>
Culture is discipling them.

<a id="BOOK-B0610"></a>
Screens are discipling them.

<a id="BOOK-B0611"></a>
Social media is discipling them.

<a id="BOOK-B0612"></a>
Entertainment is discipling them.

<a id="BOOK-B0613"></a>
Coaches, teachers, friends, influencers, algorithms, advertisers, and peer groups are all shaping what they believe about success, strength, beauty, sex, money, popularity, courage, faith, identity, and worth.

<a id="BOOK-B0614"></a>
If we are passive, we should not be surprised when culture becomes the loudest voice.

<a id="BOOK-B0615"></a>
This is not only true for teenagers. It is true in every home, every marriage, every friendship, every community. Someone or something is always shaping the culture of our closest relationships. The question is not whether formation is happening. The question is whether formation is happening by intention or by drift.

<a id="BOOK-B0616"></a>
That is why Kristi and I have tried, imperfectly but intentionally, to define what our family is about.

<a id="BOOK-B0617"></a>
Years ago, we wrote a Team G mission statement:

<a id="BOOK-B0618"></a>
“We are a Christ-centered family who loves to have fun. We believe it is important to share our faith, hope, and love. We do this by encouraging others, taking action, and leading by example.”

<a id="BOOK-B0619"></a>
Our family life verse became Matthew 5:16:

<a id="BOOK-B0620"></a>
“In the same way, let your light shine before others, that they may see your good deeds and glorify your Father in heaven.”

<a id="BOOK-B0621"></a>
That verse has given purpose to so much of what we try to live and model. Let your light shine. Not so people think more highly of you. Not so your family looks impressive. Not so your children perform perfectly. Let your light shine so others see the goodness of God and glorify the Father.

<a id="BOOK-B0622"></a>
That is a different kind of family mission.

<a id="BOOK-B0623"></a>
When we bought our new home, before the floors went in, we spent an evening writing that mission statement, our life verse, and Scripture throughout the house. We wrote on the floors. We wrote in bedrooms. We wrote verses in spaces that would eventually be covered, hidden beneath tile, carpet, wood, and furniture. One of the most special parts of that night was watching our kids write Scriptures in each other’s rooms for their siblings.

<a id="BOOK-B0624"></a>
Most people would never see those words.

<a id="BOOK-B0625"></a>
But we would know they were there.

<a id="BOOK-B0626"></a>
And that felt right to me because every home has something written beneath the surface. Every family has values underneath the visible structure. Every household is built on something, whether named or unnamed. Some homes are built on hurry. Some are built on performance. Some are built on fear. Some are built on image. Some are built on avoidance. Some are built on faith, love, service, forgiveness, joy, truth, and grace.

<a id="BOOK-B0627"></a>
The question is not whether your home has a foundation.

<a id="BOOK-B0628"></a>
The question is what has been written there.

<a id="BOOK-B0629"></a>
Deuteronomy 6 gives us a picture of formation that happens in the ordinary rhythms of life. God tells His people to impress His commandments on their children—to talk about them when they sit at home, when they walk along the road, when they lie down, and when they get up. In other words, formation was never meant to happen only in formal religious moments. It was meant to happen in the everyday flow of family life.

<a id="BOOK-B0630"></a>
At the table.

<a id="BOOK-B0631"></a>
In the car.

<a id="BOOK-B0632"></a>
On the walk.

<a id="BOOK-B0633"></a>
At bedtime.

<a id="BOOK-B0634"></a>
During conflict.

<a id="BOOK-B0635"></a>
After mistakes.

<a id="BOOK-B0636"></a>
In celebration.

<a id="BOOK-B0637"></a>
In disappointment.

<a id="BOOK-B0638"></a>
In the ordinary moments that do not feel dramatic at the time but quietly become the story of a home.

<a id="BOOK-B0639"></a>
Family stewardship begins when we stop assuming our values are obvious and start making them visible.

<a id="BOOK-B0640"></a>
That starts in marriage.

<a id="BOOK-B0641"></a>
For those who are married, your marriage is not an accessory to the life you are building. It is one of the primary places where your life is being built. It deserves attention, protection, honesty, affection, forgiveness, pursuit, and time.

<a id="BOOK-B0642"></a>
A strong marriage is not built first in the dramatic moments. It is built in ordinary repeated moments where two people choose attention over assumption.

<a id="BOOK-B0643"></a>
For Kristi and me, that has looked different in different seasons.

<a id="BOOK-B0644"></a>
There have been seasons when we were good at date nights. There have been other seasons when date nights were hard to plan. When the kids were younger and life was full of activities, school, sports, work, and travel, a traditional date night was not always realistic. So early morning neighborhood walks became a way to connect. Monday lunches at home became date night in another form. They were not fancy. They did not require a reservation. But they gave us space to look at each other, talk without interruption, and remember that we were not just managing a household. We were building a life together.

<a id="BOOK-B0645"></a>
That has been one of the biggest lessons for us.

<a id="BOOK-B0646"></a>
Intentionality matters more than the form.

<a id="BOOK-B0647"></a>
A busy season does not have to become a disconnected season, but connection rarely survives without being protected.

<a id="BOOK-B0648"></a>
There are still seasons when one of us will feel like we are not as connected as we need to be. Sometimes Kristi will call it out. Sometimes I will. It may be as simple as saying, “We need some time together,” or “We need to get something on the calendar.” That kind of honesty is a gift. It is easy to wait until distance becomes resentment. It is wiser to protect connection before distance becomes normal.

<a id="BOOK-B0649"></a>
Marriage also shapes parenting.

<a id="BOOK-B0650"></a>
The conversations Kristi and I have during those walks, lunches, and uninterrupted times are often where we talk about the kids, discipline, boundaries, and what each child needs in a particular season. When our kids were little, it seemed like each of them would push boundaries for two or three months at a time, testing whether the boundaries we had set were actually solid. As they got older, those boundary-testing seasons sometimes lasted longer and became more complicated.

<a id="BOOK-B0651"></a>
Parenting has not been without heartache or challenge.

<a id="BOOK-B0652"></a>
No honest parent would say otherwise.

<a id="BOOK-B0653"></a>
But Kristi and I being consistent, staying on the same page, and returning to the same values made parenting healthier. Not perfect. Not easy. But healthier.

<a id="BOOK-B0654"></a>
That is important because family stewardship is not about creating a pain-free home. There is no such thing. Children struggle. Marriages face pressure. Families walk through conflict, disappointment, fear, discipline, loss, transition, and seasons that do not go according to plan. Some families face major health issues, grief, addiction, estrangement, disability, financial strain, or pain far beyond what our family has experienced. I do not want to minimize that. Some of you have walked through deep waters, and your family stewardship has required courage I may never fully understand.

<a id="BOOK-B0655"></a>
But the values still hold true.

<a id="BOOK-B0656"></a>
Presence still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0657"></a>
Faith still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0658"></a>
Forgiveness still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0659"></a>
Communication still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0660"></a>
Consistency still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0661"></a>
Prayer still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0662"></a>
Love still matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0663"></a>
And in many ways, the harder seasons reveal whether our family values are decorative or foundational.

<a id="BOOK-B0664"></a>
Over time, my own journey of faith and the development of the Stewardship Blueprint have made marriage, parenting, and friendship more meaningful and less stressful. Not because life became easier, but because the lens changed. I love Kristi and the kids more every day. I am learning to embrace the good and the bad, the highs and the lows, the joy and the challenges. I see difficult moments less as interruptions and more as opportunities to ask, “What can we learn through this?” while trusting God with the ultimate outcome.

<a id="BOOK-B0665"></a>
That shift has made me less distracted as a dad and more engaged. More intentional with my time. More aware of my presence. More committed to focus.

<a id="BOOK-B0666"></a>
And that matters because children do not only hear what we say.

<a id="BOOK-B0667"></a>
They absorb what our lives make normal.

<a id="BOOK-B0668"></a>
They notice whether faith is alive or compartmentalized. They notice whether marriage is affectionate or merely functional. They notice whether money creates fear, pride, generosity, or wisdom. They notice whether work is a calling or an idol. They notice how conflict is handled. They notice whether apologies happen. They notice whether boundaries are real. They notice whether joy is scheduled or always postponed. They notice whether they are seen.

<a id="BOOK-B0669"></a>
A distracted parent asks, “Do my children know I love them?”

<a id="BOOK-B0670"></a>
A steward asks, “Are my children experiencing my love in the rhythms of our home?”

<a id="BOOK-B0671"></a>
A busy spouse asks, “Does my spouse understand how much pressure I am under?”

<a id="BOOK-B0672"></a>
A steward asks, “Am I protecting connection before distance becomes normal?”

<a id="BOOK-B0673"></a>
A passive mentor asks, “Will someone guide the next generation?”

<a id="BOOK-B0674"></a>
A steward asks, “Who has God placed within my influence?”

<a id="BOOK-B0675"></a>
That last question matters deeply.

<a id="BOOK-B0676"></a>
Because legacy is not limited to biology.

<a id="BOOK-B0677"></a>
You may not have children in your home. You may be single. You may be an empty nester. You may be retired. You may be a young professional who thinks you do not have enough life experience to mentor anyone yet. But there is almost always someone who could benefit from your presence, your encouragement, your wisdom, your honesty, or your example.

<a id="BOOK-B0678"></a>
A nephew.

<a id="BOOK-B0679"></a>
A niece.

<a id="BOOK-B0680"></a>
A grandchild.

<a id="BOOK-B0681"></a>
A neighbor.

<a id="BOOK-B0682"></a>
A young employee.

<a id="BOOK-B0683"></a>
A student.

<a id="BOOK-B0684"></a>
A friend’s son or daughter.

<a id="BOOK-B0685"></a>
Someone at church.

<a id="BOOK-B0686"></a>
Someone who does not need you to be perfect, but does need you to be available.

<a id="BOOK-B0687"></a>
Robbie was that kind of mentor for me. I think of him often. He and Susan were a few steps ahead of Kristi and me in life, and their example helped us imagine a different kind of home and family life. Their faith was not performative. Their home had warmth. Their family had intentionality. Their example encouraged me then, and it still encourages me now to invest in others—whether through work, friendship, or opportunities like the study with Colton and his buddies.

<a id="BOOK-B0688"></a>
That is how relational stewardship multiplies.

<a id="BOOK-B0689"></a>
Someone pours into you.

<a id="BOOK-B0690"></a>
You pour into someone else.

<a id="BOOK-B0691"></a>
And the blessing moves forward.

<a id="BOOK-B0692"></a>
When we finished the Wild at Heart study, I wanted to give those six young men something tangible. Not because a gift could capture everything that had happened, but because symbols matter. We do not have many rituals in our culture that welcome young men into manhood. We have graduations. We have birthdays. We have college commitments and athletic banquets. But we do not always pause to say, “I see you. I am proud of you. You are becoming a man. You are capable of great things. You were made for more.”

<a id="BOOK-B0693"></a>
So I asked a local knife maker to make six custom knives. He was the same knife maker who had made Colton his first hunting knife, which made it even more meaningful to me. All six knives were cut from the same steel. That mattered. One steel. One brotherhood. They had the same handles. Each knife had the young man’s initials etched into the blade, along with the words:

<a id="BOOK-B0694"></a>
Made for More.

<a id="BOOK-B0695"></a>
If I had more room, I probably would have written Created for More, because that is what I truly believe. God created each of them with purpose. But Made for More captured the message.

<a id="BOOK-B0696"></a>
After the study ended, we invited the parents over for dinner. Kristi had opened our home for fourteen weeks, fed teenage boys constantly, and created a space where they knew they were welcome. Some of those boys still come over constantly. Some will come over for hours even when Colton is not there. They know they are welcome in our house.

<a id="BOOK-B0697"></a>
And they definitely know they are welcome in our pantry.

<a id="BOOK-B0698"></a>
That night, with the parents there, I gave the boys their knives.

<a id="BOOK-B0699"></a>
I talked to them about brotherhood. I talked about Proverbs 27:17, “As iron sharpens iron, so one person sharpens another.” I told them they were truly made and created for more. I told them I loved them. I told them I would always be there for them—a year from now or twenty years from now. I wanted them to know that I saw them as young men, that I was proud of them, and that I believed they were capable of great things.

<a id="BOOK-B0700"></a>
They loved the knives.

<a id="BOOK-B0701"></a>
But more than that, I think they understood the meaning.

<a id="BOOK-B0702"></a>
Several of the parents reached out afterward to thank Kristi and me for opening our home, feeding their boys, and pouring life into them. That meant a lot. But the greater reward has been watching those young men continue to lean in. Some have come to me with life questions. Some have asked for reference letters for college. Some have interviewed me for classwork. Some simply keep showing up.

<a id="BOOK-B0703"></a>
That is when you realize mentorship is not always a formal title.

<a id="BOOK-B0704"></a>
Sometimes mentorship looks like a Tuesday night dinner.

<a id="BOOK-B0705"></a>
Sometimes it looks like a fireplace conversation.

<a id="BOOK-B0706"></a>
Sometimes it looks like Ping-Pong after a hard discussion.

<a id="BOOK-B0707"></a>
Sometimes it looks like telling a young man the truth before culture gets the loudest voice.

<a id="BOOK-B0708"></a>
Sometimes it looks like making space in your home and your week and your heart for someone who needs to know he is not alone.

<a id="BOOK-B0709"></a>
And anyone can do this.

<a id="BOOK-B0710"></a>
That may be one of the most important things I want you to hear in this chapter. Do not merely say, “We should have conversations with our kids.” Have them. Schedule them. Buy a book. Invite the friends. Make the dinner. Ask better questions. Create a safe place. Do not wait until you feel fully prepared. Do not assume someone else is doing it. Do not underestimate the power of opening your home and giving young people a consistent place to belong.

<a id="BOOK-B0711"></a>
It might be life-changing for them.

<a id="BOOK-B0712"></a>
It might also be life-changing for you.

<a id="BOOK-B0713"></a>
Watching Colton grow in his faith and maturity right in front of me, while sharing honestly with his buddies, is something I will never forget. It was one of the most rewarding experiences I have had as a father. Pouring into them filled me up. It restored and re-energized me.

<a id="BOOK-B0714"></a>
That may sound surprising, because we often assume giving ourselves away drains us. And sometimes it does. There is a kind of overcommitment that exhausts and depletes. But there is another kind of investment that restores because it aligns with purpose. When you are doing what God has called you to do, with people He has entrusted to you, you can end the night tired and full at the same time.

<a id="BOOK-B0715"></a>
That is what those Tuesday nights were for me.

<a id="BOOK-B0716"></a>
Tired and full.

<a id="BOOK-B0717"></a>
Relational stewardship does not always require more time than we have. Often, it requires more intention with the time already available.

<a id="BOOK-B0718"></a>
The question is not, “Can I do everything?”

<a id="BOOK-B0719"></a>
You cannot.

<a id="BOOK-B0720"></a>
The question is, “What has God entrusted to me in this season, and who needs my intentional presence?”

<a id="BOOK-B0721"></a>
The form of relational stewardship changes with the season, but the assignment remains: pay attention to the people actually in front of you. Young children need presence and formation; teenagers need trust and guidance; adult children need respect and continuing relationship; spouses, friends, parents, and mentees need intentional care rather than assumption.

<a id="BOOK-B0722"></a>
No matter your life stage, relational stewardship moves in four directions.

<a id="BOOK-B0723"></a>
First, presence.

<a id="BOOK-B0724"></a>
Presence means more than proximity. You can sit at the same table and still be absent. You can attend the game and still be mentally at work. You can live in the same house and still miss what is happening in someone’s heart.

<a id="BOOK-B0725"></a>
Presence means attention.

<a id="BOOK-B0726"></a>
It means eye contact. It means listening without reaching for your phone. It means asking the second question. It means noticing changes in tone, energy, mood, and behavior. It means refusing to let the people closest to you become background noise.

<a id="BOOK-B0727"></a>
Second, protection.

<a id="BOOK-B0728"></a>
Relationships need guardrails long before they are in danger. A marriage needs protected time. Children need protected rhythms. Friendships need space. Your home may need technology boundaries. Your calendar may need work cutoffs. Your family may need meals where phones stay away and conversation returns. Your closest relationships may need recurring check-ins before distance becomes normal.

<a id="BOOK-B0729"></a>
Love is revealed by what we protect.

<a id="BOOK-B0730"></a>
Third, formation.

<a id="BOOK-B0731"></a>
Every family has a culture, whether intentional or not. Every home teaches values. The only question is whether those values are chosen or inherited by default. What does your home make normal? Hurry or peace? Faith or compartmentalization? Generosity or consumption? Honesty or avoidance? Service or self-focus? Courage or comfort? Gratitude or entitlement?

<a id="BOOK-B0732"></a>
Formation happens through repeated rhythms.

<a id="BOOK-B0733"></a>
The prayer before school.

<a id="BOOK-B0734"></a>
The way money is discussed.

<a id="BOOK-B0735"></a>
The way conflict is repaired.

<a id="BOOK-B0736"></a>
The way Sunday is treated.

<a id="BOOK-B0737"></a>
The way generosity is practiced.

<a id="BOOK-B0738"></a>
The way people are honored when they are not in the room.

<a id="BOOK-B0739"></a>
The way mistakes are handled.

<a id="BOOK-B0740"></a>
The way Scripture is opened.

<a id="BOOK-B0741"></a>
The way laughter fills the house.

<a id="BOOK-B0742"></a>
The way apologies are given.

<a id="BOOK-B0743"></a>
The way joy is scheduled and not merely wished for.

<a id="BOOK-B0744"></a>
And fourth, multiplication.

<a id="BOOK-B0745"></a>
A healthy family does not turn inward forever. It becomes a source of blessing. It raises children who understand responsibility. It mentors young people who need direction. It opens doors to community. It models faith. It creates stability for others. It uses wisdom, experience, and resources to strengthen lives beyond its own walls.

<a id="BOOK-B0746"></a>
That is why family includes more than the people who share your last name. It includes close friends, neighbors, and those you spend time with and do life with. Some will be there for a season. Some will be there for life. Either way, we are called to be intentional and invest in those relationships.

<a id="BOOK-B0747"></a>
Let your light shine before others.

<a id="BOOK-B0748"></a>
Not for applause.

<a id="BOOK-B0749"></a>
Not for image.

<a id="BOOK-B0750"></a>
Not for control.

<a id="BOOK-B0751"></a>
For the glory of the Father.

<a id="BOOK-B0752"></a>
That is family stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0753"></a>
It is not perfect family photos.

<a id="BOOK-B0754"></a>
It is not pretending everything is easy.

<a id="BOOK-B0755"></a>
It is not raising children who never struggle, building a marriage that never hurts, or maintaining friendships that never require repair.

<a id="BOOK-B0756"></a>
It is faithful presence.

<a id="BOOK-B0757"></a>
It is intentional formation.

<a id="BOOK-B0758"></a>
It is protected connection.

<a id="BOOK-B0759"></a>
It is open-handed multiplication.

<a id="BOOK-B0760"></a>
It is seeing the people God has entrusted to you and refusing to let them receive only what is left over.

<a id="BOOK-B0761"></a>
Before legacy becomes financial, it is relational.

<a id="BOOK-B0762"></a>
Before it shows up in an estate plan, it shows up at the dinner table.

<a id="BOOK-B0763"></a>
Before it becomes something you leave behind, it becomes something the people closest to you experience while you are still here.

<a id="BOOK-B0764"></a>
So before we move to the next pillar, pause here.

<a id="BOOK-B0765"></a>
Think about your spouse.

<a id="BOOK-B0766"></a>
Think about your children.

<a id="BOOK-B0767"></a>
Think about your parents.

<a id="BOOK-B0768"></a>
Think about your closest friends.

<a id="BOOK-B0769"></a>
Think about your neighbors.

<a id="BOOK-B0770"></a>
Think about your church or community.

<a id="BOOK-B0771"></a>
Think about the young people who may be watching your life more closely than you realize.

<a id="BOOK-B0772"></a>
Who is receiving your best?

<a id="BOOK-B0773"></a>
Who is receiving your leftovers?

<a id="BOOK-B0774"></a>
Who needs your presence?

<a id="BOOK-B0775"></a>
Who needs your apology?

<a id="BOOK-B0776"></a>
Who needs your guidance?

<a id="BOOK-B0777"></a>
Who needs to hear, “You were made for more”?

<a id="BOOK-B0778"></a>
One of the commitments we made with those six young men was that the study would not end only with words. We began preparing for a big adventure together: hiking the Grand Canyon Rim to Rim, from the North Rim to the South Rim, roughly twenty-four miles in one day. That kind of challenge is not just about completing a hike. It is about doing something hard together. It is about brotherhood, endurance, courage, and shared memory. It is about young men learning that purpose often requires effort, discomfort, preparation, and perseverance.

<a id="BOOK-B0779"></a>
That adventure also points naturally to the next pillar.

<a id="BOOK-B0780"></a>
Because family stewardship requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0781"></a>
Marriage requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0782"></a>
Parenting requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0783"></a>
Mentorship requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0784"></a>
Community requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0785"></a>
You cannot consistently pour into others if your own body is neglected, depleted, and running on fumes. Your body carries your calling. Your health affects your presence. Your physical strength shapes your capacity to love, lead, serve, mentor, and enjoy the people God has entrusted to you.

<a id="BOOK-B0786"></a>
So in the next chapter, we will turn to fitness—not as vanity, not as image, but as stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0787"></a>
But for now, stay with this first pillar a little longer.

<a id="BOOK-B0788"></a>
Family is not simply something you have.

<a id="BOOK-B0789"></a>
Family is something entrusted to you.

<a id="BOOK-B0790"></a>
And stewardship begins when love becomes intentional.

<a id="BOOK-B0791"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 5

<a id="BOOK-B0792"></a>
This week, begin by identifying what your family or closest relationships are being built on. Begin with the values you drafted in Chapter 4. Do not make this complicated. Write down three to five values you want your home or relational circle to be known for. Maybe they are faith, love, courage, generosity, joy, service, honesty, humility, discipline, adventure, hospitality, or peace. Then ask: Are these values visible in my closest relationships, or are they only words I admire?

<a id="BOOK-B0793"></a>
Next, choose one relationship that needs intentional investment. It may be your spouse, one of your children, a parent, a sibling, a close friend, a grandchild, a young employee, someone at church, or a person you are called to mentor. Decide what intentional investment looks like over the next thirty days. A meal. A weekly call. A handwritten note. A walk. A standing coffee. A book study. An apology. A conversation you have been avoiding.

<a id="BOOK-B0794"></a>
Then create one relational guardrail. Choose one boundary that protects your family or closest relationships from drift. It may be a phone-free dinner, one protected family night each week, a monthly date night, a work cutoff in the evening, a Sunday rhythm, a recurring check-in with your spouse, or a commitment to be fully present at one event without multitasking.

<a id="BOOK-B0795"></a>
Finally, identify one person younger than you who needs encouragement, identity, wisdom, or guidance. Reach out. Do not overthink it. You do not need a formal program to begin mentoring. Sometimes stewardship begins with a simple invitation: “I’d love to spend some time with you and hear how things are going.”

<a id="BOOK-B0796"></a>
The goal is not perfection.

<a id="BOOK-B0797"></a>
The goal is intentional love.

<a id="BOOK-B0798"></a>
Because relationships rarely drift toward depth accidentally.

<a id="BOOK-B0799"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 5

<a id="BOOK-B0800"></a>
Who are the people most clearly entrusted to me in this season?

<a id="BOOK-B0801"></a>
Are they receiving my presence or mainly my provision?

<a id="BOOK-B0802"></a>
Which relationship needs intentional leadership, repair, encouragement, or protection?

<a id="BOOK-B0803"></a>
What rhythm would strengthen connection over time?

<a id="BOOK-B0804"></a>
Where am I assuming love is understood instead of making it experienced?

<a id="BOOK-B0805"></a>
What one conversation or act of presence will I schedule this week?

<a id="BOOK-B0806"></a>
What would change if I saw my family not simply as something I have, but as something entrusted to me?


<a id="BOOK-B0807"></a>
### Chapter 6 — Fitness

<a id="BOOK-B0808"></a>
#### The Body Carries the Calling

<a id="BOOK-B0809"></a>
As I write this, we are preparing for one of those experiences that sounds inspiring in conversation and demanding once the training begins:

<a id="BOOK-B0810"></a>
hiking the Grand Canyon Rim to Rim with Colton and the boys.

<a id="BOOK-B0811"></a>
Roughly twenty-four miles in one day.

<a id="BOOK-B0812"></a>
North Rim to South Rim.

<a id="BOOK-B0813"></a>
A challenge like that sounds inspiring when you talk about it around a table. It sounds meaningful when you imagine the brotherhood, the views, the conversations, the shared memories, and the satisfaction of doing something hard together. It sounds like the kind of experience those young men will remember for the rest of their lives.

<a id="BOOK-B0814"></a>
But eventually, inspiration is not enough.

<a id="BOOK-B0815"></a>
Eventually, your body gets a vote.

<a id="BOOK-B0816"></a>
You do not casually wander into a Rim-to-Rim hike. You prepare for it. You think about hydration. You think about the heat. You think about your legs, your lungs, your back, your feet, your pacing, your recovery, your sleep, and your ability to keep moving when the excitement has worn off and the climb out still remains.

<a id="BOOK-B0817"></a>
That is true of a hike.

<a id="BOOK-B0818"></a>
It is also true of a life.

<a id="BOOK-B0819"></a>
In the previous chapter, we talked about family as one of the core pillars of stewardship. We talked about marriage, parenting, mentorship, friendship, community, and the responsibility to intentionally lead the relationships God has entrusted to us. We talked about those six young men gathered around our table, walking through a study together, talking about faith, manhood, courage, purpose, temptation, identity, and the kind of men they wanted to become.

<a id="BOOK-B0820"></a>
But there is something that becomes obvious when you start moving from conversation to action.

<a id="BOOK-B0821"></a>
Family stewardship requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0822"></a>
Marriage requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0823"></a>
Parenting requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0824"></a>
Mentorship requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0825"></a>
Community requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0826"></a>
Adventure requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0827"></a>
Legacy requires energy.

<a id="BOOK-B0828"></a>
You cannot consistently pour into others if your own body is neglected, depleted, and running on fumes. You cannot fully show up for the people you love if your health has been ignored for years. You cannot say yes to every meaningful opportunity if your body is quietly saying no because of years of drift.

<a id="BOOK-B0829"></a>
Your body is not your calling.

<a id="BOOK-B0830"></a>
But your body carries your calling.

<a id="BOOK-B0831"></a>
In Chapter 4, you began naming what your life is for. That matters here because health stewardship is not about chasing an image. It is about preserving capacity for purpose. Your body carries your calling, and your habits either strengthen or weaken your ability to live the mission you say matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0832"></a>
That is why fitness belongs in the Stewardship Blueprint.

<a id="BOOK-B0833"></a>
Not as vanity.

<a id="BOOK-B0834"></a>
Not as image.

<a id="BOOK-B0835"></a>
Not as ego.

<a id="BOOK-B0836"></a>
Not as punishment for being out of shape.

<a id="BOOK-B0837"></a>
Fitness, rightly understood, is stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0838"></a>
It is the intentional care of the body God has entrusted to you so that you can love with presence, serve with endurance, lead with clarity, enjoy meaningful experiences, and remain available for the people and purposes that matter most.

<a id="BOOK-B0839"></a>
Your body is not your god.

<a id="BOOK-B0840"></a>
But your body does matter.

<a id="BOOK-B0841"></a>
That distinction is important because a chapter like this can easily be misunderstood. For some people, fitness immediately brings up shame. It brings up failed diets, gym memberships that went unused, old injuries, numbers on a scale, clothes that no longer fit, comparison, embarrassment, frustration, or the feeling that they have already failed too many times to begin again.

<a id="BOOK-B0842"></a>
For others, fitness brings up pride. It becomes a place to prove discipline, chase admiration, control insecurity, or build an identity around appearance, performance, pace, strength, or body image. A person can make an idol out of almost anything, and the body is no exception.

<a id="BOOK-B0843"></a>
That means we have to begin with a guardrail.

<a id="BOOK-B0844"></a>
Stewarding the body is not the same as worshiping the body.

<a id="BOOK-B0845"></a>
There is a version of fitness that is not stewardship at all. It is obsession. It is comparison. It is vanity dressed up as discipline. It is the pursuit of a desired body that slowly crowds out the space that should be filled with God. It is measuring worth by a mirror, a scale, a race time, a personal record, a clothing size, a watch, a calorie tracker, or the approval of other people.

<a id="BOOK-B0846"></a>
That is not freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B0847"></a>
That is just another form of bondage.

<a id="BOOK-B0848"></a>
The goal of physical stewardship is not to create a body that receives worship. The goal is to honor a body that remains surrendered to God.

<a id="BOOK-B0849"></a>
First Corinthians reminds us that our bodies are temples of the Holy Spirit. That truth should humble us. It does not mean we idolize the temple. It means we honor what God has entrusted. A temple is not valuable because people worship the building. A temple is valuable because of the presence of God within it.

<a id="BOOK-B0850"></a>
That means health is not about self-exaltation.

<a id="BOOK-B0851"></a>
It is about faithful availability.

<a id="BOOK-B0852"></a>
The question is not, “How do I build the most impressive body?”

<a id="BOOK-B0853"></a>
The better question is, “How do I steward the body I have been given so I can remain faithful to the life God has entrusted to me?”

<a id="BOOK-B0854"></a>
That question changed the way I began thinking about health.

<a id="BOOK-B0855"></a>
For years, I understood fitness in familiar categories. I had been an athlete. I played football at Arizona State. I knew what it meant to train, compete, push, perform, and prepare. I had trained for half marathons and eventually a marathon. I understood goals, finish lines, discipline, and the satisfaction of doing hard things.

<a id="BOOK-B0856"></a>
But being a former athlete does not automatically make you a faithful steward of your current body.

<a id="BOOK-B0857"></a>
That is an important distinction.

<a id="BOOK-B0858"></a>
It is possible to have an athletic past and an unhealthy present. It is possible to know what to do and still not do it. It is possible to understand discipline in one season and drift away from it in another. It is possible to spend years taking care of responsibilities while quietly neglecting the body that carries those responsibilities.

<a id="BOOK-B0859"></a>
I know because I lived it.

<a id="BOOK-B0860"></a>
There was a season when none of our three kids were driving yet. If you have lived through that stage of parenting, you know what that means. The house becomes a command center. The calendar becomes a battlefield. Someone has practice. Someone has a game. Someone has dance. Someone has soccer. There are school events, church commitments, team dinners, forgotten cleats, late-night pickups, early-morning drop-offs, and constant decisions about who needs to be where and when.

<a id="BOOK-B0861"></a>
Kristi and I were trying to be present for all of it. We wanted to support our kids. We wanted to be at the games, the performances, the activities, and the moments that mattered. At the same time, Strategic Income Group was growing. There were clients to serve, decisions to make, people depending on me, opportunities to pursue, and responsibilities that did not slow down just because family life was full.

<a id="BOOK-B0862"></a>
At first, it felt like sacrifice.

<a id="BOOK-B0863"></a>
And sacrifice can feel noble.

<a id="BOOK-B0864"></a>
I told myself this is what good husbands, fathers, and leaders do. You give. You serve. You show up. You put others first. You do what needs to be done.

<a id="BOOK-B0865"></a>
There is truth in that. Love requires sacrifice. Leadership requires responsibility. Parenting requires putting the needs of your children ahead of your convenience.

<a id="BOOK-B0866"></a>
But somewhere along the way, I began confusing sacrifice with self-neglect.

<a id="BOOK-B0867"></a>
There seemed to be time for everyone except me, and part of me believed that was how it should be. Taking time to work out felt selfish. Prioritizing recovery felt selfish. Creating margin felt selfish. Going to bed earlier when there was still more work to do felt selfish. Paying attention to my own health felt like taking something away from my family, the business, or the people I was called to serve.

<a id="BOOK-B0868"></a>
But the body has a way of telling the truth long before we are ready to admit it.

<a id="BOOK-B0869"></a>
I was out of shape. My energy was lower than I wanted to acknowledge. My sleep was inconsistent. Stress was creeping into more places than I realized. I had drifted from some of the rhythms that once helped me stay grounded, clear, and strong.

<a id="BOOK-B0870"></a>
Then my back issues intensified.

<a id="BOOK-B0871"></a>
Eventually, I ended up with three herniated discs.

<a id="BOOK-B0872"></a>
Pain became a constant companion. It affected how I moved, how I slept, how I thought, and how I felt about myself. Chronic pain does something to you emotionally. It wears down your patience. It narrows your focus. It makes you more irritable than you want to be and more discouraged than you are willing to say out loud.

<a id="BOOK-B0873"></a>
The more pain I felt, the less motivated I was to get back in shape.

<a id="BOOK-B0874"></a>
The less I moved, the worse I felt.

<a id="BOOK-B0875"></a>
The worse I felt, the more stress increased.

<a id="BOOK-B0876"></a>
The more stress increased, the worse I slept.

<a id="BOOK-B0877"></a>
And the worse I slept, the less capacity I had to show up as the husband, father, and leader I wanted to be.

<a id="BOOK-B0878"></a>
That is how unhealthy cycles begin.

<a id="BOOK-B0879"></a>
Rarely with one dramatic decision.

<a id="BOOK-B0880"></a>
Usually with a series of reasonable ones.

<a id="BOOK-B0881"></a>
Health stewardship looks different across seasons and bodies. The goal is not comparison or a perfect program; it is honest care for the capacity you have been entrusted to carry.

<a id="BOOK-B0882"></a>
This chapter is not about shame.

<a id="BOOK-B0883"></a>
It is not about pretending everyone has the same body, the same capacity, the same health, the same limitations, or the same opportunities. We cannot all control the body we get. We cannot all control the injuries we carry, the diagnoses we receive, the genetics we inherit, the pain we endure, or the limitations that shape our lives.

<a id="BOOK-B0884"></a>
Some people are dealing with serious sickness. Some are living with chronic pain. Some are navigating disability, autoimmune disease, cancer, surgeries, depression, trauma, neurological conditions, or physical realities that make movement difficult in ways others cannot fully understand.

<a id="BOOK-B0885"></a>
So let me be clear: physical stewardship is not control over outcomes.

<a id="BOOK-B0886"></a>
Physical stewardship is faithfulness with what has been entrusted.

<a id="BOOK-B0887"></a>
That matters.

<a id="BOOK-B0888"></a>
Because not everyone can run a marathon.

<a id="BOOK-B0889"></a>
Not everyone can hike Rim to Rim.

<a id="BOOK-B0890"></a>
Not everyone can lift heavy weights, run six-minute miles, train like an ultramarathoner, or do what someone else’s body can do.

<a id="BOOK-B0891"></a>
I certainly am not a six-minute miler or an ultramarathoner.

<a id="BOOK-B0892"></a>
But most of us can do something.

<a id="BOOK-B0893"></a>
For one person, faithfulness may look like training for a demanding hike. For another, it may look like walking around the block. For another, it may look like physical therapy. For another, it may look like stretching every morning. For another, it may look like getting serious about sleep. For another, it may look like meeting with a doctor, changing nutrition, reducing alcohol, addressing stress, asking for help, or simply standing up and moving more often throughout the day.

<a id="BOOK-B0894"></a>
The point is not comparison.

<a id="BOOK-B0895"></a>
The point is stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0896"></a>
Comparison asks, “Why can’t I do what they can do?”

<a id="BOOK-B0897"></a>
Stewardship asks, “What can I faithfully do with what I have been given?”

<a id="BOOK-B0898"></a>
That question removes both pride and despair. It humbles the person who is tempted to make fitness an idol, and it encourages the person who is tempted to give up because their limitations feel too great.

<a id="BOOK-B0899"></a>
Your body may not be everything you wish it were.

<a id="BOOK-B0900"></a>
But it is still entrusted.

<a id="BOOK-B0901"></a>
And stewardship begins where you are, not where you wish you were.

<a id="BOOK-B0902"></a>
For me, one of the most motivating pictures of health stewardship is not a race medal, a number on a scale, or a pace on a watch.

<a id="BOOK-B0903"></a>
It is a backyard.

<a id="BOOK-B0904"></a>
I picture myself years from now as a grandfather. I picture grandkids running through the yard, jumping into the pool, asking me to play, asking me to swim, asking me to go outside, asking me to throw the ball, asking me to come to the game, asking me to go to the concert, asking me to say yes.

<a id="BOOK-B0905"></a>
And I want to be able to say yes.

<a id="BOOK-B0906"></a>
I know I cannot control everything about the body I will have in that season. None of us can. Injury may come. Sickness may come. Age will come. Limitations may come that I did not choose and cannot fully change.

<a id="BOOK-B0907"></a>
But as much as it depends on me, I want to steward what I have been given now so I can be as present, active, and available as possible then.

<a id="BOOK-B0908"></a>
I want to walk my daughters down the aisle.

<a id="BOOK-B0909"></a>
I want to celebrate key anniversaries with Kristi.

<a id="BOOK-B0910"></a>
I want to keep mentoring young people.

<a id="BOOK-B0911"></a>
I want to host small groups.

<a id="BOOK-B0912"></a>
I want to open our home.

<a id="BOOK-B0913"></a>
I want to invest in the next generation.

<a id="BOOK-B0914"></a>
I want to remain strong enough, mobile enough, and energized enough to keep saying yes to the moments that matter.

<a id="BOOK-B0915"></a>
That is what I am training for.

<a id="BOOK-B0916"></a>
Not a perfect body.

<a id="BOOK-B0917"></a>
A faithful life.

<a id="BOOK-B0918"></a>
This is where the stewardship of health becomes deeply connected to legacy. Legacy is not only what you leave behind when you die. Legacy is what people experience from you while you are still living. It is the walk with your spouse. The game with your grandchild. The conversation around the table. The trip you were strong enough to take. The young person you had energy to mentor. The family member you could serve. The friend you could encourage. The ministry you could keep showing up for.

<a id="BOOK-B0919"></a>
Health is not ultimate.

<a id="BOOK-B0920"></a>
But poor health eventually affects everything.

<a id="BOOK-B0921"></a>
It affects patience. It affects emotional steadiness. It affects leadership. It affects decision-making. It affects marriage. It affects parenting. It affects work. It affects joy. It affects adventure. It affects finances. It affects your capacity to be present.

<a id="BOOK-B0922"></a>
That does not mean every health problem is your fault. That would be unfair and untrue. But it does mean that where we do have agency, we should take it seriously.

<a id="BOOK-B0923"></a>
Discipline creates freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B0924"></a>
That phrase may sound strange because many people think discipline limits freedom. But wise discipline often protects future freedom. Financial discipline creates future options. Relational discipline protects trust. Spiritual discipline deepens faith. Physical discipline preserves capacity.

<a id="BOOK-B0925"></a>
The person who builds strength today may have more mobility later.

<a id="BOOK-B0926"></a>
The person who learns to sleep today may have more patience tomorrow.

<a id="BOOK-B0927"></a>
The person who practices moderation today may have better energy over time.

<a id="BOOK-B0928"></a>
The person who moves consistently today may be able to say yes to more meaningful experiences in the future.

<a id="BOOK-B0929"></a>
Discipline is not the enemy of freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B0930"></a>
Discipline is often the price of future freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B0931"></a>
But discipline must be paired with recovery.

<a id="BOOK-B0932"></a>
That is where many driven people get health wrong. They assume stewardship of the body means pushing harder, doing more, grinding longer, sleeping less, and treating exhaustion as proof of commitment. But God did not design the human body, or the human soul, for endless output.

<a id="BOOK-B0933"></a>
Recovery is not weakness.

<a id="BOOK-B0934"></a>
Recovery is wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B0935"></a>
And for the follower of Jesus, recovery is more than a performance strategy. It is connected to Sabbath.

<a id="BOOK-B0936"></a>
Sabbath was not merely a helpful suggestion. It was woven into the rhythm of creation and later given as a command. God commanded His people to stop, not because He wanted them to be less productive, but because He wanted them to remember who they were. They were not machines. They were not slaves. They were not defined by endless production. They were God’s people, dependent on His provision, invited into His rest.

<a id="BOOK-B0937"></a>
That matters in a culture that celebrates exhaustion.

<a id="BOOK-B0938"></a>
Somewhere along the way, many of us began treating rest as something we earn after we have done enough. But Sabbath reminds us that rest is not only a reward for completion. It is an act of trust. It is a way of saying, “God, You are God, and I am not. The world will keep turning even when I stop. My worth is not measured by my output. My identity is not secured by my productivity. My body and soul need renewal because I am a creature, not the Creator.”

<a id="BOOK-B0939"></a>
There is a reason Sabbath feels hard for ambitious people.

<a id="BOOK-B0940"></a>
Stopping exposes what we are trusting.

<a id="BOOK-B0941"></a>
If I cannot stop, maybe I am more dependent on achievement than I want to admit.

<a id="BOOK-B0942"></a>
If I cannot rest, maybe I am afraid of being forgotten.

<a id="BOOK-B0943"></a>
If I cannot disconnect, maybe I am confusing availability with importance.

<a id="BOOK-B0944"></a>
If I cannot slow down, maybe I have allowed urgency to become my master.

<a id="BOOK-B0945"></a>
That is why Sabbath belongs in a chapter on fitness and health. Recovery is not only about muscles and sleep cycles. It is about worship. It is about surrender. It is about remembering that the body carries the calling, but God sustains the body.

<a id="BOOK-B0946"></a>
A healthy life needs rhythms of effort and rhythms of renewal.

<a id="BOOK-B0947"></a>
Work and rest.

<a id="BOOK-B0948"></a>
Training and recovery.

<a id="BOOK-B0949"></a>
Movement and stillness.

<a id="BOOK-B0950"></a>
Strength and surrender.

<a id="BOOK-B0951"></a>
Exertion and Sabbath.

<a id="BOOK-B0952"></a>
Jesus modeled this. Even with crowds pressing, needs surrounding Him, and a mission more important than anything we will ever carry, He withdrew to quiet places. He prayed. He rested. He slept in a boat during a storm. He moved with urgency when urgency was required, but He was not ruled by hurry.

<a id="BOOK-B0953"></a>
That should humble us.

<a id="BOOK-B0954"></a>
If the Son of God lived with rhythms of withdrawal and communion with the Father, why do we assume we can live without them?

<a id="BOOK-B0955"></a>
This is why a health stewardship plan must be more than a workout plan.

<a id="BOOK-B0956"></a>
It must be a whole-life rhythm.

<a id="BOOK-B0957"></a>
There are five areas I want you to consider as you think about stewarding your body.

<a id="BOOK-B0958"></a>
The first is movement.

<a id="BOOK-B0959"></a>
The body was made to move. Movement does not have to be impressive to be meaningful. A walk matters. Stretching matters. Taking the stairs matters. Hiking matters. Swimming matters. Biking matters. Playing pickleball matters. Strength training matters. Yardwork can matter. The point is not that everyone must move the same way. The point is that most of us need to move more consistently than we do.

<a id="BOOK-B0960"></a>
Movement reminds the body it has not been abandoned.

<a id="BOOK-B0961"></a>
The second is strength and mobility.

<a id="BOOK-B0962"></a>
Strength is not only for athletes. Strength is for parents lifting children, grandparents getting off the floor, travelers carrying bags, servants helping others, homeowners doing chores, and aging adults preserving independence. Mobility matters because a life of stewardship is not only about how long you live, but how fully you are able to participate in the life God gives you.

<a id="BOOK-B0963"></a>
I do not want to be strong only for today.

<a id="BOOK-B0964"></a>
I want to preserve strength for the long road.

<a id="BOOK-B0965"></a>
The third is fuel.

<a id="BOOK-B0966"></a>
What we eat and drink affects more than appearance. It affects energy, clarity, mood, sleep, focus, inflammation, and long-term health. This does not mean food should become another idol. Diet culture can be just as spiritually unhealthy as physical neglect. But it does mean we should pay attention. Food is not merely entertainment. It is fuel. It is also fellowship, celebration, and gift. Stewardship does not require obsession, but it does require wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B0967"></a>
The fourth is recovery.

<a id="BOOK-B0968"></a>
Recovery includes sleep, rest, Sabbath, and margin. It includes knowing when your body needs to stop. It includes refusing to glorify depletion. It includes arranging your life in a way that allows renewal before breakdown. Some people need to recover from overtraining. Others need to recover from overworking. Some need to recover from emotional stress. Others need to recover from years of living as if exhaustion were normal.

<a id="BOOK-B0969"></a>
Sleep is not weakness.

<a id="BOOK-B0970"></a>
Sabbath is not laziness.

<a id="BOOK-B0971"></a>
Rest is not failure.

<a id="BOOK-B0972"></a>
Recovery is part of faithfulness.

<a id="BOOK-B0973"></a>
The fifth is medical wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B0974"></a>
This is the one many people ignore until pain forces them to pay attention. Stewardship includes humility. It includes checkups, screenings, bloodwork, physical therapy, counseling, professional guidance, and listening to warning signs before whispers become consequences. Sometimes the most faithful health decision is not another workout. It is making the appointment you have been avoiding.

<a id="BOOK-B0975"></a>
These five areas—movement, strength and mobility, fuel, recovery, and medical wisdom—are not meant to overwhelm you. They are meant to give you a simple inventory.

<a id="BOOK-B0976"></a>
Where am I strong?

<a id="BOOK-B0977"></a>
Where am I drifting?

<a id="BOOK-B0978"></a>
Where do I need help?

<a id="BOOK-B0979"></a>
Where do I need a guardrail?

<a id="BOOK-B0980"></a>
Where do I need a keystone rhythm?

<a id="BOOK-B0981"></a>
Do not try to change everything at once. That is where many people fail. They become inspired, overhaul their entire life for two weeks, then collapse when real life returns. Stewardship is not built by unsustainable intensity. It is built by repeated faithfulness.

<a id="BOOK-B0982"></a>
One workout.

<a id="BOOK-B0983"></a>
One walk.

<a id="BOOK-B0984"></a>
One better meal.

<a id="BOOK-B0985"></a>
One earlier bedtime.

<a id="BOOK-B0986"></a>
One doctor appointment.

<a id="BOOK-B0987"></a>
One Sabbath rhythm.

<a id="BOOK-B0988"></a>
One return after a missed week.

<a id="BOOK-B0989"></a>
One small act of faithfulness repeated long enough to become part of who you are.

<a id="BOOK-B0990"></a>
A meaningful life is rarely built in one dramatic moment. It is built through repeated stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B0991"></a>
That brings me back to the Grand Canyon.

<a id="BOOK-B0992"></a>
As I write this, we have not yet completed the hike. That means I cannot tell you the final story yet. I cannot tell you which moment will stand out, which boy will encourage another, which section will feel harder than expected, or what it will feel like when we climb out of the canyon together.

<a id="BOOK-B0993"></a>
But I can already tell you what the preparation is teaching me.

<a id="BOOK-B0994"></a>
Meaningful experiences often require physical preparation.

<a id="BOOK-B0995"></a>
You cannot separate adventure from capacity.

<a id="BOOK-B0996"></a>
You cannot separate calling from endurance.

<a id="BOOK-B0997"></a>
You cannot separate legacy from the body that carries you into the moments where legacy is formed.

<a id="BOOK-B0998"></a>
When those boys and I step onto the trail on June 8, the goal will not simply be to complete twenty-four miles. The goal will be to do something hard together. To encourage one another. To prepare well. To endure. To learn that purpose often requires effort, discomfort, discipline, humility, and perseverance.

<a id="BOOK-B0999"></a>
A sustainable health plan begins smaller than most people expect. It may be a consistent bedtime, two walks, strength training appropriate to your body, an overdue appointment, better nourishment, or protected recovery. The best first step is the one you can repeat.

<a id="BOOK-B1000"></a>
Do not use shame as fuel. Shame may create a short burst of effort, but stewardship asks for truth, wisdom, and patient responsibility. The goal is not to prove worth through the body; it is to care for the body that carries your calling.

<a id="BOOK-B1001"></a>
Choose one practice, put it on the calendar, and let consistency rebuild capacity.

<a id="BOOK-B1002"></a>
And that points us to the next chapter.

<a id="BOOK-B1003"></a>
Because fitness is not only about responsibility. It is also about joy. Adventure. Experience. Memory. Play. Travel. Laughter. Shared moments that remind us life was not meant merely to be endured, but enjoyed.

<a id="BOOK-B1004"></a>
In the next chapter, we will turn to fun—not as frivolous escape, but as meaningful experience and adventure within a life of stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B1005"></a>
Because a life well stewarded should not only be faithful.

<a id="BOOK-B1006"></a>
It should also be alive.

<a id="BOOK-B1007"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 6

<a id="BOOK-B1008"></a>
This week, complete a simple Health Stewardship Inventory. Do not begin with shame. Begin with awareness. Look honestly at the five areas of physical stewardship: movement, strength and mobility, fuel, recovery, and medical wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B1009"></a>
Ask yourself where you are currently strongest and where you are most vulnerable to drift. Maybe you are moving regularly but ignoring sleep. Maybe you are eating well but avoiding strength training. Maybe you are disciplined in exercise but have allowed the pursuit of your body to become too central in your identity. Maybe you are recovering from injury or sickness and need to stop comparing your body to someone else’s and begin asking what faithfulness looks like for you.

<a id="BOOK-B1010"></a>
Next, write a one-sentence “why” for your health using the purpose and values you began drafting in Chapter 4. Do not make it about appearance alone. Connect it to calling, family, joy, and legacy. For example: “I am stewarding my health so I can remain present with my family, strong for future adventures, available for ministry, and faithful with the life God has entrusted to me.”

<a id="BOOK-B1011"></a>
Then choose one next faithful step for the next seven days. Not ten. One. A walk. A workout. A bedtime. A doctor appointment. A Sabbath rhythm. A meal plan. A physical therapy exercise. A hydration goal. A phone-free evening that helps you sleep. Choose something small enough to do and meaningful enough to matter.

<a id="BOOK-B1012"></a>
Finally, create one health guardrail. It may be no work email after a certain hour, no skipping sleep three nights in a row without a recovery plan, no major commitments without looking at your calendar, no ignoring pain that persists, no comparing your body to someone else’s, or no allowing fitness goals to crowd out time with God.

<a id="BOOK-B1013"></a>
The goal is not perfection.

<a id="BOOK-B1014"></a>
The goal is faithful stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B1015"></a>
#### Stewardship Practice — The Five-Part Health Inventory

<a id="BOOK-B1016"></a>
Movement: Am I moving consistently in a way that supports energy, health, and longevity?

<a id="BOOK-B1017"></a>
Strength and mobility: Am I preserving the strength, balance, and mobility I will need for the future?

<a id="BOOK-B1018"></a>
Fuel: Am I eating and drinking in a way that supports the life I am called to live?

<a id="BOOK-B1019"></a>
Recovery: Am I sleeping, resting, and practicing Sabbath as if my body is entrusted, not unlimited?

<a id="BOOK-B1020"></a>
Medical wisdom: Am I paying attention to pain, warning signs, checkups, screenings, and professional guidance?

<a id="BOOK-B1021"></a>
After you answer those questions, complete this sentence:

<a id="BOOK-B1022"></a>
“My next faithful step in stewarding my health is __________.”

<a id="BOOK-B1023"></a>
Then take that step this week.

<a id="BOOK-B1024"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 6

<a id="BOOK-B1025"></a>
What has my body been telling me that my calendar has ignored?

<a id="BOOK-B1026"></a>
How is my physical condition affecting my presence, patience, and calling?

<a id="BOOK-B1027"></a>
Which health practice would create the greatest positive cascade?

<a id="BOOK-B1028"></a>
Where do I confuse exhaustion with faithfulness or comfort with recovery?

<a id="BOOK-B1029"></a>
What professional care or wise counsel have I delayed?

<a id="BOOK-B1030"></a>
What one repeatable step will I take this week?


<a id="BOOK-B1031"></a>
### Chapter 7 — Fun

<a id="BOOK-B1032"></a>
#### The Stewardship of Joy (Fun), Adventure, and Shared Experiences

<a id="BOOK-B1033"></a>
Most responsible people know how to schedule obligations. Meetings get scheduled. Practices get scheduled. Appointments get scheduled. Deadlines get scheduled. Flights, calls, workouts, school events, client reviews, projects, chores, and responsibilities find their way onto the calendar because they have to.

<a id="BOOK-B1034"></a>
Joy often does not.

<a id="BOOK-B1035"></a>
It waits for leftover space. It waits for a quieter season. It waits until the inbox is empty, the kids are less busy, the business is more stable, the finances feel more secure, the house is finished, the schedule lightens, or retirement arrives. We tell ourselves we will make time for adventure, friendship, play, rest, laughter, travel, date nights, hobbies, Sabbath, and meaningful experiences when life slows down. But life rarely slows down on its own. If joy is not protected, it is usually crowded out by urgency.

<a id="BOOK-B1036"></a>
That is one danger. But there is another danger too.

<a id="BOOK-B1037"></a>
Some people do not postpone joy. They chase it constantly. They build their lives around pleasure, entertainment, escape, spending, travel, parties, hobbies, screens, food, drinking, shopping, or whatever gives them the next lift. They may call it fun, but sometimes it is not joy at all. It is distraction. It is avoidance. It is relief without renewal. It is pleasure without purpose. It is an attempt to feel alive without doing the harder work of building a life that actually is alive.

<a id="BOOK-B1038"></a>
So we need to be honest from the beginning. Some people neglect joy because they are too busy trying to build a successful life. Others chase pleasure because they are trying to escape a misaligned life. Both are forms of drift. One turns responsibility into dryness. The other turns enjoyment into escape. Neither produces the kind of life we are called to build.

<a id="BOOK-B1039"></a>
That is why joy belongs in the Stewardship Blueprint. At first, that may sound strange. Family makes sense. Fitness makes sense. Finances make sense. Faith certainly makes sense. But joy? Fun? Adventure? Laughter? Shared experiences? Many people hear those words and assume they are too light for a serious conversation about stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B1040"></a>
I understand that reaction. Fun can be misunderstood. It can become excess, distraction, indulgence, irresponsibility, or another form of consumption. But that is not the kind of fun I mean. I am talking about joy, adventure, celebration, Sabbath, meaningful experiences, shared memories, laughter, beauty, wonder, and the kind of life-giving activities that remind us we are not merely machines designed to produce.

<a id="BOOK-B1041"></a>
A life that matters is not only efficient. It is alive.

<a id="BOOK-B1042"></a>
God did not create a gray world and then tell us to endure it. He created oceans, mountains, deserts, forests, stars, flavors, music, friendship, seasons, laughter, feasts, and the capacity to feel wonder. Scripture is full of commands and invitations to remember, celebrate, feast, rest, sing, rejoice, give thanks, and delight in the goodness of God. Joy is not a distraction from faithfulness. Properly understood, joy is part of faithfulness.

<a id="BOOK-B1043"></a>
But joy must be stewarded. That means it must be protected from neglect, and it must be protected from distortion. The person who never makes room for joy needs to learn how to receive. The person who only chases pleasure needs to learn how to discern. Both need a better question. Not simply, “Am I having fun?” but, “Is this joy helping me build the life I am called to build?”

<a id="BOOK-B1044"></a>
#### When Responsibility Crowds Out Joy

<a id="BOOK-B1045"></a>
The danger for many high-capacity people is not reckless pleasure. It is joylessness.

<a id="BOOK-B1046"></a>
They do not reject joy. They simply postpone it. They become so committed to building, providing, leading, solving, improving, and achieving that they slowly forget how to receive. They begin to evaluate every hour by productivity. They measure a good day by what got done, not by whether they were present, connected, grateful, or renewed. Eventually, life becomes useful but thin. The calendar may be full, but the soul feels undernourished. The family may be together often, but not always connected. The money may be increasing, but wonder may be decreasing. The work may be impressive, but laughter may be rare.

<a id="BOOK-B1047"></a>
That is not the life most of us intend to build. No one gets married dreaming of a relationship that becomes nothing more than logistics. No parent looks at a newborn child and says, “I hope we become efficient roommates.” No one starts a career hoping success will cost them their capacity to laugh. No one approaches retirement hoping they have enough money but no memories. But drift does not ask permission. It slowly rearranges the life we meant to build.

<a id="BOOK-B1048"></a>
This is especially true when we confuse seriousness with faithfulness. Some of us have inherited the idea that responsible people should always be productive, always available, always improving, always preparing for the future, always sacrificing. We may even spiritualize it. We call it diligence. We call it leadership. We call it provision. We call it sacrifice. And sometimes it is. But there is a difference between sacrifice and joyless striving. There is a difference between diligence and depletion. There is a difference between responsibility and a life that has forgotten how to delight.

<a id="BOOK-B1049"></a>
A steward does not waste what has been entrusted. But a steward also does not despise what has been given. Joy is a gift. Beauty is a gift. Laughter is a gift. Adventure is a gift. Shared memory is a gift. Rest is a gift. Celebration is a gift. And gifts are meant to be received with gratitude, not buried under endless obligation.

<a id="BOOK-B1050"></a>
#### When Fun Becomes Escape

<a id="BOOK-B1051"></a>
But there is another side to this chapter. Some people do not need to be convinced to schedule fun. Their lives are already organized around it: the next trip, the next purchase, the next night out, the next show, the next event, the next drink, the next game, the next scroll, the next rush, the next experience. At first, that may look like joy. But not all fun is joy.

<a id="BOOK-B1052"></a>
