A wise plan does not remove emotion. It gives emotion a place to submit.

<a id="BOOK-B1774"></a>
This is where behavior matters as much as knowledge. Many people know intellectually that markets move up and down. They know long-term investing requires patience. They know headlines are not a strategy. They know chasing performance can be dangerous. They know panic can be costly. But knowledge under pressure is fragile when it is not supported by structure.

<a id="BOOK-B1775"></a>
That is why wise growth needs guardrails. An investment guardrail is not a prediction. It is a pre-decision. It says, "When markets fall, this is how we will respond." It says, "When markets rise quickly, this is how we will avoid overconfidence." It says, "When someone offers an exciting opportunity, these are the questions we will ask before committing capital." It says, "When comparison makes us impatient, we will return to our purpose rather than someone else's return."

<a id="BOOK-B1776"></a>
This is not about making investing mechanical or emotionless. Money is connected to real hopes and real fears. A child's education, a spouse's security, retirement provision, charitable dreams, business transitions, health care, and family legacy are not abstract. They matter deeply. But because they matter deeply, they should not be governed by whatever emotion is loudest on a difficult day.

<a id="BOOK-B1777"></a>
A plan written only for calm markets is not a plan. It is a preference. A real plan tells you how you intend to behave when markets are noisy, when opportunity is tempting, when fear is rising, and when patience feels foolish.

<a id="BOOK-B1778"></a>
#### Time Horizon Gives Risk a Place

<a id="BOOK-B1779"></a>
Wise growth begins by matching money to purpose, time horizon, and risk. Time horizon is one of the simplest and most important ideas in financial stewardship. Money needed soon should be treated differently than money needed later.

<a id="BOOK-B1780"></a>
That sounds obvious, but many mistakes come from confusing the two. People ask long-term investments to provide short-term certainty, then become anxious when they do not. Others put short-term money at unnecessary risk because the possibility of higher return is tempting. Others hold long-term money too cautiously because a recent downturn made volatility feel unbearable.

<a id="BOOK-B1781"></a>
Every investment decision should be asked, "What is this money assigned to do, and when will it likely be needed?"

<a id="BOOK-B1782"></a>
That question will not guarantee outcomes. Nothing does. But it brings order to risk. It helps keep short-term money stable, intermediate money balanced, and long-term money positioned for growth. It helps you avoid selling long-term assets simply because short-term needs were not protected. It helps you avoid chasing return with money that cannot afford volatility. It helps you stop judging every account by the same standard.

<a id="BOOK-B1783"></a>
Risk is not one thing. There is the risk of losing money, the risk of failing to grow enough, the risk of needing liquidity and not having it, the risk of inflation eroding purchasing power, the risk of concentrating too much in one company or idea, the risk of paying unnecessary taxes, the risk of panic decisions, and the risk of building a strategy you cannot emotionally stay with. A steward does not ask only, "How much return can I get?" A steward asks, "What risks am I taking, why am I taking them, and what purpose do they serve?"

<a id="BOOK-B1784"></a>
This is also why diversification matters. Diversification is not exciting. It will almost always mean you own something that is not performing as well as something else. That can feel frustrating in a world where people love to talk about winners. But diversification is not designed to make every part of the plan feel impressive at the same time. It is designed to keep one wrong bet, one unexpected event, or one difficult season from damaging the whole structure.

<a id="BOOK-B1785"></a>
A concentrated position may be appropriate in some situations, especially for business owners, executives, or families with legacy holdings, but concentration should be understood rather than ignored. If one stock, one business, one property, one sector, one strategy, or one advisor's idea carries too much of the family's future, the plan may be more fragile than it looks.

<a id="BOOK-B1786"></a>
The question is not whether concentration has ever created wealth. It has. The question is whether concentration should be allowed to quietly risk the life you are building without being named, measured, and governed.

<a id="BOOK-B1787"></a>
#### Room for Error Is Not Lack of Faith

<a id="BOOK-B1788"></a>
A plan that only works if everything goes perfectly is not wise. It is fragile.

<a id="BOOK-B1789"></a>
Life rarely unfolds in straight lines. Markets surprise. Jobs change. Health changes. Tax laws change. Family needs change. Inflation changes. Business conditions change. Your own desires and priorities may change. You may discover that a goal you once had no longer fits the life you are called to build. You may face a need you did not expect. You may receive an opportunity you could not have planned.

<a id="BOOK-B1790"></a>
Room for error is not pessimism. It is humility.

<a id="BOOK-B1791"></a>
It may look like liquidity. It may look like diversification. It may look like conservative assumptions. It may look like avoiding concentration. It may look like not borrowing to the edge. It may look like maintaining reserves even when investing feels more exciting. It may look like tax flexibility, giving flexibility, spending flexibility, or the willingness to grow more slowly so the structure remains durable.

<a id="BOOK-B1792"></a>
This kind of humility is deeply consistent with stewardship. A steward does not pretend to control the future. A steward prepares wisely, acts faithfully, and holds outcomes with open hands.

<a id="BOOK-B1793"></a>
In Scripture, Joseph's wisdom in Egypt included preparation for seasons of famine before the famine arrived. That was not fear. It was stewardship. The wise builders in Proverbs pay attention to danger and take refuge. That does not mean they live terrified. It means they do not confuse presumption with courage.

<a id="BOOK-B1794"></a>
The Accumulation Phase requires the same posture. You invest for tomorrow while remembering tomorrow is not yours to control. You build with discipline while refusing to worship the plan. You leave margin because margin is one way wisdom makes room for reality.

<a id="BOOK-B1795"></a>
#### Comparison Tempts You to Abandon Your Assignment

<a id="BOOK-B1796"></a>
Comparison is one of the great enemies of wise growth.

<a id="BOOK-B1797"></a>
Comparison makes patient strategies feel slow. It makes enough feel small. It makes someone else's risk look like genius when markets are rising. It makes your plan feel boring when a friend, neighbor, colleague, or headline appears to be winning faster.

<a id="BOOK-B1798"></a>
But comparison rarely shows the full picture. You see the return, not the risk. You see the lifestyle, not the debt. You see the exit, not the years of uncertainty. You see the confidence, not the sleepless nights. You see the highlight, not the whole household.

<a id="BOOK-B1799"></a>
Comparison tempts you to abandon your assignment. That is why the question is not, "Am I beating someone else?" The question is, "Are my resources growing in a way that serves the life I am called to build?"

<a id="BOOK-B1800"></a>
That kind of growth may look boring at times. It may require patience when others are chasing speed. It may require discipline when others are celebrating risk. It may require staying the course when the market is noisy. It may require ignoring opportunities that sound impressive but do not fit your purpose, liquidity needs, risk tolerance, tax situation, values, or season of life.

<a id="BOOK-B1801"></a>
This is one reason I believe every investor needs some form of investment policy, even if the document is simple. An investment policy is a written expression of purpose, time horizon, risk boundaries, liquidity needs, tax considerations, values, and behavior rules. It helps you remember what you believe when the market is trying to disciple you into reaction.

<a id="BOOK-B1802"></a>
An investment policy does not need to be complicated to be powerful. It may answer questions like these: What is this money for? When might we need it? How much risk is appropriate? What would make us change the plan? What would not make us change the plan? How will we respond to a major downturn? How will we evaluate new opportunities? What portion, if any, is set aside for higher-risk opportunities? What money is not allowed to be put at risk? How will generosity be funded along the way?

<a id="BOOK-B1803"></a>
Those questions are not merely technical. They protect the heart from drift.

<a id="BOOK-B1804"></a>
#### Tax Stewardship and Generosity Along the Way

<a id="BOOK-B1805"></a>
Tax stewardship also belongs in the Accumulation Phase, but it should remain in its proper place. Taxes matter because inefficiency can erode resources that could have served provision, generosity, freedom, and impact. Thoughtful use of retirement accounts, charitable strategies, tax-loss harvesting, donor-advised funds, Roth conversion analysis, business planning, and estate strategies may all have a place in the right situation.

<a id="BOOK-B1806"></a>
But tax strategy should serve the plan, not dominate it. Some people make poor investment decisions because they are trying to avoid taxes at any cost. Others ignore taxes completely and miss opportunities for wise efficiency. The goal is not tax obsession. The goal is tax-aware stewardship. Efficiency matters when it helps resources serve purpose more effectively.

<a id="BOOK-B1807"></a>
Generosity should not wait until the end of the accumulation phase. That may be one of the most important things to remember.

<a id="BOOK-B1808"></a>
Some people assume they will become generous later, when the accounts are larger, the mortgage is gone, the kids are through school, the business has sold, the retirement number has been reached, or enough finally feels secure. But if generosity is always postponed until abundance feels effortless, it may never become part of the heart.

<a id="BOOK-B1809"></a>
Giving is not merely a future event. It is a formative rhythm.

<a id="BOOK-B1810"></a>
That does not mean everyone gives the same way or at the same level. It does mean generosity should be considered part of the life you are building, not an optional add-on after every other financial desire has been satisfied. Giving teaches the heart that money is a tool. It reminds you that accumulation is not the ultimate goal. It keeps your resources connected to love, mission, church, family, community, and causes that matter.

<a id="BOOK-B1811"></a>
A donor-advised fund, appreciated-stock giving, charitable bunching, qualified charitable distributions in later years, or other strategies may be useful tools in certain situations. But the deeper issue is not the tool. The deeper issue is whether generosity is present in the design.

<a id="BOOK-B1812"></a>
A family can be accumulating and generous at the same time. A business owner can reinvest in growth and still give. A young couple can build reserves and still practice generosity. A successful family can tax-plan wisely without reducing generosity to a deduction. The form may change with the season, but the heart should not be postponed.

<a id="BOOK-B1813"></a>
#### When Growth Starts Working

<a id="BOOK-B1814"></a>
There is a quiet moment in the Accumulation Phase when discipline begins to feel different. At first, saving and investing can feel like sacrifice. You are setting aside money that could have been spent. You are choosing patience when there are more exciting uses for cash. You are building something that may not feel visible yet.

<a id="BOOK-B1815"></a>
Over time, however, repeated faithfulness begins to compound. The account grows. Margin increases. Options expand. Debt pressure may decline. Giving capacity increases. The family begins to see that today's restraint is creating tomorrow's freedom. The business owner gains flexibility. The young family sees progress. The future retiree begins to feel confidence. The person who once felt trapped begins to see possible paths.

<a id="BOOK-B1816"></a>
That is a beautiful thing when it remains attached to purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B1817"></a>
But success in accumulation creates its own temptations. Growing resources can make you more confident than you should be. It can make lifestyle expansion feel harmless. It can make you assume past returns are permanent. It can make you believe you are wiser than you were. It can make you hold too tightly because now there is more to lose.

<a id="BOOK-B1818"></a>
The goal is not only to build resources. The goal is to build the kind of character that can manage growing resources faithfully.

<a id="BOOK-B1819"></a>
As resources grow, keep returning to the same questions. What is this money for? What has changed in our life? What risks have increased? What opportunities have opened? What giving should grow? What lifestyle drift should be resisted? What does enough mean now? What would faithfulness look like if this growth continues? What would faithfulness look like if growth slows?

<a id="BOOK-B1820"></a>
Accumulation is not a season to drift. It is a season to build tomorrow with wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B1821"></a>
There will be times when wise accumulation feels slow, especially when a culture of instant results keeps celebrating speed. But slow is not the same as weak. Patient growth can be one of the strongest forms of stewardship because it allows resources, character, and clarity to mature together. You are not only trying to produce a balance. You are trying to become the kind of person or family who can hold growing resources without being mastered by them.

<a id="BOOK-B1822"></a>
#### From Growth to Freedom

<a id="BOOK-B1823"></a>
The Accumulation Phase eventually raises a new question. If the foundation is protected and resources are growing, what should those resources become?

<a id="BOOK-B1824"></a>
For some, the answer will involve traditional retirement. For others, it will involve semi-retirement, a business transition, a career change, a sabbatical, a new venture, increased generosity, or greater flexibility to be present with family. The next chapter will call this the Strategic Income Phase, but do not let the word income make it feel small. The goal is not merely to replace a paycheck.

<a id="BOOK-B1825"></a>
The goal is to design freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B1826"></a>
That freedom will not happen simply because you accumulated enough. It has to be designed with the same clarity, humility, and purpose that guided the first two phases. Growth without a freedom plan can become endless accumulation. Accumulation without enough can become fear with a larger balance. Wealth without purpose can become self-protection with nicer options.

<a id="BOOK-B1827"></a>
So build tomorrow, but do not lose sight of why you are building. Let the Accumulation Phase do its work. Let resources grow. Let discipline compound. Let wise risk serve real purpose. Let generosity keep your heart open. Let comparison lose its voice. Let the plan be strong enough to carry you toward the next season.

<a id="BOOK-B1828"></a>
Because one day, the question will change. It will no longer be only, "How do we grow this?" It will become, "What should this make possible?"

<a id="BOOK-B1829"></a>
That is where we go next.

<a id="BOOK-B1830"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 14

<a id="BOOK-B1831"></a>
This week, create an Investment Policy Snapshot. This does not need to be a formal institutional document. It simply needs to bring clarity to the way your resources are being asked to grow.

<a id="BOOK-B1832"></a>
Start with assignment. Write down the primary purposes for the money you are investing or accumulating. Is it future retirement income, education, business opportunity, generosity, legacy, flexibility, or something else?

<a id="BOOK-B1833"></a>
Then write the time horizon for each purpose. Which resources may be needed within the next one to three years? Which are intermediate? Which are truly long-term?

<a id="BOOK-B1834"></a>
Next, name your risk boundaries. What level of volatility can you financially withstand? What level can you emotionally withstand? What money is not allowed to be put at risk? Where might you be too concentrated? Where do you need room for error?

<a id="BOOK-B1835"></a>
Then write your behavior rules. How will you respond to a significant market downturn? How will you evaluate an exciting opportunity? What would justify changing the plan, and what would not? Who should help you make decisions before fear, greed, or comparison take over?

<a id="BOOK-B1836"></a>
Finally, write one sentence that connects accumulation to purpose: "We are growing these resources so that ________."

<a id="BOOK-B1837"></a>
Let that sentence become a guardrail against restless accumulation.

<a id="BOOK-B1838"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 14

<a id="BOOK-B1839"></a>
What is the money I am accumulating assigned to do?

<a id="BOOK-B1840"></a>
Does the strategy match the purpose and time horizon?

<a id="BOOK-B1841"></a>
Where do I need more room for error or less concentration?

<a id="BOOK-B1842"></a>
What behavior rule would protect me during fear, greed, or comparison?

<a id="BOOK-B1843"></a>
How are tax stewardship and generosity included without becoming the whole plan?

<a id="BOOK-B1844"></a>
What is one action that would make growth more aligned and disciplined?


<a id="BOOK-B1845"></a>
### Chapter 15 — Design Freedom, Not Just Income

<a id="BOOK-B1846"></a>
#### The Strategic Income Phase: Turning Wealth Into Provision, Peace, and Purpose

<a id="BOOK-B1847"></a>
The first paycheck from a portfolio feels different than the paycheck from work.

<a id="BOOK-B1848"></a>
For decades, many people live inside a familiar rhythm. They work, earn income, pay bills, save, invest, give, make plans, and keep moving. The paycheck may come from an employer, a business, a practice, or some combination of work and ownership, but it arrives because active effort is producing current income.

<a id="BOOK-B1849"></a>
Then a new season approaches. Retirement may be near. A business may be sold. A role may change. Work may become optional or less central. The question that once sounded far away begins to feel immediate: Can these resources now provide for the life ahead?

<a id="BOOK-B1850"></a>
That question is often asked with spreadsheets, projections, and account statements. It should be. The numbers matter. Income design, risk, taxes, withdrawal strategy, liquidity, inflation, health care, Social Security, pensions, business proceeds, real estate income, and investment allocation can all matter deeply.

<a id="BOOK-B1851"></a>
But the question is never only financial.

<a id="BOOK-B1852"></a>
When someone asks, "Will I have enough?" they may also be asking, "Will we be safe? Will I still matter? Will we become a burden? Can we help our children wisely? Can we give? Can we enjoy life without guilt? Can we handle the unknown? Will I know who I am when work changes?"

<a id="BOOK-B1853"></a>
A good income plan should answer the financial questions. A good stewardship plan also makes room for the human ones.

<a id="BOOK-B1854"></a>
This is the Strategic Income Phase, and its purpose is to Design Your Freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B1855"></a>
That phrase is intentional. The goal is not merely to create income. The goal is to create income that supports freedom, and freedom that supports faithfulness. If the Foundation Phase is about protecting today, and the Accumulation Phase is about building tomorrow, the Strategic Income Phase is about turning what has been built into provision, peace, generosity, and purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B1856"></a>
This phase often becomes most visible around retirement, semi-retirement, business transition, or financial independence. But it is not limited to a certain age. A business owner in midlife may need to design freedom after a liquidity event. A family may need to design income after a career change. A couple may need to redesign resources after a health event. A professional may reach a point where the question is no longer, "Can I keep working?" but, "What kind of work am I called to keep doing?"

<a id="BOOK-B1857"></a>
The Strategic Income Phase asks a different set of questions. What does provision require? What risks could disrupt peace? What does enough make possible? How should resources produce income without becoming ruled by fear? How can generosity be practiced while we are living? How does this next season remain connected to calling instead of becoming only comfort?

<a id="BOOK-B1858"></a>
#### Freedom Needs a Definition

<a id="BOOK-B1859"></a>
Before freedom can be designed, it has to be defined.

<a id="BOOK-B1860"></a>
Many people use the word freedom without naming what they mean. Sometimes freedom means escape: "I just want to stop working." Sometimes it means comfort: "I want to do whatever I want." Sometimes it means control: "I never want to worry again." Sometimes it means status: "I want to be able to live at a certain level." None of those definitions is unusual, but all of them are incomplete.

<a id="BOOK-B1861"></a>
In a stewardship framework, freedom is the ability to live more faithfully into what matters most.

<a id="BOOK-B1862"></a>
That may include rest, travel, hobbies, family time, or the ability to step away from work that has become draining. It may also include mentoring, serving, giving, creating, building, caring for aging parents, helping adult children wisely, strengthening a marriage, investing in grandchildren, supporting ministry, or beginning work that is more aligned even if it is less lucrative.

<a id="BOOK-B1863"></a>
Freedom is not the absence of responsibility. It is the presence of wisely chosen responsibility.

<a id="BOOK-B1864"></a>
That distinction matters because some people work hard for decades to become free from something but never define what they want to be free for. They build enough resources to leave a job, but they have not built enough clarity to enter a meaningful next season. They have accumulated assets, but they have not named purpose. They know what they want to stop, but they have not discerned what they are called to start, continue, or deepen.

<a id="BOOK-B1865"></a>
This is why retirement can be more emotionally complex than people expect. For some, retirement is joyful and healthy. For others, it exposes how much identity was attached to a title, a calendar, a business, a paycheck, or being needed. When the external structure changes, the inner questions get louder.

<a id="BOOK-B1866"></a>
Who am I now? What is my life for now? Where do I contribute? How do I spend time without drifting into comfort, consumption, or isolation? How do I remain generous, useful, connected, and spiritually alive?

<a id="BOOK-B1867"></a>
A financial plan cannot answer all of those questions by itself. But a wise financial plan should make room for them. The income strategy should not merely ask, "How much can we withdraw?" It should ask, "What kind of life is this income meant to support?"

<a id="BOOK-B1868"></a>
#### Enough Is More Than a Number

<a id="BOOK-B1869"></a>
The word enough returns in this chapter because it is impossible to design freedom without it.

<a id="BOOK-B1870"></a>
In accumulation, enough protects you from endless more. In strategic income, enough helps you turn resources into a life. Enough is not only a retirement number. It is a clarity word. It names what provision means, what peace requires, what generosity should look like, what risks need to be addressed, and what kind of freedom is worth designing.

<a id="BOOK-B1871"></a>
A couple may have enough to retire financially but not enough clarity about how they want to spend their days. A business owner may have enough wealth but not enough liquidity. A family may have enough income but not enough protection against long-term care risk. A retiree may have enough assets but not enough confidence to give. Another may have enough resources but no definition of contentment, so every market movement feels like a threat.

<a id="BOOK-B1872"></a>
The deeper question is not only, "Do we have enough?" It is, "Enough for what?"

<a id="BOOK-B1873"></a>
Enough for basic provision. Enough for peace of mind. Enough for flexibility. Enough for generosity. Enough for health care. Enough for family support without enabling dependence. Enough for travel, rest, service, and shared experiences. Enough for a surviving spouse to be protected. Enough for taxes and unexpected needs. Enough to live with open hands.

<a id="BOOK-B1874"></a>
When enough is undefined, income planning becomes a moving target. Every projection creates another question. Every downturn creates another fear. Every conversation creates another scenario. But when enough is connected to purpose, planning becomes clearer. You can make trade-offs. You can decide what matters most. You can distinguish between real risk and emotional noise. You can begin to say, "This is what our resources are meant to make possible."

<a id="BOOK-B1875"></a>
#### From Accumulation to Paycheck Design

<a id="BOOK-B1876"></a>
One reason income planning is different from accumulation is that timing matters in a new way.

<a id="BOOK-B1877"></a>
When you are accumulating, market downturns can be painful, but if you are still working and still investing, time may be on your side. You may be able to continue contributing, wait for recovery, and even buy at lower prices. The downturn is emotionally difficult, but your income may still be coming from work.

<a id="BOOK-B1878"></a>
When you are drawing income, downturns can become more complicated. If investments decline while withdrawals are also being taken, the portfolio may have less time and fewer assets available to recover. That is the simple idea behind sequence risk: difficult returns early in an income phase can matter more than the same returns later.

<a id="BOOK-B1879"></a>
You do not need to become an expert in the term. But you do need to understand the principle. When resources become your paycheck, stability and growth must work together.

<a id="BOOK-B1880"></a>
That may require cash reserves, income buckets, diversified sources, withdrawal discipline, spending flexibility, appropriate risk allocation, tax planning, or other strategies developed with wise counsel. The point is not to overwhelm you with technique. The point is to show why freedom must be designed, not merely assumed.

<a id="BOOK-B1881"></a>
A paycheck from work usually has a rhythm. It arrives regularly. You know the source. You can plan around it. A paycheck from accumulated resources has to be created from multiple parts of the plan. Some income may come from Social Security, pensions, rental properties, business sale proceeds, annuities, dividends, interest, systematic withdrawals, or part-time work. Some may come from accounts taxed differently. Some assets may be more liquid than others. Some may be more volatile than others. Some may need to grow for later years, while others may need to provide stability now.

<a id="BOOK-B1882"></a>
The design matters because it shapes both math and emotion.

<a id="BOOK-B1883"></a>
If every monthly need depends on selling whatever is most volatile at the wrong moment, the emotional pressure will eventually affect decisions. If near-term income is protected, long-term assets have more room to do their job. That may involve cash reserves, short-term buckets, income strategies, or other structures. The exact design will vary. But the principle is simple: near-term provision should not constantly force long-term assets into short-term decisions.

<a id="BOOK-B1884"></a>
A well-designed income plan helps people remain faithful under pressure. It is not only about maximizing return. It is about creating a structure that supports peace, flexibility, generosity, and endurance.

<a id="BOOK-B1885"></a>
#### Preservation Without Fear

<a id="BOOK-B1886"></a>
Preservation becomes more important in the Strategic Income Phase, but preservation should not be confused with fear.

<a id="BOOK-B1887"></a>
Preservation is respect for what the resources are assigned to do. If money is meant to provide income for the next thirty years, it should not be treated like short-term speculation. If money is meant to protect a surviving spouse, it should not be exposed unnecessarily. If money is meant to support generosity, family, health care, and legacy, it should be managed with durability in mind.

<a id="BOOK-B1888"></a>
At the same time, preservation does not mean refusing all growth. Inflation, longevity, health care costs, future needs, and generosity goals still matter. Burying resources out of fear can be just as unwise as exposing them recklessly. The goal is wise durability: enough stability to provide confidence, enough growth to sustain long-term needs, enough liquidity to handle disruption, enough flexibility to adapt, and enough generosity to keep the heart open.

<a id="BOOK-B1889"></a>
This requires humility because no one knows exactly how long they will live, what markets will do, what health will require, what family needs will arise, or what opportunities may appear. A plan can model possibilities, but it cannot eliminate uncertainty. That is why the Strategic Income Phase should be built with margin and adaptability.

<a id="BOOK-B1890"></a>
Spending flexibility is one form of adaptability. Some expenses are essential. Others can be adjusted. Some travel can be delayed. Some giving can be planned in layers. Some large purchases can be timed intentionally. Some income sources can be activated earlier or later depending on the plan. Flexibility does not mean instability. It means the plan has room to breathe.

<a id="BOOK-B1891"></a>
Tax flexibility matters too. The order in which accounts are used can affect taxes, Medicare premiums, charitable giving, estate planning, and how long resources last. Roth accounts, traditional retirement accounts, taxable accounts, business proceeds, real estate, and charitable vehicles may all have different tax implications. You do not need to master every detail, but you do need wise counsel and a plan that sees taxes as part of stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B1892"></a>
The goal is not to create the perfect plan. The goal is to create a wise plan that can be adjusted faithfully as reality unfolds.

<a id="BOOK-B1893"></a>
#### When Complexity Becomes Available

<a id="BOOK-B1894"></a>
As resources grow, more advanced tools may become available. Private markets, alternative investments, structured income strategies, charitable vehicles, business transition planning, tax strategies, trusts, family entities, insurance strategies, and other planning tools may have a place.

<a id="BOOK-B1895"></a>
But tools should never become the point. Complexity can look impressive while quietly reducing clarity.

<a id="BOOK-B1896"></a>
Every advanced strategy should be tested with simple questions. What purpose does this serve? What risk does it introduce? What liquidity does it limit? What cost does it carry? What problem does it solve? What happens if assumptions are wrong? Can we explain it clearly? Does it support the life we are building?

<a id="BOOK-B1897"></a>
If a strategy cannot pass those questions, slow down. Wisdom is not impressed by complexity for its own sake.

<a id="BOOK-B1898"></a>
This matters because the Strategic Income Phase often arrives when people are vulnerable to either fear or flattery. Fear says, "You need something that guarantees everything." Flattery says, "You have reached a level where you deserve access to sophisticated strategies." Both can lead to unwise decisions if purpose is not guiding the process.

<a id="BOOK-B1899"></a>
Some advanced tools are valuable in the right context. Others are expensive, illiquid, unnecessary, or poorly understood. The question is never simply, "Is this sophisticated?" The question is, "Is this suitable, understandable, aligned, and necessary?"

<a id="BOOK-B1900"></a>
A good advisor should be able to explain not only what a strategy might do, but why it belongs in your plan, what could go wrong, what trade-offs it creates, how it affects liquidity, how it is taxed, how it fits your income needs, and how it serves your purpose. If the explanation depends on confusion, trust should decrease rather than increase.

<a id="BOOK-B1901"></a>
#### Freedom From Fear and Freedom For Faithfulness

<a id="BOOK-B1902"></a>
One of the deepest hopes of financial freedom is freedom from fear. We need to be careful with that phrase because no amount of money can remove all uncertainty from life. There will always be risks. Markets will move. Health will change. Families will face surprises. Tax laws may shift. The future will not ask permission before becoming different than we planned.

<a id="BOOK-B1903"></a>
Financial freedom does not mean you never feel uncertainty. It means your life is not ruled by financial fear. It means you have enough clarity to make decisions, enough stability to absorb disruption, enough income design to provide confidence, enough purpose to know what freedom is for, and enough trust to hold the plan with open hands.

<a id="BOOK-B1904"></a>
Freedom from fear is not produced by a number alone. It is produced by the combination of wise planning, honest purpose, grounded expectations, and spiritual trust.

<a id="BOOK-B1905"></a>
But the more important question is not only what you want to be free from. It is what you want to be free for.

<a id="BOOK-B1906"></a>
Free for family. Free for presence. Free for generosity. Free for service. Free for mentoring. Free for travel that creates connection. Free for Sabbath. Free for work that is chosen rather than driven by fear. Free for causes that matter. Free for a life that reflects purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B1907"></a>
This is where financial freedom becomes beautiful. Not when it becomes self-indulgence, but when it becomes alignment.

<a id="BOOK-B1908"></a>
A person who has designed freedom well may still work, but the work is no longer driven by fear. A person may retire, but retirement is not retreat from purpose. A couple may travel, but travel becomes part of joy, gratitude, and shared memory. A family may give more, not because every concern has disappeared, but because generosity was built into the design. A business owner may transition gradually, not simply to escape pressure, but to create space for the next assignment.

<a id="BOOK-B1909"></a>
Freedom is not the end of stewardship. It is a new season of it.

<a id="BOOK-B1910"></a>
#### Generosity While You Are Living

<a id="BOOK-B1911"></a>
Generosity belongs in this chapter because one of the most meaningful parts of financial freedom is the ability to give while you are living. Not only at death. Not only through estate documents. While you can see the impact. While you can encourage the recipient. While you can teach your children or grandchildren what giving looks like. While you can support a church, ministry, family, cause, or opportunity that God has placed on your heart.

<a id="BOOK-B1912"></a>
There is a particular joy in living generosity. Helping a child without enabling dependence. Supporting a ministry. Funding education. Giving anonymously. Creating a donor-advised fund. Blessing a family in crisis. Supporting missions. Teaching grandchildren to give. Joining God's work with resources that could have been spent only on personal comfort.

<a id="BOOK-B1913"></a>
Generosity changes the emotional tone of wealth. It keeps money moving toward love.

<a id="BOOK-B1914"></a>
This will become a major focus in the next part of the book, but it belongs here because freedom that never becomes generosity can become self-protection with better scenery.

<a id="BOOK-B1915"></a>
Wise generosity in this phase should be both open-hearted and thoughtful. Helping adult children can be a blessing, but it can also create dependency if it is not done with wisdom. Giving to charity can be powerful, but it should be connected to conviction and clarity. Involving family in generosity can form the next generation, but it requires conversation and example, not just documents. Estate gifts can create impact, but giving while living can create shared joy and visible formation.

<a id="BOOK-B1916"></a>
A strategic income plan should therefore include generosity capacity. Not as an afterthought. Not only as a tax strategy. As part of the purpose of freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B1917"></a>
#### Purpose After Work Changes

<a id="BOOK-B1918"></a>
One of the most overlooked parts of the Strategic Income Phase is the stewardship of time and identity.

<a id="BOOK-B1919"></a>
If work has been the primary structure of your days for decades, a change in work will change more than income. It will change rhythm, relationships, responsibility, recognition, and sometimes identity. That can be wonderful, but it can also be disorienting.

<a id="BOOK-B1920"></a>
Some people need rest after intense years of work. That is good. Rest is not failure. Recovery can be stewardship. But rest becomes drift when it has no pathway back to purpose. The goal is not to replace one overloaded schedule with another. The goal is to design a rhythm that allows renewal, contribution, connection, and joy to coexist.

<a id="BOOK-B1921"></a>
Ask practical questions before the transition happens. What will my days look like? Where will I serve? Who will I mentor? How will I stay connected? What will Kristi and I, or my spouse and I, do together? What rhythms will protect health? What generosity do we want to practice? What causes do we want to learn about more deeply? What relationships need investment? What kind of work, if any, do I still feel called to do?

<a id="BOOK-B1922"></a>
These questions matter because financial independence without life direction can become emptier than people expect. The goal is not simply to be done. The goal is to be free for what matters.

<a id="BOOK-B1923"></a>
For some, that will include continued work. For others, it will include a new venture, part-time consulting, mentoring, ministry, travel, family leadership, or quiet faithfulness in places that never appear impressive from the outside. The form is less important than the alignment.

<a id="BOOK-B1924"></a>
The Strategic Income Phase is not asking you to stop building. It is asking you to build differently.

<a id="BOOK-B1925"></a>
That may require some conversations before the numbers are finalized. A spouse may have a different picture of freedom than you do. Adult children may have expectations that need to be clarified with love. A business partner may need transition plans that protect the organization and the relationship. A trusted advisor may see risks you have not considered. These conversations are not distractions from the plan. They are part of the plan because freedom affects more than your accounts. It affects people.

<a id="BOOK-B1926"></a>
The more clearly you name the purpose of freedom, the more wisely you can design the income that supports it.

<a id="BOOK-B1927"></a>
#### What Would Enough Make Possible?

<a id="BOOK-B1928"></a>
Return to the question that began this chapter: Will I have enough?

<a id="BOOK-B1929"></a>
It is a good question. But now ask the better question: What would enough make possible?

<a id="BOOK-B1930"></a>
Enough may make it possible to be more present with your spouse. Enough may make it possible to help your children wisely. Enough may make it possible to mentor, serve, travel, rest, heal, give, simplify, or build. Enough may make it possible to stop working from fear and start working from calling. Enough may make it possible to multiply impact. Enough may make it possible to live the next season with open hands.

<a id="BOOK-B1931"></a>
That is the goal. Not just income. Freedom. Not just freedom. Faithfulness.

<a id="BOOK-B1932"></a>
Part V began with a question: What is your money serving? Then we moved into foundation because unstable resources keep people trapped in reaction. Then we moved into growth because resources should grow with wisdom, patience, and purpose. Now we end with freedom because accumulation eventually raises a deeper question: What should these resources make possible?

<a id="BOOK-B1933"></a>
The answer cannot be only comfort. It cannot be only lifestyle. It cannot be only self-protection. A life that matters does not end with personal security. It begins to multiply.

<a id="BOOK-B1934"></a>
That is where we go next. Once resources provide more than survival, what should they bless? What should they build? What should they support? What should they teach? What should they outlive?

<a id="BOOK-B1935"></a>
In Part VI, we will turn from resources to multiplication: investing what you believe, raising stewards rather than consumers, and building what outlives you. Because the highest use of wealth is not simply to make life easier. It is to make faithfulness more possible.

<a id="BOOK-B1936"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 15

<a id="BOOK-B1937"></a>
This week, complete a Freedom and Income Readiness Checklist. Write answers in plain language. Do not begin with products. Begin with purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B1938"></a>
First, define freedom. What do you want to be free from, and what do you want to be free for? Name the people, responsibilities, causes, rhythms, and opportunities that should shape the next season.

<a id="BOOK-B1939"></a>
Second, define enough. What does provision require? What does peace require? What does generosity require? What risks need to be addressed? What lifestyle assumptions need to be named honestly?

<a id="BOOK-B1940"></a>
Third, identify income sources. What income sources do you understand clearly? What might come from work, Social Security, pensions, rental income, business proceeds, investments, or other sources? Where do you need more clarity?

<a id="BOOK-B1941"></a>
Fourth, review risks. What could disrupt peace or provision? Consider market downturns, sequence risk, inflation, taxes, health care, long-term care, family needs, liquidity, and the possibility that your own goals may change.

<a id="BOOK-B1942"></a>
Fifth, name generosity and purpose. What generosity do you want to practice while you are living? What purpose will guide your time if work changes? Who should be part of this planning conversation?

<a id="BOOK-B1943"></a>
Then write one sentence: "Enough would make it possible for me/us to ________."

<a id="BOOK-B1944"></a>
Let that sentence guide the next planning conversation.

<a id="BOOK-B1945"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 15

<a id="BOOK-B1946"></a>
What does financial freedom mean in this season?

<a id="BOOK-B1947"></a>
What do I want to be free from, and what do I want to be free for?

<a id="BOOK-B1948"></a>
What would enough make possible?

<a id="BOOK-B1949"></a>
What income, liquidity, and risk issues need clearer design?

<a id="BOOK-B1950"></a>
How will generosity and purpose remain active if work changes?

<a id="BOOK-B1951"></a>
What conversation do I need with my spouse, family, advisor, or trusted counsel?


<a id="BOOK-B1952"></a>
### PART VI — WHAT YOUR LIFE MULTIPLIES

<a id="BOOK-B1953"></a>
#### Turning Stewardship Into Generosity, Formation, and Lasting Impact


<a id="BOOK-B1954"></a>
### Chapter 16 — Invest What You Believe

<a id="BOOK-B1955"></a>
#### Aligning Resources with Conviction

<a id="BOOK-B1956"></a>
On Friday, March 1, 2024, I was sitting in a coffee shop with a few men from my Bible study when my friend Todd said, “This study is talking about you.” He named several things that had been happening in the business—details he could not have known—and in that instant I understood what God was asking.

<a id="BOOK-B1957"></a>
The conviction was clear: You need to stop running a Christian-owned business and start running a faith-based business.

<a id="BOOK-B1958"></a>
It was not an audible voice. No one else at the table heard it. The coffee shop did not go silent. There was no dramatic sign in the room. But in my spirit, I heard it as clearly as if the words had been spoken out loud:

<a id="BOOK-B1959"></a>
You need to stop running a Christian-owned business and start running a faith-based business.

<a id="BOOK-B1960"></a>
I knew immediately what that meant.

<a id="BOOK-B1961"></a>
It meant faith could no longer be a label attached to the business. It had to become the filter through which the business operated. Every decision I made as the owner—and more accurately, as the steward—needed to go through a Jesus filter.

<a id="BOOK-B1962"></a>
Faith could no longer be a label attached to the business. It had to become the filter for strategy, growth, hiring, communication, client service, products, investments, risk, revenue, what we would celebrate, and what we would refuse.

<a id="BOOK-B1963"></a>
And then the conviction reached the center of what we do.

<a id="BOOK-B1964"></a>
We help people manage wealth.

<a id="BOOK-B1965"></a>
For years, I had taught that money was not merely something to earn, save, invest, spend, or protect. Money was entrusted. Resources were entrusted. Wealth was a tool for provision, freedom, generosity, and impact. I believed that. I taught that. I built around that.

<a id="BOOK-B1966"></a>
But in that coffee shop, I had to face the next question.

<a id="BOOK-B1967"></a>
If money is entrusted by God, can we treat the deployment of that money as spiritually neutral?

<a id="BOOK-B1968"></a>
That question landed heavily.

<a id="BOOK-B1969"></a>
Because much of the wealth we helped manage was invested in public companies, private opportunities, funds, and strategies. And some of that capital could be invested in, and profiting from, things that did not align with a biblical worldview—things connected to pornography, abortion, exploitation, child labor issues, and practices that violate the dignity of people God created and loves.

<a id="BOOK-B1970"></a>
That was no longer a theoretical concern for me.

<a id="BOOK-B1971"></a>
It became a stewardship concern.

<a id="BOOK-B1972"></a>
For a long time, I had thought about impact investing or biblically aligned investing as something we could offer. If a client cared about it, we could talk about it. If someone asked, we could explore options. If there was interest, we could make it available.

<a id="BOOK-B1973"></a>
But that morning, the conviction became clear.

<a id="BOOK-B1974"></a>
Impact investing—or investing aligned with a biblical worldview—could not merely be something we offered.

<a id="BOOK-B1975"></a>
It needed to become part of how we invested for clients.

<a id="BOOK-B1976"></a>
As quickly as the conviction came, my answer came too.

<a id="BOOK-B1977"></a>
In my heart, I said yes.

<a id="BOOK-B1978"></a>
Yes, Lord. I will do this.

<a id="BOOK-B1979"></a>
And then the panic hit.

<a id="BOOK-B1980"></a>
For a few seconds, there was clarity. There was conviction. There was surrender. I knew what God was asking me to do, and I knew I had already said yes.

<a id="BOOK-B1981"></a>
Then came about thirty minutes of pure panic.

<a id="BOOK-B1982"></a>
I remember thinking, I think I just blew up my business.

<a id="BOOK-B1983"></a>
Then came about thirty minutes of pure panic. I thought about staff, clients, revenue, and the possibility that people I cared about would misunderstand or leave. Some did. The change brought turnover and difficult conversations.

<a id="BOOK-B1984"></a>
That cost matters because conviction sounds inspiring until it changes something you are afraid to lose. It is one thing to say faith shapes work; it is another to let faith shape personnel, revenue, investment philosophy, and the future of a business.

<a id="BOOK-B1985"></a>
My first response was an immediate yes to God. The fear came afterward. Obedience and fear can coexist; courage is choosing the next faithful step while the concern is still real.

<a id="BOOK-B1986"></a>
That was my first fear: that obedience might cost us.

<a id="BOOK-B1987"></a>
The second fear was different.

<a id="BOOK-B1988"></a>
I was afraid the options would not be good enough.

<a id="BOOK-B1989"></a>
I had absorbed a belief that many people still carry: you can either invest with your morals and values, or you can pursue a strong investment strategy, but you probably cannot do both.

<a id="BOOK-B1990"></a>
In my mind, the choice felt like this: Do you want alignment, or do you want return? Do you want to honor your convictions, or do you want a well-built portfolio? Which one matters more?

<a id="BOOK-B1991"></a>
That was the assumption I carried into the process.

<a id="BOOK-B1992"></a>
Over the next six months, our investment committee and research process began putting that assumption to the test. We started seeking out the best possible investment opportunities that could also align with a biblical worldview. We looked across asset classes. Stocks. Bonds. Private equity. Private credit. Different managers. Different strategies. Different approaches.

<a id="BOOK-B1993"></a>
What we found surprised me.

<a id="BOOK-B1994"></a>
There were more opportunities than I expected. Not perfect opportunities. Not risk-free opportunities. Not options that removed the need for wisdom, due diligence, diversification, patience, and discipline. But meaningful opportunities. Robust opportunities. Strategies that could help build out portfolios while seeking to avoid obvious conflicts with biblical values.

<a id="BOOK-B1995"></a>
In many cases, the investment did not need to be explicitly Christian or overtly advancing Kingdom causes. Sometimes the first step was simply to identify investments that were not clearly violating the convictions we were trying to honor.

<a id="BOOK-B1996"></a>
That distinction helped me.

<a id="BOOK-B1997"></a>
Not every investment has to be a missionary.

<a id="BOOK-B1998"></a>
But for a faith-based steward, investments should not knowingly work against the mission.

<a id="BOOK-B1999"></a>
That sentence became one of the clearest ways for me to think about this.

<a id="BOOK-B2000"></a>
Not every company will be Christian. Not every manager will share my worldview. Not every investment will directly advance the causes I care about most. We live in a complex economy. Companies are complicated. Supply chains are complicated. Funds are complicated. Public markets are complicated. Private markets are complicated. Perfect alignment is difficult and sometimes impossible.

<a id="BOOK-B2001"></a>
But complexity should not become an excuse for indifference.

<a id="BOOK-B2002"></a>
A steward does not need perfect visibility to begin asking better questions.

<a id="BOOK-B2003"></a>
What do I believe?

<a id="BOOK-B2004"></a>
What do I own?

<a id="BOOK-B2005"></a>
What am I supporting?

<a id="BOOK-B2006"></a>
What am I avoiding?

<a id="BOOK-B2007"></a>
What am I amplifying?

<a id="BOOK-B2008"></a>
What am I advancing?

<a id="BOOK-B2009"></a>
Those questions changed the way we thought about investing.

<a id="BOOK-B2010"></a>
And they are the questions I want to put in front of you in this chapter.

<a id="BOOK-B2011"></a>
#### Resources Are Not Neutral

<a id="BOOK-B2012"></a>
Part V established money as a tool for provision, stability, growth, freedom, and purpose. The next question is what the tool is building.

<a id="BOOK-B2013"></a>
Most investment conversations focus on risk, return, and taxes. Those categories matter, but they do not tell the whole story. A fourth question belongs in the conversation: What is this investment supporting?

<a id="BOOK-B2014"></a>
Capital is rarely idle. It funds, rewards, strengthens, and scales something. The moral picture is complex, and faithful stewardship does not require obsessive purity or impossible certainty. It does require awareness.

<a id="BOOK-B2015"></a>
If you discovered that a meaningful part of your resources directly supported something that violated your deepest convictions, would you want to know and have the opportunity to respond? Values-aligned stewardship begins with that humility.

<a id="BOOK-B2016"></a>
This is not about shame, spiritual performance, or assuming everyone will draw identical lines. It is about integration. If faith and conviction matter, they eventually reach investments, spending, business practices, partnerships, and giving.

<a id="BOOK-B2017"></a>
The question is broader than a portfolio: Do my resources reflect what I believe?

<a id="BOOK-B2018"></a>
#### The Cost of Alignment

<a id="BOOK-B2019"></a>
When faith stays abstract, it is easy to admire. When it becomes concrete, it rearranges calendars, budgets, business models, investment strategies, and what we are willing to walk away from.

<a id="BOOK-B2020"></a>
A Christian label can coexist with the same assumptions and scoreboards as everyone else. A faith-based life or business asks whether conviction still shapes decisions when no one is applauding and when alignment carries a cost.

<a id="BOOK-B2021"></a>
Faithfulness should not be careless, harsh, or arrogant. Conviction should deepen humility and communication. But fear of losing approval, revenue, or opportunity cannot become the final filter.

<a id="BOOK-B2022"></a>
Alignment begins with a question and eventually requires a decision.

<a id="BOOK-B2023"></a>
#### Risk, Return, Taxes—and Impact

<a id="BOOK-B2024"></a>
One of the reasons values-aligned investing gets pushed to the margins is that the financial industry tends to speak in a narrow language.

<a id="BOOK-B2025"></a>
Risk.

<a id="BOOK-B2026"></a>
Return.

<a id="BOOK-B2027"></a>
Taxes.

<a id="BOOK-B2028"></a>
Again, those matter. They are not unspiritual. They are part of wise management.

<a id="BOOK-B2029"></a>
Risk matters because entrusted resources should not be handled recklessly. Return matters because capital that grows wisely can support provision, generosity, freedom, and impact. Taxes matter because tax efficiency can help more resources remain available for purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2030"></a>
But those categories do not tell the whole story.

<a id="BOOK-B2031"></a>
A portfolio can be efficient and still misaligned.

<a id="BOOK-B2032"></a>
A strategy can be diversified and still support things that contradict the client’s deepest values.

<a id="BOOK-B2033"></a>
An investment can look attractive on a spreadsheet while raising deeper questions about what it funds, normalizes, or profits from.

<a id="BOOK-B2034"></a>
That is why we began asking a broader question:

<a id="BOOK-B2035"></a>
How do your investments impact the communities we live in?

<a id="BOOK-B2036"></a>
That question does not replace risk, return, or taxes. It expands the conversation. It asks whether financial performance can be pursued alongside values alignment. It asks whether capital can be deployed with greater awareness. It asks whether investors should know not only how their investments may perform, but also what their investments may support.

<a id="BOOK-B2037"></a>
For a Christian steward, this is especially important because Scripture never treats money as morally irrelevant. Money is connected to worship, generosity, justice, provision, contentment, greed, trust, wisdom, and responsibility. The Bible does not give us a modern investment menu, but it gives us a worldview. It teaches us that human beings bear the image of God. It teaches us to protect the vulnerable. It teaches us to seek first the kingdom. It teaches us not to conform unthinkingly to the patterns of the world. It teaches us to honor the Lord with our wealth.

<a id="BOOK-B2038"></a>
Those truths should shape more than our giving.

<a id="BOOK-B2039"></a>
They should shape our investing.

<a id="BOOK-B2040"></a>
But this must be said carefully: values alignment does not eliminate investment risk. It does not guarantee a better return. It does not make a portfolio immune from market volatility, manager error, economic cycles, liquidity constraints, or loss. This chapter is not investment advice. It is not a solicitation for any product or strategy. Every investor still needs to consider their goals, risk tolerance, time horizon, tax situation, liquidity needs, and the counsel of qualified professionals.

<a id="BOOK-B2041"></a>
Faith does not remove the need for wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B2042"></a>
It deepens it.

<a id="BOOK-B2043"></a>
A faith-shaped investment process should be rigorous, not sloppy. It should ask more questions, not fewer. It should care about due diligence. It should care about diversification. It should care about risk. It should care about performance. It should care about costs. It should care about whether the strategy can actually do what it claims to do.

<a id="BOOK-B2044"></a>
But it should also care about alignment.

<a id="BOOK-B2045"></a>
#### Avoid, Amplify, and Advance

<a id="BOOK-B2046"></a>
We began using a simple framework to turn conviction into practice.

<a id="BOOK-B2047"></a>
Avoid asks what should be refused because it clearly conflicts with the values we claim to hold. It is an investment Guardrail: do not knowingly allow resources to support what your convictions publicly oppose. Perfect visibility may be impossible, but complexity is not an excuse for indifference.

<a id="BOOK-B2048"></a>
Amplify asks what should be strengthened. Capital, spending, business, giving, habits, and attention all make something stronger. The question is what kind of companies, leaders, practices, causes, and outcomes you want your resources to help grow.

<a id="BOOK-B2049"></a>
Advance asks what should be moved forward through influence, partnership, questions, encouragement, and generosity. A steward does not need to control everything to be responsible for something. You may not change an industry, but you can ask better questions and decide what deserves your support.

<a id="BOOK-B2050"></a>
Avoid creates a boundary. Amplify directs support toward good. Advance uses whatever influence is available to encourage greater impact.

<a id="BOOK-B2051"></a>
The framework applies beyond investments. A business owner can test revenue and partnerships. A family can test spending. A donor can test giving. A leader can test growth. The purpose is not purity or panic; it is greater alignment.

<a id="BOOK-B2052"></a>
#### The Portfolio Is Not the Whole Point

<a id="BOOK-B2053"></a>
There is a temptation in a chapter like this to let the investment framework become the whole chapter.

<a id="BOOK-B2054"></a>
That would be a mistake.

<a id="BOOK-B2055"></a>
The goal is not merely a better portfolio.

<a id="BOOK-B2056"></a>
The goal is a more integrated life.

<a id="BOOK-B2057"></a>
A person can build a biblically screened portfolio and still live with greed. A person can avoid certain industries and still be ruled by fear. A person can talk about impact and still neglect generosity. A person can make values-aligned investments and still use money primarily for self-protection, status, or control.

<a id="BOOK-B2058"></a>
That is why we have to keep returning to the deeper question.

<a id="BOOK-B2059"></a>
What is my money serving?

<a id="BOOK-B2060"></a>
That question has followed us through the entire resources section of the book. Money can serve provision. It can serve peace. It can serve freedom. It can serve generosity. It can serve impact. But it can also serve ego, fear, comparison, comfort, and control.

<a id="BOOK-B2061"></a>
Values-aligned investing is not a loophole around heart work.

<a id="BOOK-B2062"></a>
It is one expression of it.

<a id="BOOK-B2063"></a>
Jesus did not call people merely to organize their accounts more ethically. He called people to seek first the kingdom of God. That calling reaches the heart before it reaches the spreadsheet. It asks what we treasure. What we trust. What we fear. What we love. What we are unwilling to surrender.

<a id="BOOK-B2064"></a>
That means this chapter should not send you on a frantic mission to fix every account by tomorrow. It should invite you to begin with awareness.

<a id="BOOK-B2065"></a>
Look honestly.

<a id="BOOK-B2066"></a>
Ask better questions.

<a id="BOOK-B2067"></a>
Invite wise counsel.

<a id="BOOK-B2068"></a>
Pray.

<a id="BOOK-B2069"></a>
Discern.

<a id="BOOK-B2070"></a>
Take the next faithful step.

<a id="BOOK-B2071"></a>
For some readers, that next step may be reviewing your investment portfolio with an advisor who understands your values.

<a id="BOOK-B2072"></a>
For others, it may be asking what your retirement plan is actually invested in.

<a id="BOOK-B2073"></a>
For a business owner, it may be examining whether the business is truly faith-based or merely Christian-owned.

<a id="BOOK-B2074"></a>
For a family, it may be looking at spending and asking whether consumption is forming the kind of people you want to become.

<a id="BOOK-B2075"></a>
For a donor, it may be reviewing giving and asking whether generosity is strategic, joyful, and aligned with the causes God has placed on your heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2076"></a>
For someone early in the wealth-building journey, the step may be simpler: begin deciding now that future resources will not be allowed to outrun present conviction.

<a id="BOOK-B2077"></a>
This looks different depending on the season of life you are in.

<a id="BOOK-B2078"></a>
But the principle is the same.

<a id="BOOK-B2079"></a>
Your resources should not contradict your convictions.

<a id="BOOK-B2080"></a>
And where they do, the response does not have to be shame.

<a id="BOOK-B2081"></a>
It can be stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B2082"></a>
#### A Better Definition of Impact

<a id="BOOK-B2083"></a>
Impact can become vague marketing language, so it needs a clear definition: the intentional use of resources to support outcomes aligned with what matters most.

<a id="BOOK-B2084"></a>
For a Christian steward, impact is connected to love of God and neighbor, human dignity, truth, mercy, generosity, justice, and faithfulness. It can include what you avoid, what you invest in, what you give to, the people you form, the work you create, and the practices you encourage.

<a id="BOOK-B2085"></a>
Impact is not reserved for people with large portfolios. Everyone has spending decisions, attention, habits, influence, a home culture, and some capacity to give. Faithfulness is measured by alignment, not only by size.

<a id="BOOK-B2086"></a>
Ask: What am I funding? What am I forming? What am I strengthening? What am I making more possible?

<a id="BOOK-B2087"></a>
#### When God Shows the Path

<a id="BOOK-B2088"></a>
Looking back, God did not give me the whole map in that coffee shop. He gave me conviction and enough light for the next step. Surrender was followed by communication, research, difficult conversations, investment committee work, and a process that continues to be refined.

<a id="BOOK-B2089"></a>
The opportunity set proved broader than I expected, and alignment did not require abandoning rigor. Some people left; others leaned in. What I initially feared might destroy the business became an invitation to stop compartmentalizing it.

<a id="BOOK-B2090"></a>
Sometimes what feels like destruction is reordering. God may not be asking you to stop building; He may be asking you to build differently.

<a id="BOOK-B2091"></a>
So ask what you are holding apart. Where has faith shaped belief but not resources? Where were convenience, fear, or default allowed to make decisions conviction never examined?

<a id="BOOK-B2092"></a>
Do not respond with paralysis. Choose one account, one conversation, one review, one question, or one change. A life of stewardship is built by repeatedly bringing more of life under the lordship of Christ—including capital.

<a id="BOOK-B2093"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 16

<a id="BOOK-B2094"></a>
This week, complete a Values-Aligned Resource Filter. Do not begin by trying to solve every financial issue at once. Begin with awareness. Choose one area of your resources to examine: an investment account, retirement plan, business decision, spending category, charitable giving plan, or major financial relationship.

<a id="BOOK-B2095"></a>
Start with conviction.

<a id="BOOK-B2096"></a>
Write down three to five beliefs or values that should shape your resources. They may include faith, human dignity, life, generosity, integrity, family, service, justice, community, creation care, Kingdom impact, or another deeply held conviction. Do not choose words because they sound impressive. Choose words you are willing to build around.

<a id="BOOK-B2097"></a>
Then ask what you own or support.

<a id="BOOK-B2098"></a>
Look at the account, category, or decision you chose. What investments, companies, funds, vendors, causes, products, or practices are connected to it? You may not be able to see everything at once. That is okay. Begin with what you can see.

<a id="BOOK-B2099"></a>
Next, ask the Avoid question.

<a id="BOOK-B2100"></a>
What, if anything, appears clearly out of alignment with my convictions? Is there something I should stop supporting, reduce exposure to, research more carefully, or create a guardrail around?

<a id="BOOK-B2101"></a>
Then ask the Amplify question.

<a id="BOOK-B2102"></a>
What do I want to strengthen? Are there companies, causes, funds, ministries, leaders, businesses, or opportunities that reflect the kind of good I want my resources to support?

<a id="BOOK-B2103"></a>
Then ask the Advance question.

<a id="BOOK-B2104"></a>
What could my resources help move forward? Is there a question I should ask, a conversation I should have, an advisor I should meet with, a giving priority I should clarify, a business practice I should change, or a family conversation I should begin?

<a id="BOOK-B2105"></a>
Finally, write one next faithful step.

<a id="BOOK-B2106"></a>
Not ten.

<a id="BOOK-B2107"></a>
One.

<a id="BOOK-B2108"></a>
It may be scheduling a portfolio review. It may be asking your advisor about values-aligned options. It may be reviewing your retirement plan. It may be changing one spending habit. It may be setting a generosity goal. It may be creating a business filter. It may be talking with your spouse about what you want your resources to reflect.

<a id="BOOK-B2109"></a>
The goal is alignment, not panic.

<a id="BOOK-B2110"></a>
The goal is stewardship, not perfection.

<a id="BOOK-B2111"></a>
The goal is to begin making sure your resources do not quietly contradict the convictions you say matter most.

<a id="BOOK-B2112"></a>
#### Stewardship Practice — Avoid, Amplify, Advance

<a id="BOOK-B2113"></a>
Use these three questions as a simple filter for any major resource decision.

<a id="BOOK-B2114"></a>
Avoid: What should I refuse to support because it conflicts with my convictions?

<a id="BOOK-B2115"></a>
Amplify: What should I strengthen because it reflects what is good, wise, ethical, life-giving, or aligned?

<a id="BOOK-B2116"></a>
Advance: What should I help move forward through capital, influence, generosity, questions, partnership, or encouragement?

<a id="BOOK-B2117"></a>
Before making a major investment, purchase, gift, partnership, or business decision, pause long enough to ask all three.

<a id="BOOK-B2118"></a>
Not every answer will be obvious.

<a id="BOOK-B2119"></a>
But better questions often begin better stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B2120"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 16

<a id="BOOK-B2121"></a>
Do my resources reflect what I believe?

<a id="BOOK-B2122"></a>
Where have I treated investing, spending, business, or giving as spiritually neutral?

<a id="BOOK-B2123"></a>
What should I Avoid, Amplify, and Advance?

<a id="BOOK-B2124"></a>
Where am I afraid alignment might cost me something?

<a id="BOOK-B2125"></a>
Who can help me ask better questions with rigor and humility?

<a id="BOOK-B2126"></a>
What is one next faithful step I will take this week?


<a id="BOOK-B2127"></a>
### Chapter 17 — Raise Stewards, Not Consumers

<a id="BOOK-B2128"></a>
#### Teaching the Next Generation What Money Is For

<a id="BOOK-B2129"></a>
Phoenix summers have a way of making a ninety-minute drive into the pines feel like crossing into another world. Our family cabin in Northern Arizona became a place for cooler air, hikes, creeks, meals with friends, and slower weekends.

<a id="BOOK-B2130"></a>
When Colton started driving, Kristi and I made a decision. We increased his allowance.

<a id="BOOK-B2131"></a>
That may sound like a strange place to begin a chapter about raising stewards instead of consumers. Many parents would assume the way to teach a teenager responsibility is to restrict the flow of money. In some cases, that may be wise. But for us, the increase was intentional. We wanted more dollars moving through his hands, not because we wanted him to consume more, but because we wanted him to practice managing more.

<a id="BOOK-B2132"></a>
There is a difference.

<a id="BOOK-B2133"></a>
At that age, he had real expenses. He needed gas. He wanted to eat out with friends. He might want to play golf, go bowling, grab food after church, or do something spontaneous with the guys. We did not want every one of those decisions to require a request from us and an answer from us. We wanted him to learn how to decide.

<a id="BOOK-B2134"></a>
So we used an online account with a debit card. The kids each had accounts we could manage through an app. The system allowed us to transfer money, track categories, and help them learn in a more practical way. Colton’s allowance was divided into buckets: ten percent to give, ten percent to savings, ten percent to investing, and the remaining seventy percent to spending.

<a id="BOOK-B2135"></a>
It was not magic. It was not perfect. It was not a guarantee that every decision would be wise.

<a id="BOOK-B2136"></a>
That was the point.

<a id="BOOK-B2137"></a>
A system that never allows a child to make a poor decision may feel protective in the moment, but it can quietly delay maturity. If Kristi and I made every financial decision for our kids, they might avoid small mistakes in our home only to make larger ones later when the amounts were bigger, the consequences were more serious, and we were not standing nearby.

<a id="BOOK-B2138"></a>
We would rather have our children learn the value of a dollar while the stakes are still small.

<a id="BOOK-B2139"></a>
One summer weekend, we were up at the cabin. Colton had some buddies with him, and at some point they went to a sporting goods store. While they were there, he bought a pair of riding gloves. They were probably around forty dollars. Not an outrageous purchase. Not some reckless, life-altering mistake. Just one of those things a teenager sees, wants, and decides to buy.

<a id="BOOK-B2140"></a>
The problem was that forty dollars was about the amount he would later need for gas and lunch.

<a id="BOOK-B2141"></a>
Sunday came. After church, Kristi and I left with the girls before Colton and his buddies. He was driving separately. I was heading out of town, beginning the drive back down the hill toward the heat waves of the Valley of the Sun, when my phone rang.

<a id="BOOK-B2142"></a>
It was Colton.

<a id="BOOK-B2143"></a>
“Dad, um, I don’t have enough money to buy gas and get lunch before we head back.”

<a id="BOOK-B2144"></a>
He said it like a statement, but any parent of a teenager would have heard the question behind it.

<a id="BOOK-B2145"></a>
Dad, can you send me money?

<a id="BOOK-B2146"></a>
Dad, can you fix this?

<a id="BOOK-B2147"></a>
Dad, can you make this uncomfortable consequence go away?

<a id="BOOK-B2148"></a>
I did not know when I answered the phone what he was going to ask. But the moment he said it, I understood. And honestly, I could have solved the problem in seconds. The app was on my phone. I could have pulled over, tapped a few buttons, transferred money into his account, and the problem would have disappeared before I even reached the highway.

<a id="BOOK-B2149"></a>
That would have been easy.

<a id="BOOK-B2150"></a>
It also might have stolen the lesson.

<a id="BOOK-B2151"></a>
So I paused and said, “Wow, bud, that sounds like a tough decision. Let me know what you decide to do.”

<a id="BOOK-B2152"></a>
There was silence on the other end of the phone.

<a id="BOOK-B2153"></a>
Not angry silence. More like confused silence.

<a id="BOOK-B2154"></a>
“Uh… okay,” he said. “I’ll let you know.”

<a id="BOOK-B2155"></a>
“All right, bud,” I said. “Drive safe. I love you, and I’ll see you at home.”

<a id="BOOK-B2156"></a>
Then we hung up.

<a id="BOOK-B2157"></a>
Later, I found out he chose gas. He made sure he had enough to get home and waited to eat.

<a id="BOOK-B2158"></a>
That may sound like a small decision unless you have ever been a seventeen-year-old young man after church, on a summer weekend, with buddies in the car and food on your mind. For a growing teenager, choosing gas over lunch is not nothing.

<a id="BOOK-B2159"></a>
But that little moment mattered.

<a id="BOOK-B2160"></a>
He was not in danger. That is important to say. I was not leaving my son stranded somewhere unsafe. He had enough money to get home. He had friends with him. He was only about ninety minutes away. This was not a crisis. It was discomfort. It was inconvenience. It was the natural consequence of a small spending decision.

<a id="BOOK-B2161"></a>
And sometimes discomfort is a better teacher than another lecture.

<a id="BOOK-B2162"></a>
The lesson was not, “Your dad will not help you.”

<a id="BOOK-B2163"></a>
The lesson was, “Your decisions have consequences, and you are capable of making the responsible choice.”

<a id="BOOK-B2164"></a>
That was good for Colton.

<a id="BOOK-B2165"></a>
It was also good for me.

<a id="BOOK-B2166"></a>
Because if I am honest, part of the reason he called is that there had been plenty of times in the past when Kristi and I had given him extra money or paid for things that could have been his responsibility. Sometimes we did it because we wanted to be generous. Sometimes because it was easier. Sometimes because we did not want the hassle. Sometimes because we could. And sometimes, I am sure, because rescuing our kids can make us feel like we are helping even when we may actually be delaying the growth they need.

<a id="BOOK-B2167"></a>
That day was probably as surprising for him as it was for me.

<a id="BOOK-B2168"></a>
Teaching moments do not always come naturally to me. I wish I could say that every time one of our children faces a consequence, I calmly discern the long-term formation opportunity and respond with perfect wisdom. That is not true. I have to think about it. I have to slow down. I have to resist the urge to fix every problem just because I have the power to fix it.

<a id="BOOK-B2169"></a>
Parenting is not only about protecting children from danger. It is also about preparing them for responsibility.

<a id="BOOK-B2170"></a>
And those are not the same thing.

<a id="BOOK-B2171"></a>
#### The Hidden Curriculum of Money

<a id="BOOK-B2172"></a>
Every family teaches about money—intentionally or accidentally. Children learn from the way adults talk about bills, work, generosity, debt, vacations, taxes, comparison, people with more, and people with less. They learn what creates stress, what earns praise, and what the family celebrates.

<a id="BOOK-B2173"></a>
They are learning whether money is a tool or a scoreboard, whether generosity is first or leftover, whether comfort is the goal, and whether faith shapes financial decisions.

<a id="BOOK-B2174"></a>
Financial facts matter, but information alone is not formation. A consumer can understand money and still be mastered by it. A steward learns what money is for.

<a id="BOOK-B2175"></a>
Culture will train appetite by default. Stewardship teaches better questions: Do I need this? What am I giving up? Does this align with who I am becoming? Is there a better assignment for these dollars?

<a id="BOOK-B2176"></a>
#### Let Money Pass Through Their Hands

<a id="BOOK-B2177"></a>
When our kids were younger, we used jars.

<a id="BOOK-B2178"></a>
Three jars sat on their dressers in their rooms, where the kids could see them: give, save, and spend. We also used buttons. The buttons eventually represented twenty-five cents. The kids could earn them for tasks, chores, being helpful, showing responsibility, or loving on their siblings. It was part reward system, part allowance system, and part financial classroom.

<a id="BOOK-B2179"></a>
The system did more than track behavior. It made money visible.

<a id="BOOK-B2180"></a>
A young child does not naturally understand numbers on a screen. Even cash can feel abstract at first. But buttons in a jar are tangible. They can be counted, moved, exchanged, and watched. A child can see the difference between an empty jar and a full one. They can experience the satisfaction of accumulation and the cost of spending.

<a id="BOOK-B2181"></a>
We would help them exchange buttons for quarters. Then four quarters became a dollar. Five dollars could become a five-dollar bill. Slowly, money became more than a concept. It became something they could touch, count, trade, assign, save, and give.

<a id="BOOK-B2182"></a>
The jars taught them that money has destinations.

<a id="BOOK-B2183"></a>
Some is given.

<a id="BOOK-B2184"></a>
Some is saved.

<a id="BOOK-B2185"></a>
Some is spent.

<a id="BOOK-B2186"></a>
As they grew, the system grew with them. The jars became digital accounts. The allowance increased. The categories matured. We added an investing bucket. The principles stayed the same, but the tools changed.

<a id="BOOK-B2187"></a>
That progression matters.

<a id="BOOK-B2188"></a>
A system that works for a six-year-old should not be expected to work the same way for a sixteen-year-old. Stewardship training must grow with responsibility. Young children need visible categories and simple habits. Teenagers need increasing freedom, increasing responsibility, and enough real-world decision-making to feel the weight of their choices.

<a id="BOOK-B2189"></a>
That is why Colton’s allowance increased when he started driving. We did not increase it simply because he was older. We increased it because his responsibilities increased. Gas became his responsibility. Some meals with friends became his responsibility. Activities like golf or bowling became decisions he could make within the limits of what he had.

<a id="BOOK-B2190"></a>
More money moved through his hands because more stewardship was being transferred to him.

<a id="BOOK-B2191"></a>
This is a principle worth considering for every parent, grandparent, mentor, or leader:

<a id="BOOK-B2192"></a>
Children cannot learn to steward money that never passes through their hands.

<a id="BOOK-B2193"></a>
If we buy everything for them, decide everything for them, approve everything for them, and rescue every consequence, we may keep the family system running smoothly for a while. But we may also train them to consume without counting the cost.

<a id="BOOK-B2194"></a>
This is why Kristi and I have tried to avoid being the automatic “yes” or “no” to every purchase.

<a id="BOOK-B2195"></a>
