When one of the kids asks for something, we do not always have to say, “No, you cannot have that.” Sometimes the better answer is, “You have money. Do you want to spend your money on that?”

<a id="BOOK-B2196"></a>
That question changes the conversation.

<a id="BOOK-B2197"></a>
When the money is coming from Mom and Dad, the desire often feels simple. Of course they want it. Why wouldn’t they? But when the purchase requires their own money, discernment begins. Suddenly the item has a cost. Suddenly the trade-off is visible. Suddenly the question is not only, “Do I want it?” but, “Do I want it enough to give up something else?”

<a id="BOOK-B2198"></a>
That is stewardship practice.

<a id="BOOK-B2199"></a>
It does not mean they always choose wisely. Neither do adults. But every decision becomes a small rehearsal for larger decisions later.

<a id="BOOK-B2200"></a>
A forty-dollar pair of riding gloves can teach something.

<a id="BOOK-B2201"></a>
So can an empty spending account.

<a id="BOOK-B2202"></a>
So can the choice between gas and lunch.

<a id="BOOK-B2203"></a>
Small mistakes can become valuable tuition.

<a id="BOOK-B2204"></a>
#### Give, Save, Invest, and Spend on Purpose

<a id="BOOK-B2205"></a>
From the beginning, our system gave money four destinations: give, save, invest, and spend. The percentages are not sacred; the direction is.

<a id="BOOK-B2206"></a>
Giving first teaches that money is not only for us and that God, not appetite, receives the first word. Saving teaches patience and margin. Investing introduces time, ownership, risk, and compounding. Spending teaches gratitude, trade-offs, and the word enough.

<a id="BOOK-B2207"></a>
Spending is not unspiritual. Food with friends, hobbies, and meaningful experiences can be good gifts. The issue is whether spending is thoughtful and grateful or compulsive, comparison-fueled, and disconnected from purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2208"></a>
Every dollar needs direction before desire grabs it: give first, save intentionally, invest patiently, and spend wisely.

<a id="BOOK-B2209"></a>
#### Do Not Confuse Provision With Preparation

<a id="BOOK-B2210"></a>
One of the hardest parts of parenting is knowing when to provide and when to prepare.

<a id="BOOK-B2211"></a>
Provision is good. Parents are called to care for their children. We provide food, shelter, clothing, safety, education, guidance, love, discipline, encouragement, and support. We do not make children fend for themselves in ways they are not ready for. We do not pretend that neglect is maturity training.

<a id="BOOK-B2212"></a>
But preparation is also part of love.

<a id="BOOK-B2213"></a>
If provision never becomes preparation, a child can grow up surrounded by resources but lacking resilience. They can have access without wisdom. Comfort without gratitude. Opportunity without responsibility. Confidence without competence.

<a id="BOOK-B2214"></a>
That is not stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B2215"></a>
This is especially important for families who have been blessed financially. The more resources a family has, the easier it can be to remove every obstacle. Pay the fee. Buy the item. Cover the shortfall. Upgrade the experience. Solve the discomfort. Smooth the road.

<a id="BOOK-B2216"></a>
And sometimes that is appropriate.

<a id="BOOK-B2217"></a>
But not always.

<a id="BOOK-B2218"></a>
A parent can remove so many small struggles that the child never develops the muscles needed for larger ones.

<a id="BOOK-B2219"></a>
That is where I have to examine myself. When Colton called about gas and lunch, the issue was not whether I loved him. I did. The issue was not whether I had the ability to help him. I did. The issue was whether helping him in that moment would have been love or merely rescue.

<a id="BOOK-B2220"></a>
Those are not always easy to separate.

<a id="BOOK-B2221"></a>
Rescue often looks like love because it brings immediate relief. It relieves the child’s discomfort and the parent’s discomfort. No one has to feel awkward. No one has to be hungry. No one has to sit with the consequence. No one has to learn the hard way.

<a id="BOOK-B2222"></a>
But immediate relief is not always the same as long-term formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2223"></a>
Sometimes the most loving thing a parent can do is refuse to steal a lesson their child is ready to learn.

<a id="BOOK-B2224"></a>
That sentence is easy to write and much harder to live.

<a id="BOOK-B2225"></a>
It requires discernment. It requires patience. It requires emotional strength. It requires a willingness to let your child be frustrated with you for a moment. It requires remembering that your goal is not to be needed forever. Your goal is to help them become faithful, wise, responsible people who can make decisions when you are not there.

<a id="BOOK-B2226"></a>
The same principle applies beyond parenting. Leaders and mentors can create dependence when they continually solve problems others are ready to learn to solve. Influence always teaches; the question is what it is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2227"></a>
#### Talk About What Money Is Doing

<a id="BOOK-B2228"></a>
Money should not be a taboo subject in the home.

<a id="BOOK-B2229"></a>
That does not mean children need to know every financial detail before they are mature enough to carry it. It does not mean parents should create anxiety by oversharing adult concerns. It does not mean every child needs to know income, net worth, account balances, or complex planning details at the same age.

<a id="BOOK-B2230"></a>
But silence is not the same as wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B2231"></a>
When families never talk about money, children are left to interpret what they see without guidance. They may see spending but not planning. They may see generosity but not sacrifice. They may see income but not work. They may see lifestyle but not discipline. They may see blessing but not responsibility.

<a id="BOOK-B2232"></a>
So we have tried to talk with our kids along the way.

<a id="BOOK-B2233"></a>
We have talked about assets that appreciate and things that depreciate. We have talked about the difference between buying something that can grow in value and buying something that begins losing value the moment you own it. We have talked about debt. We have tried to distinguish between debt that can be used prudently, like a responsible mortgage on a home, and debt that can quietly steal future freedom, like credit card debt, unnecessary auto debt, personal loans, or lifestyle borrowing.

<a id="BOOK-B2234"></a>
The point is not to make them financial experts as teenagers.

<a id="BOOK-B2235"></a>
The point is to give them categories.

<a id="BOOK-B2236"></a>
A child who has categories can begin to interpret the world.

<a id="BOOK-B2237"></a>
They can see an advertisement and understand that marketing is designed to create desire. They can see a car payment and understand that the monthly amount is not the same as the real cost. They can see a credit card and understand that convenience can become bondage if not handled wisely. They can see an investment account and understand that time is part of growth. They can see a college savings account and understand that preparation often happens long before opportunity arrives.

<a id="BOOK-B2238"></a>
We have also told our children that we have college savings accounts for them. That is a form of provision. But even that conversation can become formation if framed correctly. The message is not, “You are entitled to whatever education costs, no matter what.” The message is, “We have been preparing because education matters, your future matters, and resources should be directed toward purpose.”

<a id="BOOK-B2239"></a>
The words we attach to financial decisions matter.

<a id="BOOK-B2240"></a>
Buying something for a child can teach entitlement or gratitude.

<a id="BOOK-B2241"></a>
Saying no can teach scarcity or discernment.

<a id="BOOK-B2242"></a>
Giving an allowance can teach consumption or stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B2243"></a>
Paying for college can teach privilege or purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2244"></a>
Helping with a car can teach dependence or responsibility.

<a id="BOOK-B2245"></a>
The difference is often in the conversation.

<a id="BOOK-B2246"></a>
#### The Home Is a Discipleship Environment

<a id="BOOK-B2247"></a>
Deuteronomy 6 places formation inside ordinary rhythms—sitting at home, walking along the road, lying down, and getting up. Money formation works the same way: in the purchase request, the empty account, the first paycheck, the giving decision, the college choice, and the conversation after a mistake.

<a id="BOOK-B2248"></a>
The home is always teaching. A jar, budget, generous tip, decision not to buy, market reaction, apology after overspending, or family gift can all become part of the curriculum.

<a id="BOOK-B2249"></a>
As children mature, responsibility should increase. They need to manage real accounts, make plans before money arrives, experience trade-offs, and make some mistakes while the consequences are still small.

<a id="BOOK-B2250"></a>
A system does not form character by itself, but it creates repeated practice—and repeated practice shapes instincts.

<a id="BOOK-B2251"></a>
#### What They See in Us

<a id="BOOK-B2252"></a>
There is one part of this chapter that would be easier to skip.

<a id="BOOK-B2253"></a>
Our children will not only learn from the systems we create for them. They will learn from the way we live.

<a id="BOOK-B2254"></a>
If I tell my children to be generous but they never see generosity cost me anything, the lesson is thin.

<a id="BOOK-B2255"></a>
If I tell them to avoid comparison but I am always chasing the next visible sign of success, they will notice.

<a id="BOOK-B2256"></a>
If I tell them money is a tool but my stress, mood, identity, and peace rise and fall with financial outcomes, they will notice.

<a id="BOOK-B2257"></a>
If I tell them debt can be dangerous but I use debt to maintain appearances, they will notice.

<a id="BOOK-B2258"></a>
If I tell them to be grateful but I am constantly talking about what we still do not have, they will notice.

<a id="BOOK-B2259"></a>
This is not a call to perfect parenting. No parent has modeled this flawlessly. I have not. Kristi and I have had to learn as we go. We have given extra money when we probably should have let the lesson land. We have paid for things that could have been the kids’ responsibility. We have had to adjust systems. We have had moments where convenience won over formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2260"></a>
That is part of the journey.

<a id="BOOK-B2261"></a>
But even our imperfections can become lessons if we are willing to name them.

<a id="BOOK-B2262"></a>
A parent who says, “I think I rescued you too quickly last time, and I want to help you grow in responsibility,” is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2263"></a>
A parent who says, “We bought that impulsively, and looking back, I do not think it was wise,” is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2264"></a>
A parent who says, “We can afford it, but we are choosing not to buy it because our money has other priorities,” is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2265"></a>
A parent who says, “We are giving to this because God has blessed us and we want to be a blessing,” is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2266"></a>
A parent who says, “I was wrong,” is teaching.

<a id="BOOK-B2267"></a>
Stewardship formation does not require flawless parents.

<a id="BOOK-B2268"></a>
It requires honest ones.

<a id="BOOK-B2269"></a>
The goal is not for our kids to think we never make mistakes. The goal is for them to see us continually bring our decisions back under faith, wisdom, and purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2270"></a>
#### This Is Bigger Than Parenting

<a id="BOOK-B2271"></a>
Not every reader has children, but everyone is forming someone. Grandparents, employers, teachers, coaches, pastors, advisors, aunts, uncles, neighbors, and mentors all model what money, ambition, generosity, gratitude, and success mean.

<a id="BOOK-B2272"></a>
The next generation is shaped in homes, churches, businesses, schools, teams, neighborhoods, and ordinary conversations. Before wealth transfers, wisdom must transfer.

<a id="BOOK-B2273"></a>
Resources can expand opportunity or magnify immaturity. The deeper inheritance question is not only, “How much should they receive?” It is, “Who are they becoming, and what are we helping them become?”

<a id="BOOK-B2274"></a>
A consumer asks, “What can this do for me?” A steward asks, “What has been entrusted to me, and how can I use it faithfully?” The goal is not financially impressive children or mentees. It is people who can receive blessing without entitlement and manage resources without being mastered by them.

<a id="BOOK-B2275"></a>
#### The Conversation Starts Now

<a id="BOOK-B2276"></a>
The temptation with a chapter like this is to agree in principle and delay in practice.

<a id="BOOK-B2277"></a>
One day we will talk to the kids about money.

<a id="BOOK-B2278"></a>
One day we will explain giving.

<a id="BOOK-B2279"></a>
One day we will set up the allowance system.

<a id="BOOK-B2280"></a>
One day we will include them in a generosity conversation.

<a id="BOOK-B2281"></a>
One day we will stop rescuing every shortfall.

<a id="BOOK-B2282"></a>
One day we will talk to our adult children about values and inheritance.

<a id="BOOK-B2283"></a>
One day we will mentor someone younger.

<a id="BOOK-B2284"></a>
But formation rarely happens through one dramatic conversation someday. It happens through small conversations now.

<a id="BOOK-B2285"></a>
Start where you are.

<a id="BOOK-B2286"></a>
If your children are young, start with jars. Make money visible. Teach give, save, and spend. Let them count. Let them exchange coins. Let them experience the joy of giving and the cost of spending.

<a id="BOOK-B2287"></a>
If your children are teenagers, increase responsibility. Give them categories to manage. Let them pay for some of the things they want. Let them make decisions while the stakes are still small. Resist the urge to turn every shortfall into a transfer.

<a id="BOOK-B2288"></a>
If your children are adults, begin having honest conversations. Not controlling conversations. Not manipulative conversations. Honest ones. Talk about values. Talk about work. Talk about generosity. Talk about what kind of life resources are meant to serve. Ask questions. Listen well.

<a id="BOOK-B2289"></a>
If you are a grandparent, connect gifts to meaning. Consider creating experiences around generosity, service, education, or family values instead of only consumption.

<a id="BOOK-B2290"></a>
If you are a mentor or leader, do not underestimate the power of one practical conversation. A young person may need someone to explain debt, saving, giving, investing, or financial boundaries in plain language. They may need someone to model a different way.

<a id="BOOK-B2291"></a>
You do not need a perfect system to begin.

<a id="BOOK-B2292"></a>
You need one conversation, one practice, and one modeling commitment.

<a id="BOOK-B2293"></a>
One conversation: talk about what money is for.

<a id="BOOK-B2294"></a>
One practice: create a rhythm that gives money a purpose before it is spent.

<a id="BOOK-B2295"></a>
One modeling commitment: let someone see you make a financial decision shaped by faith, wisdom, generosity, or restraint.

<a id="BOOK-B2296"></a>
That is how stewardship begins to multiply.

<a id="BOOK-B2297"></a>
#### The Lesson on the Hill

<a id="BOOK-B2298"></a>
I still think about the call from Colton and how easy it would have been to solve the problem in seconds. The silence after I handed the decision back to him was the sound of responsibility being returned—not harshly or dangerously, but clearly.

<a id="BOOK-B2299"></a>
If the life we are building is meant to multiply, we cannot think only about what resources can buy. We must ask what they are forming: gratitude or entitlement, responsibility or dependence, generosity or consumption, wisdom or impulse.

<a id="BOOK-B2300"></a>
No parent, mentor, or leader can make every choice forever. We can help form the kind of person who knows how to choose.

<a id="BOOK-B2301"></a>
Raise stewards, not consumers. The highest use of wealth is not merely to make life easier for the next generation; it is to help them faithfully manage whatever God entrusts to them.

<a id="BOOK-B2302"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 17

<a id="BOOK-B2303"></a>
This week, create a Family Stewardship Conversation Guide or a mentoring plan. Do not make it complicated. The purpose is to move from intention to formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2304"></a>
Begin with one conversation. Choose one child, grandchild, adult child, mentee, employee, or younger person in your sphere of influence. Ask a simple question: “What do you think money is for?” Then listen. Do not correct too quickly. Let the answer show you what has already been formed.

<a id="BOOK-B2305"></a>
Next, choose one practice. If you have young children, consider a give-save-spend jar system. If you have teenagers, consider a digital account with clear categories for giving, saving, investing, and spending. If you have adult children, consider a values conversation around generosity, work, debt, or future family decisions. If you are mentoring someone, help them create a simple plan for the next dollar they receive.

<a id="BOOK-B2306"></a>
Then choose one modeling commitment. Let the next generation see one financial decision you are making because of faith, wisdom, generosity, restraint, or purpose. Explain it in plain language. You might say, “We are choosing not to buy this because our money has another assignment,” or “We give first because we want our hearts to remember that everything we have is entrusted,” or “We are saving for this because future freedom matters.”

<a id="BOOK-B2307"></a>
Finally, identify one rescue pattern you may need to reconsider. Where are you consistently solving a problem that someone else may be ready to learn from? Be careful. Do not remove support where protection is needed. But be honest about where your help may be delaying responsibility.

<a id="BOOK-B2308"></a>
The goal is not to create financial perfection.

<a id="BOOK-B2309"></a>
The goal is formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2310"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 17

<a id="BOOK-B2311"></a>
What did the home I grew up in teach me about money?

<a id="BOOK-B2312"></a>
What are younger people learning from the way I handle resources?

<a id="BOOK-B2313"></a>
Where am I modeling stewardship well, and where am I modeling fear, comparison, or consumption?

<a id="BOOK-B2314"></a>
What age-appropriate responsibility or practice could I transfer now?

<a id="BOOK-B2315"></a>
Where might rescue be delaying formation?

<a id="BOOK-B2316"></a>
What one conversation will I begin this week about what money is for?


<a id="BOOK-B2317"></a>
### Chapter 18 — Build What Outlives You

<a id="BOOK-B2318"></a>
#### Generosity, Family Mission, and the Stewardship of Impact

<a id="BOOK-B2319"></a>
I have a friend about twenty years ahead of me whose family gives me a picture of intentional legacy. Each year, the adult children, spouses, and grandchildren gather for a family trip. They enjoy the time together, but they also review the projects their family supports around the world. One daughter now oversees much of that work and reports to the family.

<a id="BOOK-B2320"></a>
What moves me is not only the scale of the giving. It is the transfer of conviction. The next generation is already learning, discerning, evaluating, and participating rather than waiting to inherit assets and then decide what they were for.

<a id="BOOK-B2321"></a>
That picture shapes my prayers for Team G Foundation. I want our children and future grandchildren to be invited into generosity, to know why we give, and to help discern the people and causes God places on our family’s heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2322"></a>
We are not there yet. The Foundation is a meaningful beginning, and our current giving includes Christian education, anti-trafficking work, and other causes that matter deeply to us. The next step is to make generosity more intentionally part of family formation rather than only financial structure.

<a id="BOOK-B2323"></a>
Good legacy work often begins not with a finished system but with a growing conviction.

<a id="BOOK-B2324"></a>
#### A Funnel, Not Bigger Barns

<a id="BOOK-B2325"></a>
I believe God designed us to be a funnel for His blessings, not simply builders of bigger barns to store more stuff.

<a id="BOOK-B2326"></a>
A funnel receives and releases. It is open on both ends. It does not exist to hoard what passes through it. It receives so that something can flow through toward its intended purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2327"></a>
A barn is different.

<a id="BOOK-B2328"></a>
In Luke 12, Jesus told a story about a rich man whose land produced abundantly. The man looked at everything he had accumulated and decided the answer was to tear down his barns and build bigger ones. He had more than he could store, so his solution was more storage. Bigger barns. Greater security. More accumulated comfort. Then he said to himself that he had plenty stored up for many years and could take life easy.

<a id="BOOK-B2329"></a>
But God called him a fool.

<a id="BOOK-B2330"></a>
Not because productivity is wrong. Not because saving is wrong. Not because planning is wrong. The problem was what abundance did to his heart. The increase turned inward. It became storage without surrender, accumulation without stewardship, blessing without purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2331"></a>
That story confronts me because I understand the temptation to build bigger barns. Not literal barns, but financial barns. Lifestyle barns. Security barns. Achievement barns. We can convince ourselves that one more layer of accumulation will finally make us safe enough, free enough, generous enough, or ready enough.

<a id="BOOK-B2332"></a>
But if the heart is not formed along the way, more storage does not create more stewardship. It simply creates more to protect.

<a id="BOOK-B2333"></a>
This does not mean building wealth is wrong. It does not mean financial wisdom is unspiritual. This book has already spent several chapters talking about stability, growth, freedom, income, protection, and long-term planning. Those things matter. They are part of stewardship. But the deeper question is always: What is this for?

<a id="BOOK-B2334"></a>
If God entrusts more to me, does more simply stop with me? Or does more move through me toward the people, causes, and purposes God has placed on my heart?

<a id="BOOK-B2335"></a>
That is one reason I appreciate charitable giving structures like foundations and donor-advised funds. They can help turn generosity into a rhythm instead of an afterthought. They can create a place where resources are set aside for giving before lifestyle absorbs them. They can also be wise from a stewardship and tax perspective when used properly.

<a id="BOOK-B2336"></a>
Rather than only giving cash, families may be able to give appreciated investments into a qualified charitable structure. When done correctly, that can create a charitable deduction, avoid or reduce taxes that may have been triggered if the investment had been sold personally first, and allow more funds to be available for grants to ministries and causes. Different vehicles have different rules, costs, and limitations, so families should work with qualified tax, legal, and planning professionals. But the principle is important: tax efficiency is not the opposite of generosity. When wise planning allows more of what God has entrusted to be used for God-given purposes, that is not only generosity. It is also good stewardship.

<a id="BOOK-B2337"></a>
The danger is when the tool becomes the point. A foundation can become another barn. Or it can become a funnel. The difference is the heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2338"></a>
#### The Heart Before the Amount

<a id="BOOK-B2339"></a>
Generosity must never sound like a practice reserved for people with enough wealth to create formal structures. Planning and scale can expand impact, but the amount is not what matters most. Faithfulness, surrender, and intentionality do.

<a id="BOOK-B2340"></a>
Jesus made that clear when He noticed the widow’s two small coins. Her gift was small by visible measures and large in what it represented. The story protects generosity from comparison.

<a id="BOOK-B2341"></a>
Generosity also includes time, talent, hospitality, attention, encouragement, and service. The question is not whether your contribution looks impressive. It is whether what has been entrusted is moving toward love.

<a id="BOOK-B2342"></a>
A check can be generous. So can an afternoon. So can a conversation. So can a meal. So can serving together as a family.

<a id="BOOK-B2343"></a>
Generosity is not only what leaves your account. It is what flows through your life.

<a id="BOOK-B2344"></a>
#### What Is Written Beneath the Surface

<a id="BOOK-B2345"></a>
When we moved into our home, before the wood floors were finished, Kristi and I gathered the kids and did something that still means a lot to me.

<a id="BOOK-B2346"></a>
We wrote on the floors.

<a id="BOOK-B2347"></a>
We wrote Scripture. We wrote our family mission. We wrote words and verses in rooms, hallways, and spaces that would eventually be covered. In a matter of days, those words would disappear beneath the finished flooring, rugs, furniture, backpacks, shoes, and everyday life. Guests would come over and never see them. Friends would gather in our home and never know what was underneath their feet. Even we would not see the words anymore once the floors were complete.

<a id="BOOK-B2348"></a>
But we would know they were there.

<a id="BOOK-B2349"></a>
There was something powerful about writing what we wanted our home to stand on before the visible structure was finished. It became a picture for me of how family legacy works. The most important things in a home are not always the most visible. Values often live beneath the surface before they show up in visible decisions. Faith, love, generosity, courage, humility, gratitude, discipline, joy, and service are rarely built in one dramatic moment. They are written slowly into the structure of a family.

<a id="BOOK-B2350"></a>
Every home has something written beneath the surface.

<a id="BOOK-B2351"></a>
Some homes are built on hurry. Some are built on image. Some are built on fear. Some are built on performance. Some are built on avoidance. Some are built on comfort. Some are built on faith, love, forgiveness, truth, generosity, and mission.

<a id="BOOK-B2352"></a>
The question is not whether something is being written. The question is whether we are writing it intentionally.

<a id="BOOK-B2353"></a>
For our family, the mission statement we wrote was simple: “We are a Christ-centered family who loves to have fun. We believe it is important to share our faith, hope, and love. We do this by encouraging others, taking action, and leading by example.”

<a id="BOOK-B2354"></a>
Our family life verse became Matthew 5:16, where Jesus teaches that our light should shine before others so they may see our good deeds and glorify our Father in heaven. That verse has shaped so much of our family language. Let your light shine. Not so people think more highly of us. Not so our family looks impressive. Not so our children perform perfectly. Let your light shine so others see the goodness of God.

<a id="BOOK-B2355"></a>
But words written beneath the floor only matter if they become words lived above it.

<a id="BOOK-B2356"></a>
A family mission statement can sound meaningful and still fail to shape the calendar. A Scripture verse can be framed on a wall and still remain disconnected from decisions. A foundation can be created and still fail to form anyone. A giving statement can be beautifully written and still become nothing more than paper.

<a id="BOOK-B2357"></a>
The goal is not to have inspiring language. The goal is to let that language shape a life.

<a id="BOOK-B2358"></a>
That is why I care about the future of Team G Foundation. The foundation is not the legacy. It does not automatically make our family generous. A foundation can organize money, but it cannot create a generous heart. It can move dollars, but it cannot guarantee formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2359"></a>
But a foundation can create a table.

<a id="BOOK-B2360"></a>
A giving structure can create a reason for a family to gather, pray, evaluate, decide, and remember. It can turn generosity from something parents do privately into something children learn to practice. It can help a family ask what God has placed on their heart. It can give future generations a place to participate in the story rather than merely benefit from it.

<a id="BOOK-B2361"></a>
The structure is not the point.

<a id="BOOK-B2362"></a>
But the right structure can help the point become practiced.

<a id="BOOK-B2363"></a>
#### When Giving Becomes Personal

<a id="BOOK-B2364"></a>
One of the clearest pictures of generosity becoming personal in our family came through Colton.

<a id="BOOK-B2365"></a>
The kids’ school has an incredible program called Engage. Each year, during spring break, they send around three hundred high school students on mission trips across Arizona, around the United States, and internationally. The school partners with local Christian organizations so students are not just traveling to have an experience, but serving alongside people who are already doing faithful work in those communities.

<a id="BOOK-B2366"></a>
One year, Colton went to Kenya. In Nairobi, the school partnered with Mission of Hope International, often called MOHI, which serves children and families in some of the poorest communities in the city. Colton’s group helped put on Vacation Bible School activities in the slums, and during that time he met a seven-year-old boy named Adrian.

<a id="BOOK-B2367"></a>
I do not want to tell Colton’s story as if it belongs to me. It does not. But as a dad, I had the privilege of watching what happened in him.

<a id="BOOK-B2368"></a>
Colton saw the conditions. He saw poverty in a way that is hard to understand from a distance. He saw children living in circumstances very different from the life he knew in Arizona. He was not just hearing about need as a concept. He was standing in it. He was serving in it. He was seeing faces, names, and stories.

<a id="BOOK-B2369"></a>
One of those faces was Adrian.

<a id="BOOK-B2370"></a>
During Vacation Bible School, Colton got to lead Adrian through activities. He spent time with him. He helped him. He saw his smile. He saw the reality of his life. Adrian was no longer an abstract example of poverty or a picture in a sponsorship packet. He was a seven-year-old boy Colton had met.

<a id="BOOK-B2371"></a>
When Colton came home, he wanted to sponsor Adrian. Even more meaningful to me, he wanted to do it with his own money from his “give” bucket.

<a id="BOOK-B2372"></a>
I could have sponsored Adrian myself. Kristi and I could have written the check, and that would have been good. There would have been nothing wrong with that. But something different happened when Colton chose to give from his own money.

<a id="BOOK-B2373"></a>
Generosity became personal.

<a id="BOOK-B2374"></a>
It was not just our family supporting a child. It was not just Mom and Dad deciding to give to a ministry. It was my son deciding that some of the money entrusted to him had an assignment beyond himself. The “give” bucket was no longer just a category in an app or a system we had taught him. It had become connected to a person.

<a id="BOOK-B2375"></a>
That is when financial training becomes formation.

<a id="BOOK-B2376"></a>
In Chapter 17, we talked about raising stewards rather than consumers. We talked about helping the next generation understand that money has a purpose before desire grabs it. Give, save, invest, spend. Those categories can sound simple, maybe even too simple, until a teenager’s give bucket becomes the way he sponsors a seven-year-old boy he met in Kenya.

<a id="BOOK-B2377"></a>
Then the category becomes a story.

<a id="BOOK-B2378"></a>
The next year, Colton was able to go back to Kenya and see Adrian again. I have pictures of them together that are very special to me. They are not special because they show a polished version of generosity. They are special because they remind me of what I hope our family is becoming.

<a id="BOOK-B2379"></a>
I want my children to see people. I want them to know that giving is not only about money leaving an account. It is about love taking action. It is about relationship. It is about responsibility. It is about realizing that God can use what we have, even when it feels small compared to the need, to bless someone else.

<a id="BOOK-B2380"></a>
Adrian was not a charitable deduction. He was not a line item. He was not a category. He was a child God used to shape something in my son.

<a id="BOOK-B2381"></a>
That is what generosity can do. It blesses the recipient, but it also forms the giver. It teaches us to see beyond our own wants, comfort, plans, and world. It teaches us that money can move toward love instead of merely toward lifestyle. It teaches us that impact is not always measured by the size of the gift, but by the faithfulness of the response.

<a id="BOOK-B2382"></a>
Not every emotional connection becomes a lifelong assignment. Not every mission trip must lead to sponsorship. Not every need in front of our children automatically becomes our family’s responsibility. But we should pay attention when God uses a person, a place, or a story to awaken compassion.

<a id="BOOK-B2383"></a>
Sometimes a mission trip becomes a classroom.

<a id="BOOK-B2384"></a>
Sometimes a child’s give bucket becomes a doorway.

<a id="BOOK-B2385"></a>
Sometimes a relationship across the world becomes part of the legacy being written inside your home.

<a id="BOOK-B2386"></a>
#### Serving Together

<a id="BOOK-B2387"></a>
Generosity is not only something we fund. It is something we practice.

<a id="BOOK-B2388"></a>
That is one reason serving together as a family can be so powerful. There is a different kind of formation that happens when kids and grandkids do more than hear adults talk about generosity. They feel it in their hands. They give time. They pack meals. They show up. They pray. They begin to understand that the world is larger than their own preferences, schedules, and comforts.

<a id="BOOK-B2389"></a>
We have served together in different ways, and experiences like Feed My Starving Children can create a kind of impact that is hard to replicate through a conversation alone. A child may not understand a family foundation, appreciated stock, tax strategy, charitable grants, or estate planning. But a child can understand putting food in a bag that will help feed another child. A grandchild can understand that their hands are part of helping someone else live.

<a id="BOOK-B2390"></a>
A check can support the mission. Presence can connect your heart to the mission. Both matter.

<a id="BOOK-B2391"></a>
For some families, the next faithful step is not creating a foundation. It is serving together once a quarter. It is taking the kids to a ministry. It is letting them meet someone whose life is different from theirs. It is helping them write a note to a missionary. It is inviting them to choose a cause for part of the family’s giving. It is asking them what breaks their heart and listening carefully to the answer.

<a id="BOOK-B2392"></a>
Legacy is not formed only in planning meetings or estate documents. It is also formed in service projects, mission trips, dinner table conversations, quiet acts of kindness, and moments when children realize they have something to give.

<a id="BOOK-B2393"></a>
#### Giving With Purpose

<a id="BOOK-B2394"></a>
Families practice generosity through many structures: recurring gifts, separate giving accounts, donor-advised funds, private foundations, estate gifts, service, and family meetings. The tool matters less than the direction it gives conviction.

<a id="BOOK-B2395"></a>
Generosity needs discernment because no person or family can meet every need. Not every good opportunity is your assignment. A soft heart without a wise filter becomes scattered; a wise filter without compassion becomes cold.

<a id="BOOK-B2396"></a>
A simple giving statement helps name what you believe, the causes that consistently stir your heart, how you want the next generation involved, and the kinds of requests you are most likely to accept or decline.

<a id="BOOK-B2397"></a>
This connects to my mission: helping people use God-given resources for God-given purposes—not the purposes of the loudest request, but the people and causes God has placed on their hearts.

<a id="BOOK-B2398"></a>
A giving statement helps you say yes with conviction. A No Letter helps you decline with kindness when a request does not fit. Together, they protect generosity from drift.

<a id="BOOK-B2399"></a>
#### The No Letter

<a id="BOOK-B2400"></a>
One of the most helpful tools I have developed for myself and for clients is what I call a No Letter.

<a id="BOOK-B2401"></a>
The idea is simple. If your family has a giving statement, then your generosity has direction. If your generosity has direction, there will be times when you need to decline a request that does not fit. Not because the cause is bad. Not because the person asking is wrong. Not because you do not care. But because saying yes to every good thing can prevent you from saying yes to the God-given purposes that have been placed on your heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2402"></a>
A No Letter gives you language before the emotional pressure of the moment.

<a id="BOOK-B2403"></a>
It might say something like this:

<a id="BOOK-B2404"></a>
“Thank you for thinking of us and for sharing this opportunity. We are grateful for the work you are doing and the need you are addressing. As a family, we have prayerfully identified a few giving priorities for this season, and we are trying to stay focused so we can support those areas faithfully. Because of that, we are not able to participate financially at this time. We hope the work continues to be blessed and are grateful you gave us the opportunity to consider it.”

<a id="BOOK-B2405"></a>
That kind of response does not have to be cold. In fact, it can help you say no with kindness rather than avoidance. Many people do not say no well because they feel guilty, overwhelmed, or unsure what to say. So they delay. They ignore the request. They give reluctantly. Or they say yes but feel resentful afterward.

<a id="BOOK-B2406"></a>
A No Letter protects integrity. It allows your words to match your convictions. It helps you say no to requests that do not align so you can say yes more fully to the causes that are most meaningful to you.

<a id="BOOK-B2407"></a>
#### The Tools Are Not the Legacy

<a id="BOOK-B2408"></a>
I believe in good planning.

<a id="BOOK-B2409"></a>
Wills, trusts, beneficiary designations, powers of attorney, healthcare directives, guardianship decisions, charitable plans, donor-advised funds, and foundations can all be part of wise stewardship. These tools can create clarity, reduce confusion, protect loved ones, and help resources move according to purpose rather than chaos.

<a id="BOOK-B2410"></a>
For families with minor children, guardianship matters. For business owners, succession matters. For families with meaningful assets, trust design and beneficiary planning matter. For people who care about charitable impact, planning matters. For anyone who loves people who would be affected by a crisis or death, clarity matters.

<a id="BOOK-B2411"></a>
This is not the place for technical legal, tax, or financial advice. Every family should work with qualified professionals who understand their situation. But the principle is simple: stewardship prepares. It does not prepare because it is fearful. It prepares because it is loving.

<a id="BOOK-B2412"></a>
Yet as much as legal and financial documents matter, they cannot say everything.

<a id="BOOK-B2413"></a>
I have seen people spend months perfecting estate documents but never write the words their family most needs to hear. Legal clarity matters. Financial planning matters. But there are some things a trust cannot say.

<a id="BOOK-B2414"></a>
A trust can transfer assets.

<a id="BOOK-B2415"></a>
A letter can transfer heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2416"></a>
A will can say who receives what. A trust can say how assets are managed. A beneficiary designation can direct an account. A foundation can organize giving. A donor-advised fund can create a vehicle. But none of those documents can fully explain what you loved, what you learned, what you regret, what you hope, what you pray, what you believe, and what kind of people you hope your family becomes.

<a id="BOOK-B2417"></a>
That is why I believe in the idea of a Legacy Letter.

<a id="BOOK-B2418"></a>
A Legacy Letter is not a legal document. It does not replace a will, trust, or estate plan. It is not about controlling people from beyond the grave. It is simply a way of putting your heart into words. It might include your testimony, biggest life lessons, blessings, prayers, hopes, warnings, and the wisdom you want your children or grandchildren to carry.

<a id="BOOK-B2419"></a>
But even here, I want to be careful. A Legacy Letter is good, but living it out and talking about these values and beliefs on a regular basis is even better. A letter should not be the first time your family hears what mattered to you. If possible, let the letter confirm what your life has already been saying.

<a id="BOOK-B2420"></a>
Unspoken legacy becomes guesswork.

<a id="BOOK-B2421"></a>
Unlived legacy becomes confusion.

<a id="BOOK-B2422"></a>
The goal is both: speak it and live it.

<a id="BOOK-B2423"></a>
#### Legacy While You Are Living

<a id="BOOK-B2424"></a>
Legacy is not only what remains after death. It is what your life is multiplying now through faith, stories, generosity, example, relationships, and influence.

<a id="BOOK-B2425"></a>
Inheritance can transfer assets; legacy transfers meaning. Estate documents, trusts, beneficiary designations, and charitable structures matter, but they cannot fully communicate what you loved, learned, regretted, believed, or prayed for. A Legacy Letter can put heart into words, but the letter should confirm what your life has already been saying.

<a id="BOOK-B2426"></a>
Do not postpone generosity or legacy until the conditions feel impressive. The amount and structure may change over time, but the heart should not be delayed. Start with one gift, one service rhythm, one family conversation, one paragraph, or one person to encourage.

<a id="BOOK-B2427"></a>
I pray Team G Foundation becomes a family table where generosity is discussed, practiced, and passed on. Your legacy need not look like ours. The invitation is to ask what God has placed on your heart, who you are called to bless, and what your life should multiply beyond you.

<a id="BOOK-B2428"></a>
That is legacy. That is stewardship. And now it is time to put the whole Blueprint on paper.

<a id="BOOK-B2429"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 18

<a id="BOOK-B2430"></a>
This week, complete a Legacy and Generosity Map. Do not begin by making this only an estate planning project. You may eventually need an attorney, advisor, tax professional, or charitable planning expert. Those tools matter. But begin with heart, clarity, and direction.

<a id="BOOK-B2431"></a>
First, name your people of impact. Who are the people you feel called to influence, invest in, encourage, bless, or form? Include family, but do not stop there. Think about children, grandchildren, nieces, nephews, friends, mentees, employees, church community, neighbors, clients, ministry leaders, or younger people God has placed within your sphere of influence.

<a id="BOOK-B2432"></a>
Next, write what you want to leave in them. Not only what you might leave for them someday, but what you want to leave in them now. Faith. Wisdom. Courage. Responsibility. Gratitude. Generosity. Integrity. Work ethic. Joy. Love. Discernment. Hope. Write the words that matter most.

<a id="BOOK-B2433"></a>
Then name three to five causes that stir your heart. What needs in the world consistently move you to prayer, compassion, action, or giving? What ministries, organizations, leaders, or problems do you feel drawn toward? Do not choose what sounds impressive. Choose what actually stirs your heart.

<a id="BOOK-B2434"></a>
After that, draft a simple personal or family giving statement. Use this structure if helpful:

<a id="BOOK-B2435"></a>
“Our family believes __________. We give because __________. In this season, we feel called to support __________. We want our giving to form __________ in us and bless __________ beyond us. We will tend to say yes when __________. We will tend to say no when __________. Our next faithful step is __________.”

<a id="BOOK-B2436"></a>
Then draft a simple No Letter. Write two or three sentences you could use when a request does not align with your giving statement. Make it kind, honest, and clear. The goal is not to become less generous. The goal is to protect the generosity God has actually assigned to you.

<a id="BOOK-B2437"></a>
Finally, choose one practical step. It may be increasing your giving percentage. It may be opening a separate giving account. It may be researching a donor-advised fund. It may be reviewing your estate plan. It may be writing the first paragraph of a Legacy Letter. It may be inviting your spouse or children into a giving conversation. It may be serving together as a family. It may be sponsoring one child. It may be choosing one ministry to support consistently. It may be scheduling time with an advisor to align your giving, tax planning, and estate plan.

<a id="BOOK-B2438"></a>
Do not choose ten steps. Choose one.

<a id="BOOK-B2439"></a>
The goal is not to finish your legacy this week. The goal is to begin living it with intention.

<a id="BOOK-B2440"></a>
#### Stewardship Practice — The Family Giving Statement

<a id="BOOK-B2441"></a>
A family giving statement is a simple written filter that helps generosity become intentional instead of reactive. It is not meant to remove prayer, compassion, or flexibility. It is meant to help your family name what God has placed on your heart before pressure, emotion, or urgency makes every request feel equally important.

<a id="BOOK-B2442"></a>
Begin with these prompts:

<a id="BOOK-B2443"></a>
What do we believe about God, money, ownership, and stewardship?

<a id="BOOK-B2444"></a>
Why do we give?

<a id="BOOK-B2445"></a>
What percentage or rhythm of giving do we want to move toward?

<a id="BOOK-B2446"></a>
What are the three to five causes that consistently stir our hearts?

<a id="BOOK-B2447"></a>
What kinds of requests are we most likely to say yes to?

<a id="BOOK-B2448"></a>
What kinds of requests might we need to say no to?

<a id="BOOK-B2449"></a>
How do we want children, grandchildren, or loved ones involved?

<a id="BOOK-B2450"></a>
How can we practice generosity through time, talent, and treasure?

<a id="BOOK-B2451"></a>
Where could we serve together, not only give financially?

<a id="BOOK-B2452"></a>
What giving story do we want our family to remember?

<a id="BOOK-B2453"></a>
What is one next faithful step we can take this year?

<a id="BOOK-B2454"></a>
Do not worry about perfect language. You can refine it later. A rough but honest giving statement is more useful than a polished statement that never shapes a decision.

<a id="BOOK-B2455"></a>
#### Stewardship Practice — The No Letter

<a id="BOOK-B2456"></a>
A No Letter helps you decline requests kindly when they do not align with your giving priorities. Use this as a starting point:

<a id="BOOK-B2457"></a>
“Thank you for sharing this opportunity with us. We are grateful for the work you are doing and the need you are addressing. As a family, we have prayerfully identified a few giving priorities for this season, and we are trying to stay focused so we can support those areas faithfully. Because of that, we are not able to participate financially at this time. We are grateful you gave us the opportunity to consider it and pray the work continues to be blessed.”

<a id="BOOK-B2458"></a>
Make it your own. Keep it warm. Keep it honest. Let it help you say no with grace so you can say yes with conviction.

<a id="BOOK-B2459"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 18

<a id="BOOK-B2460"></a>
What will my life multiply beyond me?

<a id="BOOK-B2461"></a>
What do I want to leave in the people I love, not only for them?

<a id="BOOK-B2462"></a>
Which causes or needs consistently stir my heart?

<a id="BOOK-B2463"></a>
Where have I postponed generosity until conditions feel more perfect?

<a id="BOOK-B2464"></a>
What words or stories does my family need to hear from me?

<a id="BOOK-B2465"></a>
What one step would make generosity and legacy more intentional now?


<a id="BOOK-B2466"></a>
### PART VII — YOUR PERSONAL BLUEPRINT

<a id="BOOK-B2467"></a>
#### Putting the Life Into Practice


<a id="BOOK-B2468"></a>
### Chapter 19 — Your Personal Blueprint

<a id="BOOK-B2469"></a>
#### Design the Life You Are Building

<a id="BOOK-B2470"></a>
Now it is time to bring the whole Blueprint together.

<a id="BOOK-B2471"></a>
Not into a perfect plan for the rest of your life, and not into a summary of everything you have read. The purpose is to create a clear guide you can return to when priorities compete, opportunities multiply, circumstances change, or drift begins to feel normal.

<a id="BOOK-B2472"></a>
We have already explored the Bedrock, Foundation, Pillars, Guardrails, Keystone Habits, Energy, resources, and Legacy. You do not need another explanation of the framework. You need to gather what has become clear, name what remains unfinished, and decide how what you have learned will shape the season you are actually living.

<a id="BOOK-B2473"></a>
That is the purpose of your Personal Stewardship Blueprint.

<a id="BOOK-B2474"></a>
#### Nearly Twenty Years in the Making

<a id="BOOK-B2475"></a>
Nearly twenty years have passed since I held Colton in that hospital room and first asked:

<a id="BOOK-B2476"></a>
If everything I am chasing works, will it actually matter?

<a id="BOOK-B2477"></a>
That moment did not give me a mission statement, a set of values, or a complete plan for the rest of my life. It gave me a question strong enough to follow.

<a id="BOOK-B2478"></a>
The answers came gradually through marriage and parenting, faith and business, success and disappointment, physical challenges, financial decisions, friendships, generosity, leadership, and seasons when I was forced to reconsider what mattered most. Some insights arrived in moments of emotional or spiritual clarity. Others formed so slowly that I did not recognize them until years later.

<a id="BOOK-B2479"></a>
There were seasons when I could see real progress. My faith deepened, my priorities became clearer, and decisions that once felt confusing began to make more sense. I could see God redirecting my ambition, reshaping my definition of success, and teaching me to think more intentionally about family, health, resources, influence, and legacy.

<a id="BOOK-B2480"></a>
There were also seasons when I could not see much progress at all. Life remained full, responsibilities kept moving, and the deeper work seemed quiet. At times, I felt as though I was circling the same questions rather than moving forward.

<a id="BOOK-B2481"></a>
Looking back, I can see that God was still working in those seasons. He was forming convictions before I had language for them. He was using people, responsibility, waiting, success, failure, fatigue, opportunity, and correction to shape the man I was becoming. The progress was not always visible, but it was not absent.

<a id="BOOK-B2482"></a>
The Blueprint I have today did not come from one retreat, one book, one prayer, or one breakthrough. It is the current expression of an almost twenty-year journey of questions, decisions, mistakes, prayers, course corrections, growth, and grace.

<a id="BOOK-B2483"></a>
Even now, it is not finished. My current Blueprint still contains places marked Needs Clarity. There are areas I understand more clearly than I did ten years ago and others I am still learning to name. There are assignments that belong to this season and dreams that may belong to another. There are values I understand deeply but am still learning to live consistently.

<a id="BOOK-B2484"></a>
That does not weaken the Blueprint. It keeps it honest.

<a id="BOOK-B2485"></a>
At the top of mine, I wrote:

<a id="BOOK-B2486"></a>
My worth is secure in Christ. My responsibility is to become increasingly faithful and fruitful with what God has entrusted to me.

<a id="BOOK-B2487"></a>
That sentence keeps worth and responsibility in the proper order. I do not have to build a perfect life to prove my value. My worth is received from God, not earned through performance, wealth, influence, leadership, recognition, or impact. At the same time, what God has entrusted to me deserves my faithful attention.

<a id="BOOK-B2488"></a>
Your Blueprint will not arrive all at once either. The goal is not a finished product, because the truth is that none of us will ever become one. As long as we are alive, God will continue forming us, people will continue needing us, circumstances will continue changing, and new seasons will require new wisdom.

<a id="BOOK-B2489"></a>
The goal is direction and intentionality.

<a id="BOOK-B2490"></a>
Take one step. Then take one more.

<a id="BOOK-B2491"></a>
#### A Blueprint for This Season

<a id="BOOK-B2492"></a>
Your Blueprint should be dated.

<a id="BOOK-B2493"></a>
Mine is called My Current Stewardship Blueprint: This Season of My Life. That wording removes the pressure to predict every future season and gives me permission to tell the truth about this one.

<a id="BOOK-B2494"></a>
A Personal Stewardship Blueprint is a living decision guide for the person you are becoming and the life you are building. It should preserve what is enduring—your identity, mission, values, and guiding truth—while allowing your calling, priorities, rhythms, and responsibilities to change with the season.

<a id="BOOK-B2495"></a>
We make plans with wisdom while holding them with open hands. We name what we believe God has entrusted to us, take responsibility for what is ours to carry, and remain willing to be redirected. That posture protects us from drifting because we never decide and from gripping our plans so tightly that we leave no room for God to lead.

<a id="BOOK-B2496"></a>
Write your Blueprint in the first person and keep the finished statements short. The longer reflection belongs in your journal, conversations, assessments, or companion materials. The Blueprint itself should help you make a decision.

<a id="BOOK-B2497"></a>
The test is practical:

<a id="BOOK-B2498"></a>
Can I open this Blueprint on a difficult Tuesday and know what to remember, what to protect, what to do, and how to return?

<a id="BOOK-B2499"></a>
If it can do that, it is becoming useful.

<a id="BOOK-B2500"></a>
#### Begin With Your North Star

<a id="BOOK-B2501"></a>
Your first goal is not to complete a polished, twelve-page life plan. It is to create Version One of a one-page North Star.

<a id="BOOK-B2502"></a>
Your North Star is the overview you can read in a few minutes. It brings the whole structure into view and reminds you what should govern the decisions of this season. The deeper Blueprint can grow over time, but this page gives you a place to begin.

<a id="BOOK-B2503"></a>
It should capture six things.

<a id="BOOK-B2504"></a>
What I am rooted in. Bring forward your identity anchor, mission, governing values, life verse or guiding Scripture, current calling, and daily purpose.

<a id="BOOK-B2505"></a>
Who I am becoming and why. Name the person your choices are forming and who will experience the difference.

<a id="BOOK-B2506"></a>
What this season requires. Clarify what you are building now, what must be protected, and what belongs in another season.

<a id="BOOK-B2507"></a>
Who and what have been entrusted to me. Name the people closest to you and the current assignment of each Pillar.

<a id="BOOK-B2508"></a>
What will protect and sustain the life. Include your most important Guardrails, one Keystone Habit, an energy-renewing rhythm, an early warning sign of drift, and a way to return.

<a id="BOOK-B2509"></a>
What I want my resources and life to multiply. Clarify your financial direction, what you refuse to trade, the impact you hope to multiply, and the next faithful step.

<a id="BOOK-B2510"></a>
Do not wait for perfect wording. Begin with what you know. Where clarity is genuinely missing, write Needs Clarity and name the question that remains.

<a id="BOOK-B2511"></a>
Your North Star is not the whole story. It is the page that helps you remember the story when life becomes noisy.

<a id="BOOK-B2512"></a>
#### The Person You Are Becoming

<a id="BOOK-B2513"></a>
A Blueprint should describe more than what you intend to accomplish. It should name who you are becoming.

<a id="BOOK-B2514"></a>
Write three to six short statements beginning with:

<a id="BOOK-B2515"></a>
I am becoming…

<a id="BOOK-B2516"></a>
You may be becoming more rooted, present, strong, courageous, generous, peaceful, emotionally healthy, disciplined, wise, free, dependable, or willing to mentor and multiply through others.

<a id="BOOK-B2517"></a>
These are not claims that you have arrived. They are directional identities. Each one should make a decision easier.

<a id="BOOK-B2518"></a>
If you write, “I am becoming present,” ask what a present person puts down, protects, or notices.

<a id="BOOK-B2519"></a>
If you write, “I am becoming courageous,” ask what conversation a courageous person would stop avoiding.

<a id="BOOK-B2520"></a>
If you write, “I am becoming generous,” ask what a generous person would begin practicing now rather than someday.

<a id="BOOK-B2521"></a>
Then write why becoming that person matters. Who benefits? What will your spouse, children, friends, team, church, clients, mentees, or community experience from you? What may they continue experiencing if nothing changes?

<a id="BOOK-B2522"></a>
The most important outcome is not the page you complete. It is the person you are becoming—and the life others experience because you chose to stop drifting.

<a id="BOOK-B2523"></a>
#### Values That Govern Real Decisions

<a id="BOOK-B2524"></a>
Bring forward the governing values you identified earlier in the book. Do not add more words simply because they sound meaningful. Choose the values that must remain visible when real priorities compete.

<a id="BOOK-B2525"></a>
For each value, write one sentence describing what it requires from you and one question it should ask when pressure arrives.

<a id="BOOK-B2526"></a>
Integrity might ask:

<a id="BOOK-B2527"></a>
Can I make this choice openly, truthfully, and consistently with what I say I believe?

<a id="BOOK-B2528"></a>
Love might ask:

<a id="BOOK-B2529"></a>
What will the people entrusted to me experience from this decision and from the way I carry it out?

<a id="BOOK-B2530"></a>
Wisdom might ask:

<a id="BOOK-B2531"></a>
What is the wisest decision when I consider the whole situation, the likely consequences, and the people affected?

<a id="BOOK-B2532"></a>
Your values should also clarify priority. When several good things cannot be preserved equally, what should generally absorb the sacrifice? Which commitments are foundational, and which ones must remain flexible?

<a id="BOOK-B2533"></a>
A priority hierarchy does not give you permission to neglect anything entrusted to you. It helps you protect what matters most when every good opportunity cannot receive the same weight.

<a id="BOOK-B2534"></a>
Values do not merely describe the life you admire. They help you decide what you will protect, pursue, decline, repair, and carry forward.

<a id="BOOK-B2535"></a>
#### Name the People and Pillars Entrusted to You

<a id="BOOK-B2536"></a>
Your Blueprint should make the people in your life visible.

<a id="BOOK-B2537"></a>
Write their names. Include the people you have been entrusted to love, lead, encourage, support, mentor, or serve in this season. Consider your spouse, children, parents, close friends, community, church, team, clients, and mentees.

<a id="BOOK-B2538"></a>
Then ask what each person needs from you now. Not what you can control for them, but what faithfulness requires from you. Presence may matter more than advice. Encouragement may matter more than correction. A difficult conversation may matter more than another gift. A recurring rhythm may matter more than a dramatic promise.

<a id="BOOK-B2539"></a>
Your Blueprint should not turn people into projects. It should protect them from becoming invisible beneath projects.

<a id="BOOK-B2540"></a>
Return next to the four Pillars and give each one a current assignment:

<a id="BOOK-B2541"></a>
Protect what is healthy and should not be crowded out.

<a id="BOOK-B2542"></a>
Strengthen what is stable but needs greater intentionality.

<a id="BOOK-B2543"></a>
Focus where strain requires meaningful attention.

<a id="BOOK-B2544"></a>
Rebuild where drift, neglect, or damage requires a deeper response.

<a id="BOOK-B2545"></a>
Do not make every Pillar a major initiative. Not every healthy area needs improvement. Some areas simply need protection while another becomes the primary focus.

<a id="BOOK-B2546"></a>
Choose one priority Pillar for the next ninety days. Your choice may not always be the area with the lowest score. Sometimes protecting a healthy marriage during a demanding season is the right assignment. Sometimes physical depletion is affecting every other part of the structure. Sometimes a financial issue requires action before it becomes chronic pressure.

<a id="BOOK-B2547"></a>
Choose the area where one intentional change is most likely to strengthen the whole life.

<a id="BOOK-B2548"></a>
#### Decide What Belongs Now

<a id="BOOK-B2549"></a>
One of the greatest threats to an intentional life is treating every meaningful possibility as a current priority.

<a id="BOOK-B2550"></a>
Responsible and creative people often have more good ideas than they have capacity to carry. A new project fits the mission. A person needs help. An opportunity appears. A future dream creates excitement. Before long, everything important begins feeling urgent.

<a id="BOOK-B2551"></a>
Your Blueprint should help you distinguish five categories.

<a id="BOOK-B2552"></a>
Build Now is the current must-win—the primary assignment that deserves your most focused building energy.

<a id="BOOK-B2553"></a>
Advance is important work that should continue moving without displacing the must-win.

<a id="BOOK-B2554"></a>
Govern includes responsibilities that remain under your leadership but should be carried through capable people, clear systems, and defined accountability rather than your constant involvement.

<a id="BOOK-B2555"></a>
Protect identifies the people and rhythms that make the rest of life sustainable: faith, marriage, family, health, friendship, recovery, reflection, and whatever else must not become collateral damage.

<a id="BOOK-B2556"></a>
For the Right Season holds dreams, ideas, projects, and opportunities that may matter but do not belong now.

<a id="BOOK-B2557"></a>
Everything can matter without everything being now.

<a id="BOOK-B2558"></a>
Once those categories are clear, ask what must stop, delegate, decline, redesign, or wait. Focus is not created only by identifying what matters. It is created by removing what repeatedly competes with it.

<a id="BOOK-B2559"></a>
A good opportunity can still be wrong for this season. Placing it in For the Right Season does not mean abandoning it. It means refusing to let a possible future assignment weaken the one already in front of you.

<a id="BOOK-B2560"></a>
#### Give Your Resources a Direction

<a id="BOOK-B2561"></a>
Your Blueprint does not replace a financial plan, but it should tell your financial plan what it is meant to serve.

<a id="BOOK-B2562"></a>
Bring forward three ideas from the financial section of this book:

<a id="BOOK-B2563"></a>
Enough.

<a id="BOOK-B2564"></a>
Freedom.

<a id="BOOK-B2565"></a>
Multiplication.

<a id="BOOK-B2566"></a>
Write what enough means in your current season. Name the kind of freedom you are building toward and what additional resources should make possible beyond yourself. Include your current financial season, your long-term direction, and one Guardrail that keeps money in its proper place.

<a id="BOOK-B2567"></a>
You might write:

<a id="BOOK-B2568"></a>
The resources entrusted to me are meant to provide for __________, create freedom for __________, protect __________, and multiply __________.

<a id="BOOK-B2569"></a>
The numbers and strategies will vary. The purpose should remain clear enough that money knows its place.

<a id="BOOK-B2570"></a>
Your financial goals may still be ambitious. There is nothing wrong with building significant capacity. But name what that capacity is for, and refuse to keep moving the goalposts merely because more remains possible.

<a id="BOOK-B2571"></a>
Money is a powerful tool but a poor identity.

<a id="BOOK-B2572"></a>
Protect, Notice, and Return

<a id="BOOK-B2573"></a>
Your Blueprint must do more than express aspirations. It needs to protect the life when pressure arrives and provide a way back when drift begins.

<a id="BOOK-B2574"></a>
Choose three to five Guardrails that address the areas where you are most vulnerable. Write them as clear, pre-decided boundaries—not vague intentions. At least one should address capacity, because every new commitment spends time, attention, and energy that must come from somewhere.

<a id="BOOK-B2575"></a>
Some of your strongest Guardrails may begin with:

<a id="BOOK-B2576"></a>
I will not allow success to cost…

<a id="BOOK-B2577"></a>
Before I add a meaningful commitment, I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2578"></a>
When a major financial decision appears, I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2579"></a>
I will not trade…

<a id="BOOK-B2580"></a>
Then create several When–Then cues.

<a id="BOOK-B2581"></a>
When work begins following me mentally into family time, then I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2582"></a>
When a new opportunity appears, then I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2583"></a>
When I feel pressure to prove myself, then I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2584"></a>
When my energy begins declining, then I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2585"></a>
When I notice myself carrying a responsibility that belongs to someone else, then I will…

<a id="BOOK-B2586"></a>
Guardrails help you act from wisdom before emotion, urgency, opportunity, or fatigue takes control.

<a id="BOOK-B2587"></a>
Your Blueprint should also name your early warning signs of drift. These may include irritability, overcommitment, spiritual dryness, financial impulsiveness, physical depletion, excessive screen use, avoidance, defensiveness, too many active ideas, disappearing joy, or becoming physically present but mentally elsewhere.

<a id="BOOK-B2588"></a>
Make the warning signs personal enough that you and someone close to you can recognize them.

<a id="BOOK-B2589"></a>
Then write a simple Return to Alignment plan. When you notice drift, pause and tell the truth. Re-read your Bedrock, Foundation, and current-season priorities. Examine the evidence in your calendar, habits, spending, attention, relationships, and energy. Identify the area creating the greatest misalignment, make one correction, reconnect with the person affected, and restore the basic rhythms that help you become present again.

<a id="BOOK-B2590"></a>
Do not interpret one difficult week as proof that the Blueprint failed.

<a id="BOOK-B2591"></a>
You do not restart your Blueprint. You return to it.

<a id="BOOK-B2592"></a>
The goal is not to eliminate drift forever. The goal is to notice sooner, recover more quickly, and keep moving in the right direction.

<a id="BOOK-B2593"></a>
#### Put the Blueprint Into Practice

<a id="BOOK-B2594"></a>
Your Blueprint becomes real when it reaches your calendar.

<a id="BOOK-B2595"></a>
Choose one or two Keystone Habits that support the assignment of this season. Do not create a long list of ideal behaviors. Choose the few rhythms most likely to produce a positive ripple across your faith, relationships, health, work, or finances.

<a id="BOOK-B2596"></a>
Make each habit specific. Identify the cue, the minimum version you can practice on a difficult day, the environment that supports it, and the way you will return after a miss.

<a id="BOOK-B2597"></a>
Then think beyond one habit and identify the recurring rhythms that help you remain aligned:

<a id="BOOK-B2598"></a>
What belongs daily?

<a id="BOOK-B2599"></a>
What needs protection weekly?

<a id="BOOK-B2600"></a>
What should be reviewed monthly?

<a id="BOOK-B2601"></a>
What requires a quarterly reset?

<a id="BOOK-B2602"></a>
What deserves deeper annual reflection?

<a id="BOOK-B2603"></a>
You do not need to fill every category with several new commitments. One daily anchor, one weekly reset, and one quarterly review may be enough to begin.

<a id="BOOK-B2604"></a>
Your rhythms must also account for Energy. Name what renews you physically, emotionally, mentally, and spiritually. Distinguish meaningful investments from necessary withdrawals and avoidable leaks. A difficult season may require more from you, but constant depletion should not become the permanent cost of faithfulness.

<a id="BOOK-B2605"></a>
Your Blueprint cannot depend on a version of you who is always rested, focused, emotionally steady, and spiritually full. Renewal must be part of the design.

<a id="BOOK-B2606"></a>
A consistent return to prayer and Scripture may strengthen identity, purpose, and emotional steadiness. A protected relationship rhythm may create more connection than a future trip that never gets scheduled. A recurring financial review may create more freedom than months of vague concern. A bedtime, workout, walk, Sabbath rhythm, focused work block, or quiet hour of reflection may strengthen several layers at once.

<a id="BOOK-B2607"></a>
Choose what is small enough to repeat and meaningful enough to matter.

<a id="BOOK-B2608"></a>
Recovery is part of the system.

<a id="BOOK-B2609"></a>
Clarify What You Refuse to Trade—and What You Want to Multiply

<a id="BOOK-B2610"></a>
Complete this sentence several times:

<a id="BOOK-B2611"></a>
I will not trade __________ for __________.

<a id="BOOK-B2612"></a>
You may refuse to trade intimacy with God for impact in His name, marriage for a platform, your children’s trust for professional success, health for endless productivity, integrity for opportunity, freedom for lifestyle, friendship for busyness, or presence for perpetual urgency.

<a id="BOOK-B2613"></a>
Your statements reveal what you intend to protect when two good things compete.

<a id="BOOK-B2614"></a>
Then complete:

<a id="BOOK-B2615"></a>
I want my life to multiply…

<a id="BOOK-B2616"></a>
Name what you hope continues through the people and causes entrusted to you. Faith. Love. Wisdom. Generosity. Courage. Opportunity. Joy. Leadership. Strong families. Mentorship. Kingdom impact. Resources redirected toward meaningful purposes. People who become multipliers themselves.

<a id="BOOK-B2617"></a>
Consider the different places your life may multiply: family, church, community, work, mentorship, generosity, leadership, or a movement you feel called to build. The scale may be large or small. Faithfulness is not measured only by reach.

<a id="BOOK-B2618"></a>
Legacy is not only what remains after death. It is what other people experience from your life now.

<a id="BOOK-B2619"></a>
#### Let Other People Into the Process

<a id="BOOK-B2620"></a>
A Personal Stewardship Blueprint is personal, but it should not remain completely private.

<a id="BOOK-B2621"></a>
Share your North Star with someone who knows you well enough to recognize both your strengths and your blind spots. That person may be your spouse, close friend, mentor, pastor, counselor, advisor, accountability partner, or small group.

<a id="BOOK-B2622"></a>
Ask questions that invite honesty:

<a id="BOOK-B2623"></a>
Does this sound like me?

<a id="BOOK-B2624"></a>
What feels most aligned?

<a id="BOOK-B2625"></a>
Where do my actions currently contradict what I have written?

<a id="BOOK-B2626"></a>
What am I failing to see?

<a id="BOOK-B2627"></a>
What do you need to experience from me in this season?

<a id="BOOK-B2628"></a>
Do not use your Blueprint to manage the people around you or pressure them to adopt your priorities. It is first a guide for your own stewardship. Their perspective may help you refine it, but your responsibility is to live it.

<a id="BOOK-B2629"></a>
Community does not replace personal responsibility. It helps responsibility survive.

<a id="BOOK-B2630"></a>
The page gives you an artifact. Relationships, rhythms, community, and accountability are what make it a living Blueprint.

<a id="BOOK-B2631"></a>
#### The Journey Beyond This Book

<a id="BOOK-B2632"></a>
A book can awaken and guide, but it cannot provide every assessment, conversation, rhythm, or layer of accountability needed over a lifetime. That is why Stewardship Blueprint is becoming a broader set of tools and experiences.

<a id="BOOK-B2633"></a>
The architecture is simple: Stewardship Blueprint is the framework. The Life You’re Building is the book that teaches it. ForgedLife is the accountability app that helps people live it.

<a id="BOOK-B2634"></a>
Additional assessments, Personal Blueprint resources, studies, small-group experiences, church materials, and updates are available through michaeljgauthier.com. Choose only the support that helps you practice what you have already said matters; more content is not the goal.

<a id="BOOK-B2635"></a>
#### Blueprint Actions — Chapter 19

<a id="BOOK-B2636"></a>
Set aside time to create Version One of your Personal Stewardship Blueprint. You may prefer one extended session, several shorter sessions, or a personal half-day retreat. Gather your notes from the earlier chapters, your calendar, and any assessments or journal entries that have helped you see your life more clearly.

<a id="BOOK-B2637"></a>
Do not wait for perfect wording. Create an honest working version.

<a id="BOOK-B2638"></a>
1. Date and Name the Season

<a id="BOOK-B2639"></a>
Complete:

<a id="BOOK-B2640"></a>
This is a season of…

<a id="BOOK-B2641"></a>
The most important realities shaping this season are…

<a id="BOOK-B2642"></a>
I believe faithfulness in this season requires me to…

<a id="BOOK-B2643"></a>
2. Create Your One-Page North Star

<a id="BOOK-B2644"></a>
Use short, first-person statements to capture:

<a id="BOOK-B2645"></a>
My Bedrock and Identity Anchor

<a id="BOOK-B2646"></a>
My Mission

<a id="BOOK-B2647"></a>
My Governing Values

<a id="BOOK-B2648"></a>
My Life Verse or Guiding Scripture

<a id="BOOK-B2649"></a>
My Current Calling or Assignment

<a id="BOOK-B2650"></a>
My Daily Purpose

<a id="BOOK-B2651"></a>
The Person I Am Becoming—and Why

<a id="BOOK-B2652"></a>
The People Entrusted to Me

<a id="BOOK-B2653"></a>
My Four Pillar Assignments

<a id="BOOK-B2654"></a>
Family:

<a id="BOOK-B2655"></a>
Fitness:

<a id="BOOK-B2656"></a>
Fun:

<a id="BOOK-B2657"></a>
Finances:

<a id="BOOK-B2658"></a>
My Priority Pillar for the Next Ninety Days

<a id="BOOK-B2659"></a>
Build Now

<a id="BOOK-B2660"></a>
Advance

<a id="BOOK-B2661"></a>
Govern

<a id="BOOK-B2662"></a>
Protect

<a id="BOOK-B2663"></a>
For the Right Season

<a id="BOOK-B2664"></a>
My Financial Stewardship Direction

<a id="BOOK-B2665"></a>
Enough:

<a id="BOOK-B2666"></a>
Freedom:

<a id="BOOK-B2667"></a>
Multiplication:

<a id="BOOK-B2668"></a>
What I Refuse to Trade

<a id="BOOK-B2669"></a>
What I Want My Life to Multiply

<a id="BOOK-B2670"></a>
Where clarity is genuinely missing, write Needs Clarity and name the question you need to keep exploring.

<a id="BOOK-B2671"></a>
3. Add Protection and Practice

<a id="BOOK-B2672"></a>
Choose:

<a id="BOOK-B2673"></a>
Three to five Guardrails.

<a id="BOOK-B2674"></a>
Two or three When–Then cues.

<a id="BOOK-B2675"></a>
Five to eight early warning signs of drift.

<a id="BOOK-B2676"></a>
A simple Return to Alignment plan.

<a id="BOOK-B2677"></a>
One Keystone Habit.

<a id="BOOK-B2678"></a>
One energy-renewing rhythm.

<a id="BOOK-B2679"></a>
Keep these realistic. Your Blueprint should make faithful action more likely, not create another burden you cannot carry.

<a id="BOOK-B2680"></a>
4. Choose One Thirty-Day Commitment

<a id="BOOK-B2681"></a>
Complete:

<a id="BOOK-B2682"></a>
Start — I will begin…

<a id="BOOK-B2683"></a>
Stop — I will end or reduce…

<a id="BOOK-B2684"></a>
Continue — I will keep doing…

<a id="BOOK-B2685"></a>
Protect — I will not allow __________ to crowd out…

<a id="BOOK-B2686"></a>
Conversation — I need to speak with…

<a id="BOOK-B2687"></a>
My primary thirty-day commitment is…

<a id="BOOK-B2688"></a>
Put the action on your calendar.

<a id="BOOK-B2689"></a>
5. Invite Accountability

<a id="BOOK-B2690"></a>
Complete:

<a id="BOOK-B2691"></a>
The person I will share this with is…

<a id="BOOK-B2692"></a>
The date I will share it is…

<a id="BOOK-B2693"></a>
The question I most need that person to answer is…

<a id="BOOK-B2694"></a>
6. Schedule Your Reviews

<a id="BOOK-B2695"></a>
Schedule a thirty-day check-in and a ninety-day Blueprint review.

<a id="BOOK-B2696"></a>
During each review, ask:

<a id="BOOK-B2697"></a>
What has become clearer?

<a id="BOOK-B2698"></a>
What action did I take?

<a id="BOOK-B2699"></a>
What began to drift?

<a id="BOOK-B2700"></a>
What needs protection?

<a id="BOOK-B2701"></a>
What needs to change?

<a id="BOOK-B2702"></a>
What belongs in a different season?

<a id="BOOK-B2703"></a>
What is the next faithful step?

<a id="BOOK-B2704"></a>
Revisit the Blueprint during major transitions as well. Marriage, parenthood, loss, illness, retirement, business change, a move, a new opportunity, or a shift in calling may require meaningful recalibration.

<a id="BOOK-B2705"></a>
7. Sign Version One

<a id="BOOK-B2706"></a>
Sign and date your North Star.

<a id="BOOK-B2707"></a>
Then write:

<a id="BOOK-B2708"></a>
I am not committing to a perfect life. I am committing to live with greater awareness, intention, faithfulness, and willingness to return when I drift.

<a id="BOOK-B2709"></a>
#### Reflection Questions — Chapter 19

<a id="BOOK-B2710"></a>
Can I open this Blueprint on a difficult Tuesday and know what to remember, protect, do, and how to return?

<a id="BOOK-B2711"></a>
Does my calendar contain the life the Blueprint describes?

<a id="BOOK-B2712"></a>
Does the Blueprint distinguish enduring Mission from Current Calling and Daily Purpose?

<a id="BOOK-B2713"></a>
Can I recognize drift before regret arrives?

<a id="BOOK-B2714"></a>
Who knows my commitments and has permission to challenge me?

<a id="BOOK-B2715"></a>
What does faithfulness require in this season?

<a id="BOOK-B2716"></a>
#### A Final Word — Take One Step, Then One More

<a id="BOOK-B2717"></a>
Nearly twenty years ago, I held my firstborn son and began asking whether the life I was chasing would ultimately matter. The little boy I held is now a young man building a life of his own.
